The Model › Quiz

Chapter 1 Quiz — The Model

Test your understanding of tokenization, embeddings, attention, layers, parameters, MoE, and number formats

Chapter 1 Quiz — The Model
1 A 70B parameter model in FP16 needs how much GPU memory just for weights? What about INT4?
FP16: 70B × 2 bytes = 140 GB (needs 2× H100-80GB). INT4: 70B × 0.5 bytes = 35 GB (fits on one GPU). This is why quantization often determines whether a model is deployable at all.
2 Why can't you just use the integer token ID (e.g., 4521 for "bank") directly as input to the model?
The model would think "bank" (ID 4521) is 4521× more important than "a" (ID ~1). Integer IDs are arbitrary lookup keys with no semantic meaning. The embedding table converts each ID into a learned vector where semantic relationships are encoded in the geometry (similar words = nearby vectors).
3 In the attention mechanism, why are Key and Value separate projections instead of one combined projection?
Separating them lets the model optimize the Key for matching ("what should I advertise to help others find me?") independently from the Value for information transfer ("what rich information should I share once selected?"). Using the full representation as both search index and payload would be noisy and inefficient.
4 An MoE model has 400B total parameters but only 50B active per token. What is its inference compute cost equivalent to?
A dense 50B model. MoE decouples parameter count from compute cost. You get the knowledge capacity of 400B (different experts store different knowledge) at the inference speed of 50B. This is why frontier labs converged on MoE for their largest models.
5 Why does BF16 have the same dynamic range as FP32 despite being half the bits?
BF16 keeps all 8 exponent bits from FP32 (which set the dynamic range) and sacrifices mantissa bits (precision) instead — 7 mantissa bits vs FP32's 23. Same range (~10⁻³⁸ to 10³⁸), but only ~2 decimal digits of precision vs ~7. For training, range matters more than precision because you need to represent both tiny gradients and large activations without overflow.
6 LLaMA-3 increased vocabulary from 32K to 128K tokens. How does this affect inference speed for Japanese text?
Dramatically faster. With a 32K English-centric tokenizer, Japanese requires ~3 tokens per word. With 128K vocab optimized for multilingual, this drops to ~1.5-2 tokens per word — roughly 2× fewer tokens, meaning 2× fewer decode steps, 2× less KV cache, and 2× lower latency for the same content.
← Sampling & DecodingMLA & Attention Variants →
Architecture Frontier › MLA & Attention Variants

MLA — Multi-head Latent Attention new

DeepSeek's architectural innovation that achieves 93% KV cache reduction without meaningful quality loss

The problem being solved — KV cache is the long-context wall

As you learned in the KV cache section, the KV cache grows linearly with sequence length. For a 70B model using standard MHA, KV cache per token is ~4.7 MB. At 100k tokens, that's 470 GB of KV cache alone — more than the model weights (140 GB in FP16). The KV cache has become the dominant memory consumer for long-context serving.

GQA and MQA attack this by sharing K/V heads across Q heads: GQA might reduce KV cache by 8× (from 64 K/V heads to 8). MLA goes further — not by sharing heads, but by compressing the information content of K and V entirely differently.

The MLA insight — low-rank compression of K and V

The key observation behind MLA: K and V vectors across all heads at a given token and layer are not independent — they contain significant redundancy. If you have 64 heads each with a 128-dimensional K vector, that's 8,192 numbers per token per layer. But the actual information content is much lower — the vectors are correlated because they all derived from the same token embedding through related projection matrices.

MLA exploits this redundancy by learning to compress all K and V information into a single low-dimensional latent vector per token per layer. Then at attention time, the full K and V vectors for all heads are reconstructed from this latent vector via learned projection matrices.

  • What gets stored in the KV cache: One small compressed latent vector per token per layer — much smaller than num_heads × d_head × 2 (for K and V)
  • What happens at attention time: Project the latent vector back up to get K and V for all heads. Small extra compute, but the memory savings are enormous.
The numbers from DeepSeek-V2

DeepSeek-V2 introduced MLA and reported a 93% reduction in KV cache memory compared to their equivalent MHA baseline, with no meaningful quality loss on benchmarks. This is dramatically more aggressive than even MQA, which would only achieve ~1/64 reduction (one K/V head for 64 Q heads). MLA achieves this because it's compressing the information itself, not just sharing pointers to it.

Practically: if GPT-3 MHA needs ~4.7 MB KV cache per token, an equivalent MLA model might need ~0.3 MB per token. At 100k tokens: 470 GB → ~30 GB. This change alone makes very-long-context serving fundamentally more economical.

MLA vs. KV cache quantization — different axes, they compose

A common point of confusion: MLA and KV cache quantization sound similar (both shrink the KV cache) but they operate on completely different axes and stack multiplicatively.

  • MLA changes the architecture. Instead of caching full per-head K and V tensors, it caches a small shared low-rank latent (plus a tiny RoPE key piece) and reconstructs K/V on the fly via learned up-projections. The cache shrinks because the representation is smaller. MLA must be trained in — you cannot bolt it onto an existing GQA or MHA model post-hoc.
  • KV cache quantization is a post-training compression of whatever K/V you already cache: store them in INT8, INT4, or FP8 instead of FP16/BF16. Architecture is unchanged; you trade precision for bytes (with per-channel/per-token scales and outlier handling to keep the quality hit small).

They compose. MLA's cache is just tensors — the latent vector plus the RoPE key — so you can quantize those tensors the same way. DeepSeek-V2/V3 production deployments do exactly this: MLA for the architectural shrink, then FP8 on the latent for another multiplicative reduction. The caveat: MLA's latent is already information-dense with low redundancy, so aggressive quantization tends to hurt it more per bit than it hurts a fat MHA cache. In practice people stay at FP8 on the latent rather than pushing INT4 without careful calibration.

Mental model: MLA reduces how many numbers you store per token; quantization reduces how many bits per number. Orthogonal axes, multiply together.

Why Kimi K2 and others adopted it

Kimi K2 uses MLA combined with their reduced attention head count and increased expert sparsity. These are all facets of the same co-design philosophy: reduce the parts of the architecture that scale badly with context (attention heads + KV cache memory), and increase the parts that scale well (MoE experts). The combination lets them serve 128k+ context requests at costs competitive with models serving much shorter contexts.

Deep Dive — MLA's compute tradeoff: when does compression pay off?

MLA's 93% KV cache reduction comes at a cost: projecting the compressed latent vector (d_c ~512) back to full K/V for all heads at attention time adds ~2 matrix multiplications per layer. For DeepSeek-V2 (80 layers): ~1.3 billion extra FLOPs per decode step. This sounds significant, but decode is memory-bandwidth-bound, not compute-bound — the arithmetic units are underutilized anyway. The 93% KV cache reduction means 93% less HBM bandwidth for loading cached KV, which is the actual bottleneck. The extra compute is free because you're using spare arithmetic capacity.

Crossover point: For very short sequences (batch=1, seq_len < ~50-100 tokens), the extra projection compute is non-trivial compared to the tiny attention matmul. Below that crossover, standard GQA can be faster since there's no decompression overhead. The exact crossover is hardware-dependent (compute-to-bandwidth ratio). On H100, MLA's advantage appears at batch sizes > 1 or seq_len > ~100 tokens. Since production serving almost always exceeds these thresholds, MLA is almost always a win in practice.

MLA is to KV cache what FlashAttention was to attention memory — it doesn't change the attention algorithm itself, but it transforms the memory cost from O(num_heads × d_head × context_length) to O(d_latent × context_length), where d_latent is much smaller. The industry is converging on MLA-style KV compression for frontier long-context models.
📖 DeepSeek-V2 paper — introduces MLA
← Chapter 1 QuizAttention-Variant Family →
Architecture Frontier › Attention-Variant Family

The attention-variant family new

Sliding window, sparse, gated, compressed, linear — the techniques sitting between vanilla MHA and full architectural alternatives

A family tree, not a list

"Attention" in modern LLMs is no longer one algorithm. It's a family of variants tuned for different memory/quality/speed tradeoffs. The MLA section covers one branch (latent-space compression of K/V). This section covers the rest. Knowing where each variant sits in the family tree makes architecture papers much faster to read:

BranchVariantsWhat it changesWhere you see it
Dense / classicalMHA, MQA, GQANumber of K/V heads. MQA collapses to one shared K/V head; GQA uses a small group.GPT-3 (MHA), PaLM (MQA), Llama 2/3 70B (GQA)
Latent compressionMLACompress K/V into a low-rank latent space; project back at attention time.DeepSeek V2/V3
Sparse / windowedSliding-window, dilated, BigBird, LongformerEach token attends to only a subset of others. Memory drops from O(N²) to O(N·W).Mistral 7B (SWA), Gemma 2 (alternating SWA + full)
GatedGated attention, GLA, RetNet-likeAdds a learned gate that modulates how much of the past is "remembered" — a soft form of recurrence inside attention.RWKV, RetNet, newer hybrid architectures
LinearLinear attention, Performer, FAVOR+Rewrites attention as a kernel feature map so cost is O(N·d²) instead of O(N²·d).Linear-attention research models; building blocks for SSMs
State-space (alternative)Mamba, Mamba-2Replaces attention with a learned linear recurrence with a selective state. See dedicated section.Mamba, Jamba, Nemotron Nano
Sliding-window attention — the most-deployed sparse variant

Sliding-window attention (SWA) caps each token's view to the most recent W tokens. KV cache memory becomes O(W × layers × heads × d_head) per sequence — independent of total context length. Mistral 7B uses W = 4096. Gemma 2 alternates SWA layers and full-attention layers (1:1) so the model still gets some long-range signal without paying the full O(N²) cost.

  • Win: Bounded KV cache regardless of context length. Long contexts become feasible on small GPUs.
  • Loss: Cannot directly attend to anything beyond W tokens. Information must propagate through layers — degraded recall on very long-range dependencies.
  • Workaround: Mix SWA with full-attention layers (alternating, every Nth, or "global tokens" that always see everything).
Gated attention — soft recurrence inside the attention block

Gated variants insert a learned gating function — typically a sigmoid over a projection of the input — that scales the attention output before it's added back to the residual stream. The gate can suppress attention contributions that would conflict with the residual signal. RWKV and RetNet are the most-cited examples; newer hybrid architectures (like the gated DeltaNet block in some 2025–2026 papers) generalize this idea. The serving consequence is small — gates are cheap, but they're a signal that the architecture is moving toward recurrence-like dynamics.

Linear attention — O(N) cost in exchange for quality

Standard attention's O(N²) cost comes from materializing the score matrix. Linear-attention variants rewrite attention using kernel feature maps — a function φ such that softmax(QKT) ≈ φ(Q)·φ(K)T. With this rewrite you can swap the order of operations, computing φ(K)T·V first (a small d×d matrix, regardless of N), then multiplying by φ(Q). Total cost: O(N · d²) instead of O(N² · d). Memory drops correspondingly.

Why it isn't everywhere yet: at typical transformer scales (d ≈ 4K–8K, N ≈ 4K–32K), the constants and the approximation quality favor real softmax attention, especially with FlashAttention. Linear attention shines at extreme context lengths (millions of tokens) and is the conceptual ancestor of state-space models — see the next section.

Compressed attention — the DeepSeek-style latent compression

Compressed attention (the most prominent example is MLA, covered in its own section) projects K and V down to a low-rank latent vector, stores only the latent, and decompresses at attention time. The KV cache shrinks dramatically (≈90% reduction for MLA on DeepSeek V2/V3) at the cost of a small extra projection per layer. Other "K/V compression" techniques include cross-layer KV sharing (later layers reuse earlier layers' KV), and KV pruning (drop low-importance tokens).

Hybrid layers — the production answer

Production frontier models rarely pick one variant. Instead, they alternate or stack:

  • Gemma 2 / Gemma 3: alternating sliding-window and full-attention layers.
  • Llama 4 reasoning models (and similar 2025–2026 designs): mix MoE FFN with hybrid attention layers.
  • Jamba / Nemotron Nano: mix Mamba blocks with attention blocks (covered in the next section).
  • Cohere Command-R+ and Mistral Nemo: use sliding-window attention with global-attention sinks for "always visible" anchor tokens.

Operationally, this matters because your serving engine has to support each variant in its attention kernel. FlashAttention 3 covers MHA/GQA/MLA and SWA; specialized kernels exist for gated and linear variants. Adding a new attention variant to a production engine is non-trivial — it's why model launches now ship with engine-side support patches (vLLM PRs, TRT-LLM plugins) on day zero.

📖 Longformer — sliding-window + global attentionPerformer — kernel-feature linear attentionRetNet — gated recurrent attention
← MLA & Attention VariantsMamba & Hybrid Models →
Architecture Frontier › Mamba & Hybrid Models

State-space models, Mamba, and hybrid architectures new

Recurrence with selective state — and why your KV cache might be replaced by a fixed-size hidden state

The recurrent return

Before transformers won in 2017, RNNs ruled NLP. They had one virtue that attention gave up: a fixed-size recurrent state. An RNN processes tokens one at a time, updating a single hidden vector and discarding the rest. Memory is constant in context length — there's no KV cache that grows with N. RNNs lost because their training was hard to parallelize and long-range dependencies blurred through repeated multiplications.

State-space models (SSMs) are the modern answer: a learned linear recurrence with carefully designed state dynamics that train as efficiently as transformers but inference like RNNs. The basic recurrence is

h_t = A · h_{t-1} + B · x_t
y_t = C · h_t

where A, B, C are learned matrices. The state h_t is a fixed-size vector regardless of how many tokens have been processed. A naive RNN already does this — the SSM contribution is a particular structure on A (diagonal-plus-low-rank, parameterized via continuous-time analogy) that lets training be parallelized as a long convolution while inference stays recurrent.

Mamba and the selective scan

Plain SSMs share A, B, C across all input positions — they can't selectively remember or forget based on content. Mamba (Gu & Dao, 2023) makes A, B, C input-dependent. The model can now look at a token and decide "this matters, push it into state" or "this is filler, leave state alone." That's the property attention had and classical RNNs lacked.

The implementation is a custom CUDA kernel called the selective scan — a parallel-prefix-sum operation that handles the input-dependent recurrence efficiently on GPUs. Mamba-2 (2024) further unified the SSM kernel with a structured matrix view that maps cleanly onto Tensor Cores, closing most of the gap with FlashAttention's hardware utilization.

What changes for serving
AspectTransformer (attention)SSM (Mamba)
Memory per token in contextO(num_layers × num_kv_heads × d_head) growing with NFixed-size hidden state per layer — O(1) in context length
Decode-step costO(N × d) — must read full KV cacheO(d²) — read state, update state, write output
Long-context ceilingHBM capacity (~32K–1M tokens)No memory ceiling; quality ceiling instead
PrefillO(N²·d) compute, parallelizableO(N·d²) compute via parallel scan
KV cacheRequired, dominates memoryNone — replaced by recurrent state

Operationally, an SSM is a serving engineer's dream: no KV cache. The 4-tier KV hierarchy disappears. PagedAttention disappears. Prefix caching becomes "snapshot the recurrent state at this prefix and restore it later" — kilobytes per session instead of gigabytes. Memory-bound decode becomes much less memory-bound.

The catch — retrieval and in-context recall

Pure SSMs systematically underperform transformers on tasks that require exact in-context retrieval: needle-in-haystack, copying long passages verbatim, perfect citation of a 50-token snippet from a 100K-token context. The fixed-size state forces lossy compression of the past; attention's KV cache stores everything verbatim and can look up exact matches. SSMs also tend to lag on tasks heavy on in-context learning (few-shot prompts).

This is the unsolved-but-managed weakness of pure SSMs and why the production answer is hybrid.

Hybrid models — Mamba blocks plus a few attention blocks

Rather than choose, frontier 2025–2026 models combine SSM and attention layers in one architecture. A small fraction of attention layers (typically 10–25% of total depth) handles the retrieval-heavy work; SSM layers carry the bulk of the compute with bounded memory.

  • NVIDIA Nemotron Nano (and the Nemotron-3 series) — Mamba-2 with periodic attention layers; ships with TRT-LLM kernel support.
  • Jamba (AI21) — Mixture-of-experts FFN combined with Mamba+attention block alternation.
  • Zamba / Falcon-Mamba — additional research-line hybrids exploring different SSM-to-attention ratios.

The serving result is a model with most of the memory benefits of SSMs (small KV cache, long-context-friendly) and most of the recall quality of transformers. Engines treat them as a special case: vLLM's hybrid-mamba support and TRT-LLM's Nemotron support both exist as 2025-era extensions to the standard transformer fast paths.

📖 Mamba: Linear-Time Sequence Modeling with Selective State SpacesMamba-2 paperJamba — Mamba-attention hybrid
← Attention-Variant FamilyMulti-token Prediction →
Architecture Frontier › Multi-token Prediction

Multi-token prediction (MTP) new

Training models to predict multiple tokens simultaneously — and why this both improves training and enables faster inference

The standard autoregressive limit

Standard language model training uses next-token prediction: given tokens 1 through N, predict token N+1. The model learns from the signal "was token N+1 correct?" This is how every GPT-style model is trained. At inference time, this means exactly one token per forward pass — 100 tokens of output requires 100 forward passes through the entire model. This is the fundamental sequential bottleneck of autoregressive decode. It is a hard ceiling on single-request decode throughput.

What MTP adds to training

MTP modifies the training objective to predict not just token N+1 but tokens N+1, N+2, N+3, ..., N+K simultaneously. The model maintains K prediction heads — one for each look-ahead offset. All K heads are trained simultaneously during each forward pass. The model receives gradient signal from all K predictions at once.

DeepSeek's specific implementation adds auxiliary prediction heads that share the main model's internal representations rather than operating independently. These heads are not separate classifiers — they share computations with the primary next-token head, forcing the model to build representations that are useful for predicting multiple steps ahead simultaneously.

Why this improves training stability

Predicting multiple steps ahead imposes a consistency constraint. If your model's prediction for token N+1 is inconsistent with a plausible prediction for token N+3, the multi-step gradient signal penalizes this inconsistency. The model is forced to maintain coherent internal representations that can support multi-step planning, not just local next-token prediction. DeepSeek observed empirically that this regularization produced more stable training convergence — the loss curves were smoother and the final model quality improved on reasoning tasks that require multi-step coherence.

The inference connection — MTP as built-in speculative decoding

Recall speculative decoding: a small draft model proposes N candidate tokens, the large model verifies all N in a single parallel forward pass (since you already have the proposed tokens, you can run verification in parallel like prefill), you accept the matching prefix, and continue. The acceptance rate determines the speedup.

The problem with external speculative decoding: the draft model is a separate smaller model with different characteristics. It may have lower quality on certain domains, leading to lower acceptance rates. You also need to hold both models in GPU memory simultaneously.

With MTP, the auxiliary prediction heads trained alongside the main model serve as the built-in draft heads. During inference: the main model generates token N+1 via its primary head. Simultaneously, the auxiliary heads propose tokens N+2, N+3, N+4. In the next decode step, instead of processing only token N+1 to generate N+2, you verify the proposals for N+2, N+3, N+4 in parallel. Accepted proposals mean you advance multiple tokens in one step.

Because the draft heads were trained jointly with the main model and share its representations, they tend to have higher acceptance rates than external draft models — they were literally trained to be consistent with the verifier. DeepSeek V3 reported approximately 1.8× decode throughput improvement from this approach in practice.

The tradeoff and when it helps most

MTP adds training cost (auxiliary heads must be trained and their gradients backpropagated). At inference, the auxiliary heads add a small amount of compute per forward pass. The net benefit is positive when output sequences are long enough for the ~1.8× decode speedup to amortize the overhead. Agentic tasks, extended reasoning chains, and long-form generation benefit most. For very short outputs (a few tokens), the overhead may not be worth it.

Multi-token prediction is one of the techniques the Dynamo podcast flagged as a potential unhobbler for decode throughput. Currently decode is capped at one token per step — MTP with speculative decoding is an architectural approach to breaking that ceiling without a separate draft model. Whether it eventually fully removes this constraint or just improves it by a useful constant is still being determined as the technique matures across more model architectures and training regimes.
📖 Better and Faster LLMs via Multi-token Prediction (Meta, 2024)DeepSeek-V3 paper — MTP implementation and results
← Mamba & Hybrid ModelsModel-Hardware Co-design →
Architecture Frontier › Model-Hardware Co-design

Model-hardware-context co-design new

Why attention head count is now an infrastructure decision, not a research one

For most of deep learning history, model architecture was designed primarily to maximize benchmark quality. Hardware was a separate concern — you trained on whatever was available, then figured out serving later. This separation is disappearing. Frontier labs now design model architecture, training curriculum, and serving infrastructure as a single integrated system. This is what "co-design" means, and it's fundamentally changing how inference infrastructure works.

The concrete example: Kimi K2's deliberate architectural tradeoff

Kimi K2 (Moonshot AI, 2025) provides the clearest public example of co-design thinking. They explicitly published a blog post explaining why they made a specific architectural choice: they reduced the number of attention heads and slightly reduced the attention dimension, while simultaneously increasing the total number of experts but keeping the number of activated experts per token constant — i.e., increasing expert sparsity.

They ran experiments showing this combination gave better performance per unit of serving cost at their target context lengths. Why does this tradeoff work at the fundamental physics level?

  • Attention scales quadratically with context length. If you have 64 attention heads instead of 32, you do 2× the attention work — and that cost grows as O(n²) with sequence length. At 128k tokens, halving the attention heads cuts attention compute by 50%, but the benefit is even larger than it sounds because of the quadratic scaling.
  • MoE experts scale constantly with context length. Each expert processes a fixed amount of compute per token regardless of how long the sequence is. Adding more experts (even with fixed activated count) increases knowledge capacity without increasing context-scaling cost.
  • The net result: Fewer attention heads + more (sparse) experts = a model that's cheaper to serve at long contexts while maintaining comparable knowledge capacity. The tradeoff is deliberately optimized for serving economics, not just training quality.
The three dimensions being co-designed simultaneously
DimensionWhat gets decidedServing implication
Model architectureNumber of attention heads, layers, expert count, expert sparsity ratio, attention head dimension (d_head), use of MLA vs. GQA vs. MHADirectly determines memory footprint, compute per token, and — critically — how costs scale with context length
Hardware targetWhich GPU generation, NVLink topology, HBM bandwidth, which Tensor Core formats are natively supported (FP8, BF16)Models trained specifically for FP8 on H100 get ~2× compute speedup at inference. Models designed for NVLink topology avoid InfiniBand bottlenecks in serving.
Context / harnessExpected context lengths in production, tool-calling schema the model will use, agentic workflow it'll run insideA model trained with the specific harness it will use learns to use it optimally rather than adapting at inference time. This improves both quality and efficiency of tool calls.
Why context length has stalled at ~1M tokens

Context length has essentially stalled near 1 million tokens for about two years. The reason is straightforward: attention's quadratic O(n²) compute cost (FlashAttention solved the O(n²) memory cost, but the compute remains unavoidably quadratic — see FlashAttention deep dive). Doubling context length quadruples attention computation. At 1M tokens on a large model, attention already consumes a disproportionate share of compute and memory. Going to 10M tokens would require attention computation to increase ~100×, which is infeasible on current hardware.

The architectural response has been exactly what co-design predicts: reduce attention heads and replace that capacity with mechanisms that scale linearly with context length — MoE experts, state space model layers (Mamba, etc.), or hybrid architectures that use attention selectively rather than at every layer. The hardware constraint (attention quadratic cost in HBM) is directly shaping model architecture choices at the frontier.

The key mental shift: "how many attention heads should this model have?" is no longer purely a research quality question. It is an infrastructure economics question, answered by analyzing serving cost at the target context length distribution, multiplied by the expected request volume. This is what makes modern AI infrastructure engineering genuinely different from classical systems work.
📖 Chinchilla scaling laws — the original co-design framework for training
← Multi-token PredictionDistillation →
Architecture Frontier › Distillation

Distillation new

Teacher-student training, synthetic-data fine-tunes, and how DeepSeek-R1's reasoning ended up running on Llama 3

Two distinct things both called "distillation"

The word covers two related but mechanically different procedures. Production papers and engine docs use the same word for both, which causes endless confusion:

Logit distillation (classical, Hinton 2015)Synthetic-data distillation (production, 2024-on)
Signal from teacherFull probability distribution over the vocabulary at every positionJust the generated tokens (a corpus the teacher wrote)
LossKL divergence between student's and teacher's distributionsStandard next-token cross-entropy on the synthetic corpus
Teacher access at trainingLive model, full forward pass for every batchNone — the teacher is consumed once to produce the dataset, then discarded
What's preservedDetailed token-level confidence — student approximates teacher's probability shapeSurface-level behavior — student reproduces what the teacher said, not how it weighted alternatives
Where you see itSmaller "distilled" variants of an open model (DistilBERT, MiniLM)"Reasoning distill" releases (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-70B), capability transfer, refusal training
DeepSeek-R1-Distill — the canonical recent example

DeepSeek-R1 is a 671B-parameter reasoning model. Most operators can't serve it. So the DeepSeek team did this:

  1. Used R1 to generate millions of step-by-step reasoning traces over math and code problems.
  2. Filtered traces for correctness against ground-truth answers.
  3. Supervised-fine-tuned a much smaller open base model (Llama 3 8B/70B, Qwen 2.5 1.5B/7B/14B/32B) on the filtered traces.
  4. Released the resulting checkpoints as DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-70B, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-32B, etc.

The distilled models inherit Llama-3-70B or Qwen-2.5-32B's architecture — same layer count, same hidden size, same attention pattern. Only the weights changed. Operationally that's the magic: the same vLLM/SGLang/TRT-LLM engine that serves Llama 3 70B serves DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-70B with no changes. Architecture-defined kernels, KV cache layout, parallelism plan — all unchanged. The serving stack just sees a Llama 3 70B with different weights and reasoning-shaped behavior.

Why distill instead of train from scratch
  • Cost. A small SFT pass on 100K–10M synthetic examples is hours-to-days of compute, often two orders of magnitude cheaper than even a small pretraining run.
  • Capability transfer is targeted. You can distill specifically reasoning, refusal, code generation, or domain expertise without touching anything else.
  • Architectural compatibility. Distilled models keep the base architecture, so engine support is automatic.
  • Safety / alignment lineage. Inherits the base's safety training; you only have to validate the new behavior, not the whole model.
Distillation, fine-tuning, and RL — what's different

All three are post-training adjustments to a base model. The differences are about what supervision signal the model learns from:

MethodSignalBest for
Supervised fine-tuning (SFT)Human-curated input-output pairsTask adaptation, instruction following, format compliance
Distillation (synthetic-data)Larger model's outputs (often filtered for quality)Capability transfer, cheap reasoning models
Distillation (logit)Teacher's full probability distributionCompressing a model while preserving its calibration
RLHF / RLAIF / DPOPreferences (A vs B), reward model, or rule-based rewardsAlignment with human/policy preferences, refining behaviors that don't have a clean ground truth

In practice frontier model post-training is a sequence: pretrain → SFT (often partly distilled from a stronger model) → RL. Each stage uses a different supervision signal and serves a different purpose.

Inference implications

For the inference engineer, the operational view of a distilled model is simple:

  • It's the base architecture, period. Engine support, parallelism plan, quantization recipes, KV cache settings — all transfer from the base.
  • Behavior changes — evals must be redone. Reasoning depth, refusal patterns, output length distribution can shift dramatically. Re-tune max_tokens, sampling parameters, output-length predictors.
  • Output length tends to grow. Reasoning-distilled models emit thinking traces. Capacity planning that was right for the base is often wrong for the distill — KV cache footprint and decode time scale with output length.
  • License inheritance. A distill of a Llama base inherits Llama's license. A distill of a closed teacher (often disallowed by the teacher's terms) is a legal hazard, not just a technical one.
📖 Distilling the Knowledge in a Neural Network — Hinton et al. (2015)DeepSeek-R1 paper — distillation methodology
← Model-Hardware Co-designFine-tuning & LoRA →
Architecture Frontier › Fine-tuning & LoRA

Fine-tuning and LoRA new

Low-rank adapters, multi-tenant serving, and why your scheduler needs to know which adapter a request wants

Full fine-tuning vs LoRA

Full fine-tuning updates every parameter of a base model on a new dataset. For a 70B model in BF16 that's ~140 GB of weights to read, write, and store the optimizer state for. Cost is comparable to a substantial fraction of pretraining, and you end up with a 140 GB checkpoint per fine-tune. For a multi-tenant SaaS where every customer wants a fine-tune, this is operationally unworkable.

LoRA — Low-Rank Adaptation (Hu et al., 2021) freezes the base model's weights and trains only a small additive update ΔW = α · A · B, where A is a tall-skinny matrix (d × r) and B is short-fat (r × k). The rank r is typically 8–64. The trained adapter is roughly 0.1–1% of the base model's parameter count: tens of MBs instead of hundreds of GBs. Training takes hours instead of weeks.

The forward pass becomes:

y = W · x + α · A · B · x

Mathematically equivalent to a single matrix multiply with (W + α·A·B); you can either merge the adapter into W ahead of time (one matmul, no overhead, but you've baked in this specific adapter), or keep the LoRA branch separate (extra small matmuls per layer, but the base stays neutral and you can swap adapters per request).

Why LoRA works

The empirical observation behind LoRA: when fine-tuning a pretrained model, the change in weights ΔW tends to have low intrinsic rank. Most fine-tuning tasks just nudge the model along a low-dimensional subspace of behaviors it can already exhibit; you don't need to update every direction independently. A rank-16 adapter captures most of the useful change for typical task adaptation. For more disruptive behavior changes (a new language, a fundamentally different style), you bump the rank or move to full fine-tuning.

QLoRA — the 4-bit cousin

QLoRA (Dettmers et al., 2023) adds one operational twist: keep the base model in 4-bit quantized form (NF4) during training and inference, train the LoRA adapter in BF16 on top. This drops the GPU-memory cost of training a 70B fine-tune from "8×H100" to "single H100" — a step-change for accessibility. At inference, the merged or unmerged adapter sits on top of the quantized base; quality is essentially indistinguishable from training on the unquantized base. Most modern open-source fine-tuning toolchains (Unsloth, axolotl, PEFT) default to QLoRA.

Multi-LoRA serving — many tenants on one base model

This is the operational payoff. A single deployed 70B base model can serve dozens to hundreds of fine-tuned variants concurrently:

  • One base model in HBM (140 GB BF16, or 35 GB at INT4).
  • N adapters at ~50–500 MB each, kept warm in HBM/host RAM.
  • Per-request adapter selection — every request specifies which adapter it wants; the engine applies that adapter's LoRA branch during the forward pass.

vLLM (with multi-LoRA enabled), SGLang, and TRT-LLM all support this. The cost of swapping adapters in/out of HBM is small relative to a model load — but not free. Cold adapters (only on object store) take ~100 ms to fault in over the network, which can break SLOs if it happens on the request's critical path.

LoRA-aware routing — connecting back to scheduling

If a tenant's adapter is warm on replica A and cold on replica B, sending a request to B costs you a 100 ms+ adapter fetch on the critical path. The scheduling chapter covers this as LoRA-aware routing: hash on adapter ID, prefer the replica with the adapter already loaded, spill to other replicas only when the preferred one is over a utilization threshold. Without it, multi-LoRA serving devolves into pathological adapter thrash where every request loads its adapter from scratch and per-tenant P99 explodes.

The tight coupling: a multi-tenant LoRA deployment is not just a model decision. It's a coordinated decision across the model (LoRA-trained adapters), the engine (multi-LoRA support), the scheduler (LoRA-aware routing), and the storage tier (where adapters live and how fast you can fetch them). Pick any one in isolation and the others will not save you.

Adapter merging — when to bake LoRA in

If you only ever serve one adapter, merge it into the base ahead of time. The forward pass then runs at full base-model speed with zero LoRA overhead — typical 1–3% throughput regression goes to zero. Multi-tenant serving can't merge (you'd lose the per-request adapter switching), so they pay the small unmerged-LoRA tax. The decision is: how many adapters per replica? If exactly one, merge. If many, keep them separate.

📖 LoRA paper — Hu et al. 2021QLoRA paper — Dettmers et al. 2023vLLM multi-LoRA docs

Architecture Frontier › Quiz

Chapter 2 Quiz — Architecture Frontier

Test your understanding of MLA, multi-token prediction, and model-hardware co-design

Chapter 2 Quiz — Architecture Frontier
1 MLA achieves 93% KV cache reduction. If standard MHA needs 470 GB of KV cache for 100K tokens on a GPT-3-scale model, how much does MLA need?
470 GB × (1 - 0.93) = ~33 GB. MLA compresses all K/V information into a single low-dimensional latent vector per token per layer, then reconstructs full K/V at attention time. The extra decompression compute is negligible because decode is memory-bandwidth-bound — you're using spare arithmetic capacity.
2 Why does Kimi K2 reduce attention heads while increasing expert count? What's the serving economics argument?
Attention scales O(n²) with context length — halving heads at 128K tokens saves more than half the attention compute due to quadratic scaling. Experts scale O(1) with context length — each processes fixed compute per token regardless of sequence length. The net result: cheaper to serve at long contexts with comparable knowledge capacity. This is co-design — architecture decisions driven by serving cost, not just benchmark quality.
3 DeepSeek-V3's MTP reports 1.8× decode throughput. Why doesn't it achieve higher, like 4× for 4 prediction heads?
The auxiliary heads predict tokens N+2, N+3, N+4, but acceptance rate drops with distance — the model is less certain about N+4 than N+2. With ~70% acceptance on the first draft and declining on subsequent ones, the expected accepted tokens per step is ~1.8, not 4. This is the same acceptance-rate dynamic as external speculative decoding, just with better alignment because the draft heads were trained jointly.
← Fine-tuning & LoRAGPU Memory & Interconnects →
The Hardware › GPU Memory & Interconnects

NVLink, InfiniBand, and GPU memory access

What lives where, how fast data moves, and whether GPUs can reach into remote servers' memory directly

The GPU memory hierarchy — bandwidth determines what's possible

Inside a GPU server, there are multiple levels of memory with dramatically different speeds. Understanding these speeds is not just trivia — they directly determine which operations are bottlenecks and why certain optimizations matter so much. Before the table, some GPU architecture terms you'll see repeatedly:

  • CUDA core: The basic arithmetic unit on an NVIDIA GPU — it performs one floating-point operation. An H100 has thousands of CUDA cores working in parallel.
  • SM (Streaming Multiprocessor): A cluster of CUDA cores plus its own small fast memory. Think of each SM as a mini-processor. An H100 has 132 SMs. All SMs run simultaneously on different chunks of data.
  • Tensor Core: A specialized arithmetic unit (inside each SM) designed specifically for matrix multiplications — the core operation in neural networks. Tensor Cores compute entire small matrix multiplies in one clock cycle rather than doing individual multiplications. They're what make H100s so much faster for ML than raw CUDA core counts suggest.
  • SRAM (Static RAM): Small, extremely fast on-chip memory sitting right next to the compute units (inside each SM). Contrast with HBM which sits off-chip. SRAM is ~6x faster than HBM but ~1000x smaller.
  • Kernel (CUDA kernel): A function that runs on the GPU. When people say "launch a kernel," they mean "execute this specific operation on the GPU." Each kernel runs across many SMs in parallel. "Kernel fusion" means combining two separate operations into one kernel to avoid writing intermediate results back to slow HBM.
Memory tierLocationSize (H100 SXM)BandwidthWhat lives here
RegistersOn-chip, per CUDA core~MB totalMultiple TB/s (within SM)Numbers actively being computed right now
Shared memory / SRAM / L1On-chip, per SM~50 MB total~20 TB/s (estimated; NVIDIA does not publish official aggregate SRAM bandwidth)Tiles of data being processed by the current kernel — where FlashAttention keeps attention tiles
L2 cacheOn-chip, shared across SMs50 MB~10 TB/sRecently accessed data; reduces HBM reads
HBM (GPU RAM)On-package, off-chip80 GB3.35 TB/sModel weights, full KV cache, activations — the primary memory for inference
CPU RAM / DRAMServer motherboard512 GB – 2 TB~300 GB/s (via PCIe)KV cache overflow (swap), CPU-offloaded model layers

The HBM bandwidth of 3.35 TB/s is the most important number for LLM inference. Decode is memory-bandwidth-bound — the speed of generating each token is almost entirely determined by how fast you can move model weights from HBM to the compute units. This is why the H100 SXM (3.35 TB/s HBM) is meaningfully faster at decode than the H100 PCIe (2 TB/s), despite having identical arithmetic performance. It's also why SRAM is so precious — it's 6× faster than HBM, which is why FlashAttention's SRAM-tiling strategy produces such large speedups.

NVLink — GPU to GPU within one server

NVLink is NVIDIA's proprietary high-bandwidth interconnect between GPUs within the same physical server. On DGX H100 systems, 8 GPUs are connected via NVLink 4.0 at 900 GB/s total bidirectional bandwidth per GPU. An NVSwitch chip creates a full all-to-all mesh — any GPU can communicate with any other GPU simultaneously at full bandwidth, not just adjacent pairs.

This bandwidth (900 GB/s) is what makes tensor parallelism practical. Tensor parallelism requires an all-reduce operation after every attention and FFN layer — every GPU must share its partial result with all others. At 900 GB/s, this all-reduce completes in microseconds, making the communication overhead small relative to the computation savings. At InfiniBand speeds (50 GB/s), the same all-reduce would dominate the step time and destroy any parallelism benefit.

InfiniBand — server to server across the cluster

InfiniBand is the high-speed network fabric connecting different physical servers in a GPU cluster. NVIDIA's Quantum-2 InfiniBand at 400 Gbps per port (~50 GB/s) is the standard in H100 clusters. Compare: NVLink at 900 GB/s is 18× faster than InfiniBand at 50 GB/s.

This enormous bandwidth gap is why tensor parallelism stays intra-node (NVLink) while pipeline parallelism and context parallelism work inter-node (InfiniBand). Tensor parallelism generates high-frequency, high-bandwidth communication (all-reduce every layer). Pipeline parallelism and context parallelism generate lower-frequency communication (activation tensors once per layer for PP; K/V chunks per ring step for CP), which InfiniBand can handle acceptably.

Can GPUs access memory on different servers directly?

Yes — through RDMA (Remote Direct Memory Access) over InfiniBand. RDMA allows a GPU on Server A to read from or write to the HBM of a GPU on Server B without either CPU being involved at all. The data path is: GPU HBM → GPU NIC → InfiniBand switch fabric → GPU NIC → GPU HBM on the remote server. No CPU copies, no OS kernel involvement, no context switches.

GPUDirect RDMA is the NVIDIA feature that enables this. Without it, the data path would include two extra CPU copies: GPU HBM → CPU RAM → NIC (on send side) and NIC → CPU RAM → GPU HBM (on receive side). With GPUDirect RDMA, the CPU is bypassed entirely, achieving effective throughput of ~40 GB/s on a 400 Gbps InfiniBand link.

This is exactly how KV cache transfer works in prefill/decode disaggregation: after prefill finishes on the prefill worker, the KV cache (potentially 10-100 GB for long prompts) is transferred via GPUDirect RDMA directly into the decode worker's HBM. The decode worker can start generating the first output token as soon as the KV cache arrives.

NVLink 4.0InfiniBand NDR 400GPCIe 5.0 (CPU-GPU)
Bandwidth900 GB/s per GPU bidirectional~50 GB/s per port~64 GB/s
ScopeWithin one server (up to 8 GPUs)Across servers in a clusterCPU to GPU within one server
Latency~1–2 microseconds~1–2 microseconds~1–10 microseconds
Use caseTensor parallelism all-reducePipeline parallelism activations, KV cache transfer, training gradient syncLoading model weights from CPU, data preprocessing
The 18× bandwidth gap between NVLink and InfiniBand is the single most important number for understanding why parallelism strategies are designed the way they are. Anything requiring frequent high-bandwidth communication must stay within one NVLink node. Anything that can tolerate lower-frequency or lower-bandwidth communication can span nodes over InfiniBand.
Deep Dive — B200/GB200 and NVLink 5.0: changing the calculus

NVLink 5.0 specs: 1.8 TB/s bidirectional per GPU (2x over H100's 900 GB/s). But the bigger shift is NVL72: 72 B200 GPUs connected via NVLink Switch with 130 TB/s bisection bandwidth at rack scale. All 72 GPUs behave like one large shared-memory system for NVLink traffic. This enables TP=72 within a single rack without InfiniBand-class latency penalties — potentially eliminating pipeline parallelism for 70B-class models entirely. However, across racks you're still limited to InfiniBand 400/800 Gbps. The fundamental constraint hasn't changed — it's just that the "within one NVLink domain" now encompasses an entire rack of 72 GPUs instead of 8.

The Hardware › Parallelism Types

Parallelism types in inference

Data, tensor, pipeline, expert, context — what each splits, why it matters, and why pipeline is not recommended for inference

No single GPU can hold or efficiently compute a large LLM alone. There are five main strategies for distributing the work. Each splits a different dimension. Production deployments combine multiple strategies simultaneously. Understanding which to use when is exactly the kind of judgment that distinguishes infra leaders from infra engineers.

Visual: data parallelism — replicate everything, split the requests

1. Data parallelism
1. Data parallelism

The simplest strategy: make N complete copies of the model, each on its own GPU or set of GPUs. Each copy (replica) handles different requests independently. A load balancer distributes incoming requests across replicas.

  • What gets split: The request stream — different requests go to different replicas
  • Communication needed: None during inference. Each replica is fully independent. (In training, gradients must be synchronized across replicas, but this doesn't apply at inference time.)
  • Requirement: The full model must fit on one GPU (or whatever the base replica size is)
  • Best for: High-throughput serving of models that fit on 1-2 GPUs (7B, 13B). Maximum simplicity. Horizontal scaling.
  • Limitation: Doesn't help when the model is too large for one GPU, and doesn't reduce per-request latency at all.
2. Tensor parallelism (recommended for inference)

Split individual weight matrices across GPUs. Every GPU holds a slice (shard) of every layer and cooperates on every single forward pass. If W_Q is 12,288×12,288 and you use 8-way TP, each GPU holds a 12,288×1,536 slice. Every GPU computes its portion of the matrix multiply in parallel. After each layer, an all-reduce operation combines the results before proceeding to the next layer. All-reduce is a collective communication pattern where every GPU sends its partial result to every other GPU, and all GPUs end up with the same combined (summed) result — think of it as "everyone shares their partial answer, everyone gets the full answer." It's the most common communication pattern in distributed ML.

  • What gets split: Weight matrices within each layer, distributed across GPUs within one node
  • Communication needed: All-reduce at every layer — very frequent, high-bandwidth required
  • Requirement: Fast interconnect — NVLink (900 GB/s). Only practical within a single 8-GPU node. The all-reduce latency over InfiniBand (50 GB/s) would dominate and erase the parallelism benefit.
  • Best for: Reducing latency (TTFT and TBT). 8-way TP means each token's forward pass runs 8× faster since all 8 GPUs work on it simultaneously. This is the primary tool for hitting low-latency SLAs.
  • Limitation: Cannot scale beyond one NVLink-connected node (8 GPUs typically) without becoming communication-bottlenecked.
Worked example: 8-way tensor parallelism on LLaMA 70B
1W_Q matrix: 8,192 × 8,192 = 67M params. With 8-way TP, each GPU holds an 8,192 × 1,024 slice = 8.4M params.
2Forward pass: All 8 GPUs compute their slice of Q = input × W_Q_slice simultaneously. Each produces a partial result.
3All-reduce: The 8 partial results are summed across all GPUs via NVLink. Data volume: 8,192 × 2 bytes (FP16) = 16 KB per all-reduce. At 900 GB/s NVLink: ~0.02 μs. At 50 GB/s InfiniBand: ~0.3 μs (15× slower — why TP stays intra-node).
4Total all-reduces per forward pass: 2 per layer × 80 layers = 160 all-reduces. Total communication overhead at NVLink: ~3 μs. At InfiniBand: ~48 μs — starts to matter at decode step times of ~20ms.
Net effect: each GPU does 1/8 of the work → forward pass runs ~8× faster → TTFT and TBT reduced ~8×. This is the primary tool for low-latency SLAs.
3. Pipeline parallelism — why it's not recommended for inference

Split the model's layers across GPUs sequentially. GPU 1 holds layers 1-24, GPU 2 holds layers 25-48, GPU 3 holds layers 49-72, GPU 4 holds layers 73-96. A token flows through each GPU one after another: GPU 1 processes it and passes activations to GPU 2, which passes to GPU 3, etc.

Visual: pipeline bubbles — why PP wastes GPU time at inference

4-way pipeline parallelism processing a single request. Each GPU holds 24 layers. Notice the idle time (bubbles).

Time → (each block = one stage's compute time)
GPU 1 (L1-24)
compute
idle — waiting for GPUs 2,3,4 to finish
step 2
GPU 2 (L25-48)
bubble
compute
idle
step 2
GPU 3 (L49-72)
bubble
compute
step 2
GPU 4 (L73-96)
bubble
compute
step 2
At inference batch size 1: each GPU is idle 75% of the time. Bubbles consume 3 out of 4 time slots.
Compare TP: all 4 GPUs work on the same token simultaneously — zero bubbles, 4× speedup.

This seems efficient but has two fundamental problems at inference scale:

  • Pipeline bubbles: At the start of each batch, GPU 2 cannot begin until GPU 1 finishes processing the first micro-batch. At the end, GPU 1 sits idle while the rest of the pipeline finishes. These idle periods — "bubbles" — scale with the number of pipeline stages. In training with batch sizes of thousands, bubbles are a small percentage of total compute time. In inference with small batches (sometimes single requests), bubbles can consume 30-50% of step time.
  • Does not reduce latency: A single token still travels sequentially through every GPU stage. Pipeline parallelism does not reduce per-token latency — it may even increase it. Tensor parallelism is fundamentally different: all 8 GPUs work on the same token simultaneously, actually reducing per-token latency.
Deep Dive — TP all-reduce: it's 2 per layer, not 1

A transformer layer has two sub-layers that each need all-reduce: (1) after attention output projection — Q/K/V projections are column-parallel (no comm needed), but the output projection W_O is row-parallel, requiring all-reduce; (2) after FFN's second linear layer — FFN's up-projection is column-parallel, but the down-projection is row-parallel, requiring all-reduce. Total: 2N all-reduce operations for N layers. For LLaMA-3 70B (80 layers): 160 all-reduces per forward pass. This is why NVLink's 900 GB/s bandwidth is non-negotiable — at InfiniBand 50 GB/s, 160 all-reduces per decode step would completely dominate step time.

Pipeline parallelism is widely used in training (where large batches minimize bubble impact) but is generally avoided for inference serving. Use tensor parallelism instead for latency reduction. Pipeline parallelism for inference is a last resort when the model is too large for even a fully NVLink-connected node.
Visual: expert parallelism — tokens dispatched to wherever their expert lives
Batch of 8 tokens → Router assigns each to top-2 experts

Load imbalance: GPU 0 has 3× the work of GPU 3. GPU 3 waits idle. This is the core EP challenge.
After processing, another All-to-All shuffle sends results back to original token positions.
4. Expert parallelism (MoE models only)

In MoE models, distribute different experts across different GPUs. Each GPU holds a subset of the expert FFNs. When the router selects experts for a token, that token must be sent to whichever GPU holds its assigned expert — requiring all-to-all communication at every MoE layer.

  • What gets split: Expert FFN modules, distributed by expert index across GPUs
  • Communication needed: All-to-all at every MoE layer — unlike all-reduce (where every GPU sends the same data and gets back the combined result), all-to-all means every GPU sends different data to every other GPU (each token goes to whichever GPU holds its assigned expert). This is the most expensive collective operation in distributed ML because the communication pattern is unpredictable and cannot be compressed.
  • Operational problem: Popular experts become hot GPUs. If the router consistently sends 30% of tokens to expert #3, GPU 3 is overloaded while GPU 7 sits mostly idle. Load imbalance is a real production challenge.
  • Best for: MoE models where you must distribute experts across GPUs to fit the total parameter count
5. Context parallelism — for very long sequences

Context parallelism (CP) handles 128k, 512k, or million-token contexts by splitting the sequence itself across GPUs. Each GPU is responsible for a contiguous range of tokens. The challenge: attention requires every token to see every other token's K and V vectors. CP solves this with ring attention.

Visual: ring attention with 4 GPUs

100K-token sequence split into 4 chunks of 25K tokens each. K/V rotates around the ring while Q stays local.

GPU 1
Q: T₁₋₂₅ₖ (stays)
GPU 2
Q: T₂₅ₖ₋₅₀ₖ (stays)
GPU 3
Q: T₅₀ₖ₋₇₅ₖ (stays)
GPU 4
Q: T₇₅ₖ₋₁₀₀ₖ (stays)
↓ K/V chunks rotate around the ring ↓
Step 1:
KV₁ → attn(Q₁,KV₁)
KV₂ → attn(Q₂,KV₂)
KV₃ → attn(Q₃,KV₃)
KV₄ → attn(Q₄,KV₄)
Rotate →
Each GPU sends its KV chunk to the next GPU in the ring (overlapped with compute)
Step 2:
KV₄ → attn(Q₁,KV₄)
KV₁ → attn(Q₂,KV₁)
KV₂ → attn(Q₃,KV₂)
KV₃ → attn(Q₄,KV₃)
Rotate →
...repeat
Step 3:
KV₃ → attn(Q₁,KV₃)
KV₄ → attn(Q₂,KV₄)
KV₁ → attn(Q₃,KV₁)
KV₂ → attn(Q₄,KV₂)
After 3 rotations (N-1 steps): every GPU has seen all KV chunks. Full attention computed.
K/V transfer is overlapped with computation — minimal overhead.

How ring attention works: Arrange GPUs in a ring. Each starts with its own Q, K, V chunk. In each step: (1) each GPU computes attention between its Q chunk and the K/V it currently holds, accumulating a partial result; (2) simultaneously, all GPUs rotate their K/V to the next GPU in the ring. After N-1 rotations, every GPU has seen all K/V chunks and has the full attention output for its token range. Critically, the K/V transfer is overlapped with computation — while GPU 1 computes attention with the K/V it just received, the next K/V chunk is already being transferred. Minimal overhead.

Difference from sequence parallelism: sequence parallelism only splits non-attention parts of the layer (LayerNorm, FFN), using an all-gather to reconstruct the full sequence before attention. Context parallelism splits the sequence including attention, using ring attention instead of all-gather. CP scales to much longer contexts because it avoids the O(N) all-gather that sequence parallelism requires.

Production combinations
CombinationTypical setupWhy this combination
TP within node8-way tensor parallelism inside one 8-GPU serverNVLink is fast enough for the frequent all-reduces. Reduces per-token latency 8×.
TP + DP8-way TP per replica; N replicas for throughput scalingLow latency per request from TP, plus horizontal throughput scaling from DP.
TP + CPTP within node; CP across nodes for long-context requestsHandles sequences too long for a single node's HBM. Ring attention over InfiniBand.
TP + EP (MoE)TP for attention layers; EP for FFN expert layersNatural fit for MoE — each component uses the parallelism strategy suited to its structure.
Choosing parallelism by model architecture — the dense-vs-MoE topology rule

For a given model the parallelism plan is largely dictated by architecture. Two canonical recipes cover most production deployments:

ArchitectureSingle-node fit?Recommended topologyNotationReasoning
Dense, fits in one node (Llama 3 70B FP8 on 8×H100)YesTP across all 8 GPUs in the node, DP across nodes for throughputTP8 × DP_NNVLink handles per-layer all-reduce; latency-optimal.
Dense, multi-node (Llama 3 405B FP16 on 16 GPUs)NoTP within each node (NVLink) + PP across nodes (InfiniBand)TP8 × PP2Keep frequent all-reduces on NVLink; pay PP bubbles only at the inter-node boundary, not per layer.
Sparse MoE (DeepSeek V3, Qwen3-235B-A22B)Usually noEP across many GPUs for the FFN/expert layers; TP for the dense attention layersEP16 (or EP32+) with TP for attentionEach token activates only 2–8 experts, so EP communication volume is much lower than TP would be on the same parameters. Attention is dense and stays under TP.

Key principle: match the parallelism strategy to the layer's structure. Dense layers (attention, dense FFN) want TP because every token touches every weight — splitting weights and synchronizing per layer is the natural fit. Sparse MoE FFN layers want EP because each token only touches a handful of experts, so it's cheaper to ship the token to the GPU holding its expert than to fragment the expert across GPUs. Modern MoE engines (vLLM, SGLang, TRT-LLM, Dynamo) implement mixed TP+EP automatically — the same forward pass uses TP collectives in attention layers and EP all-to-all in expert layers, with no manual splitting required.

Memory floor in any topology: min_GPUs ≈ ceil(precision_bytes × params / (HBM_per_GPU − KV_headroom)), where you reserve at least 50% of HBM for KV cache and activations. That's the lower bound; latency SLAs and batch-size targets usually push the actual count higher.
