The Hardware › Quiz

Chapter 3 Quiz — The Hardware

Test your understanding of GPU memory, interconnects, and parallelism strategies

Chapter 3 Quiz — The Hardware
1 Why can't you do tensor parallelism across two servers connected by InfiniBand (50 GB/s) instead of NVLink (900 GB/s)?
TP requires an all-reduce after every layer — 2 all-reduces × 80 layers = 160 per forward pass. At NVLink speeds, each takes ~0.02μs (total ~3μs overhead). At InfiniBand speeds, each takes ~0.3μs (total ~48μs). As decode step times are ~20ms, NVLink overhead is 0.015% — negligible. InfiniBand overhead is 0.24% — still small per-step, but the 18× bandwidth gap means larger tensors or higher batch sizes would saturate InfiniBand quickly. The fundamental issue: TP generates high-frequency, high-bandwidth communication that requires NVLink.
2 A 70B model's KV cache for one 100K-token request is ~32 GB (GQA, FP16). How long does it take to transfer this via InfiniBand RDMA (50 GB/s) for disaggregated inference?
32 GB / 50 GB/s = ~640ms. This exceeds most TTFT SLO targets, which is why KV transfer is the emerging bottleneck in disaggregated systems. Mitigations: layer-by-layer pipelining (start transferring layer 1 while computing layer 2), FP8 KV cache (halves to ~320ms), or NVLink within a rack (~36ms at 900 GB/s).
3 In pipeline parallelism with 4 GPUs and batch size 1, what fraction of GPU time is wasted in bubbles?
75%. GPU 1 computes for 1 time slot, then waits 3 slots for GPUs 2-4 to cascade. Each GPU is active only 1/4 of the time. This is why PP is avoided for inference (small batches) but works for training (large batches minimize the bubble fraction). Tensor parallelism has zero bubbles — all GPUs work on the same data simultaneously.
4 In ring attention with 4 GPUs on a 100K-token sequence, how many rotation steps are needed before every GPU has computed full attention?
3 steps (N-1 rotations for N GPUs). Each GPU starts with its own KV chunk and receives one new chunk per step. After 3 rotations, every GPU has seen all 4 KV chunks and accumulated the full attention result for its Q range. The key insight: K/V transfer to the next GPU is overlapped with the current step's computation, hiding transfer latency.
← Parallelism TypesContinuous Batching →
Optimization › Continuous Batching

Continuous batching

The single most important throughput improvement in modern LLM serving

Key latency metrics — defined here, used everywhere
MetricFull nameWhat it measuresWho cares
TTFTTime To First TokenTime from when a request is submitted until the very first output token is generated. Dominated by how long prefill takes.Interactive chat — users feel this as "how long until it starts responding"
TBTTime Between TokensTime between each successive output token during streaming generation. Dominated by decode step time.Streaming UX — users feel this as the smoothness and speed of the response stream
E2E latencyEnd-to-end latencyTotal time from request submission to the last output token. Equals TTFT + (output_length × TBT).Non-streaming batch jobs, evaluations
ThroughputTokens per secondTotal output tokens generated per second across all concurrent users. Determines how much the service costs to run.Capacity planning, cost optimization, business economics
The two phases of inference and why they create a scheduling problem

Prefill processes the entire input prompt in one shot. All tokens are available upfront, so they can all be processed in parallel in a single forward pass. Prefill is fast per token but uses the GPU heavily for a burst of time.

Decode generates output one token at a time. You cannot generate token N+1 until you know token N. Each decode step processes just one new token (plus the full KV cache of all previous tokens). Decode is inherently sequential, slow per token, and memory-bandwidth-bound — you're mostly loading weights, not doing arithmetic.

Different requests take wildly different amounts of time. A classification request might need 5 output tokens. A detailed explanation might need 800. With a fixed batch, everyone in the batch must wait for the slowest request to finish before the GPU can do anything else.

The three batching modes
ModeBatch compositionHow slots are freedWhere you see it
Static batchingFixed at batch start; the same N sequences run together until every one of them finishes.All slots free at once when the slowest sequence completes.Classical CV/NLP serving (Triton, ONNX Runtime), embedding inference. Pre-Orca LLM serving.
Dynamic batchingServer waits a small window (e.g., 10 ms) to accumulate arriving requests, then forms a static batch.Same as static — all-or-nothing at the end of the batch.Triton dynamic-batching mode, embedding/reranker services where requests are uniform-cost.
Continuous (in-flight) batchingThe batch is reformed at every decode step (~5–50 ms). Finished sequences leave; queued requests join.Per-sequence, immediately on EOS — slot refilled the same step.vLLM, SGLang, TRT-LLM, NVIDIA Dynamo — the LLM serving default.

Static and dynamic both batch at request granularity: pick which requests run together, then commit until they finish. Continuous batches at token granularity: every decode step is a fresh batching decision. That shift — from request-granularity to token-granularity scheduling — is why LLM throughput improved so dramatically in 2023. The rest of this section is the deep dive on why request-granularity batching is so wasteful for LLM workloads.

Naive batching — the broken approach

Collect N requests, process them all together as a batch, wait until every request has generated its final token, release the whole batch, collect the next N. Simple to implement. Terrible for GPU utilization.

Consider a batch of 32 requests where 31 finish in 20 tokens but 1 runs to 500 tokens. The GPU is serving 1 request for ~96% of the batch's runtime. GPU utilization: ~30-40%. Every new request queued behind this batch waits the full duration of the slowest member, regardless of how short its own output will be.

Analogy: a charter bus that drives to every passenger's final destination before returning to pick up new passengers. Continuous batching is a city bus — it drops passengers at each stop and immediately picks up new riders at that same stop. The vehicle is always full.
Visual: naive vs continuous batching

Naive batching — GPU waits for the slowest request. Short requests (A, B, C) pad while D runs.

Req A (20 tok)
A
idle — waiting for D
Req B (15 tok)
B
idle — waiting for D
Req C (25 tok)
C
idle — waiting for D
Req D (500 tok)
D generates 500 tokens...
Req E (waiting)
blocked — can't start until entire batch completes

Continuous batching — slots freed immediately. E starts as soon as A finishes.

Req A (20 tok)
A
✓ done, slot freed
Req B (15 tok)
B
✓ done
Req D (500 tok)
D keeps generating — never blocks others
Req E (new)
pf
E starts in A's freed slot
Active decode Prefill Idle / waiting
Continuous batching — how it actually works

At the end of every single decode step (every ~5-50ms), the scheduler wakes up and asks: which sequences just generated an end-of-sequence token and are finished? Those slots are immediately freed. Are there requests waiting in the queue? If memory allows, admit them into the freed slots right now. The batch composition changes dynamically at every decode step.

A request that needed only 5 output tokens is gone in 5 steps and its GPU memory is immediately reallocated to a new request. A 500-token request occupies its slot for 500 steps, but never blocks other short requests from using their own slots. GPU utilization: 80-90%+.

Continuous batching (also called iteration-level scheduling) was first introduced by Orca (Yu et al., OSDI 2022). vLLM (2023) adopted continuous batching and added PagedAttention as its core innovation — the paged KV cache management described in the next section. Together, these two ideas are the reason LLM serving costs dropped dramatically in 2023. Every major serving system (SGLang, TRT-LLM, Dynamo) implements continuous batching as a baseline.

The scheduler's constraints — this is where your EC2 expertise applies

The scheduler deciding what enters the batch at each step is solving a real-time bin-packing problem under two simultaneous constraints:

  • KV cache memory constraint: Every active sequence holds pages of KV cache in GPU HBM. Each new token generated by each sequence adds to that KV cache. The total KV cache usage across all active sequences cannot exceed available HBM. If you admit too many long-running sequences, you run out of memory.
  • Compute constraint: Larger batches mean more tokens to process per decode step. Beyond a certain batch size, adding more sequences doesn't increase throughput because the GPU is already saturated. You have to balance memory pressure against compute efficiency.
  • Preemption: If a request grows longer than expected and starts consuming too much KV cache memory, the scheduler can preempt it — save its KV cache to CPU RAM, free the HBM slot for other requests, and restore the sequence later when memory permits. This is swap-based preemption, exactly like OS virtual memory paging.
This scheduler is doing exactly what EC2 Auto Scaling does: bin-packing requests into available GPU memory while meeting latency SLAs. The difference is the bin size is measured in HBM bytes, the items are KV cache pages, and the SLA is TTFT and TBT rather than CPU utilization.
Deep Dive — How expensive is preemption in practice?

Concrete numbers for LLaMA-3 70B (GQA, 8 KV heads, 128 d_head, 80 layers, FP16): KV cache per token = 2 × 80 × 8 × 128 × 2 bytes = 320 KB/token. A 10k-token sequence = ~3.3 GB. Swapping to CPU at PCIe 5.0 (~64 GB/s) takes ~51 ms — roughly 1.5-2x one decode step. A round-trip (swap out + restore) costs ~100ms minimum.

Production reality: Swap-based preemption is rarely used at scale. vLLM V1 defaults to recompute preemption (drop the KV cache, re-run prefill when rescheduled) because recompute is often cheaper than PCIe transfer for moderate-length sequences, and CPU memory itself becomes a bottleneck under sustained load. The more common production responses to KV cache pressure: (1) reject new requests via admission control before OOM, (2) use GQA/MLA to shrink per-token KV footprint, (3) FP8 KV quantization (halves KV memory), (4) limit max_model_len. True swap preemption is a fallback for unexpected spikes, not a steady-state strategy.

📖 Continuous Batching for LLM Inference — Anyscale blog (the canonical explanation)vLLM / PagedAttention paper
← Parallelism TypesKV Cache & PagedAttention →
Optimization › KV Cache & PagedAttention

KV cache, prompt caching, and routing-aware scheduling

Two different caching mechanisms at two different levels — where each decision happens

The KV cache — why it exists and what happens without it

During autoregressive decode, every new token needs to run attention against all previous tokens. Attention requires the K and V vectors of every previous token. You have two options: (1) recompute K and V from scratch for every previous token on every decode step, or (2) compute them once and store them.

Without a KV cache: generating token N requires running attention over tokens 1 through N-1, recomputing all their K and V vectors. Generating token N+1 requires doing it all again for tokens 1 through N. The total compute scales as O(N²) in the output length. For a 500-token response, you'd recompute token 1's K and V vectors 499 times. This is completely infeasible at any meaningful sequence length.

With a KV cache: you compute each token's K and V vectors once during prefill, store them in GPU HBM, and append new tokens as they're generated. Each decode step costs O(N) not O(N²) — you just load the cached K and V for all previous tokens and run attention once. This is mandatory. The KV cache is not an optimization; it's what makes LLM inference computationally feasible.

PagedAttention — how vLLM manages the KV cache

Naive KV cache management: allocate a contiguous block of GPU memory upfront for each request, sized for its maximum possible sequence length. Problems: (1) you don't know how long a response will be, so you over-allocate. (2) Contiguous allocation causes fragmentation — many small unusable gaps between allocations as requests finish at different lengths. vLLM measured 60-80% memory waste from this fragmentation.

PagedAttention manages KV cache exactly like an OS manages virtual memory with pages:

  • Physical KV cache memory is divided into fixed-size blocks (e.g., each block holds 16 tokens worth of K and V vectors across all layers)
  • A page table maps each sequence's logical token positions to physical blocks (just like virtual memory page tables map virtual addresses to physical addresses)
  • Blocks are allocated on demand as sequences grow — no upfront reservation
  • Blocks are non-contiguous in physical memory — a sequence can occupy scattered blocks
  • Blocks can be shared between sequences that have a common prefix (enables prompt caching at zero marginal cost)

Result: memory fragmentation drops from ~60% to near-zero. The same HBM can now hold significantly more active sequences, directly increasing batch size and throughput.

Visual: memory fragmentation before vs after PagedAttention
Before — contiguous allocation

Pre-allocate max length per request. Gaps form as requests finish at different lengths.

60-80% wasted (red = allocated but unused)
After — PagedAttention (paged blocks)

Allocate on demand in 16-token blocks. Non-contiguous. No pre-reservation.

Near-zero waste. Green = prefix cache (shared)
Active KV blocks Fragmentation (wasted) Prefix cache (shared) Free
Prompt caching — cross-request KV reuse

Many requests share a common prefix. A customer support bot always starts with a 2,000-token system prompt. A RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation — a pattern where relevant documents are retrieved from a database and prepended to the prompt to give the model fresh knowledge) pipeline always includes the same retrieved documents. Without prompt caching, every request independently runs prefill on that shared prefix, generating the same KV vectors over and over. This is pure compute waste.

Prompt caching stores the computed KV vectors for known prefixes and reuses them. When a new request arrives, the scheduler checks: does the first N tokens of this request's prompt match any cached prefix (by hashing the token sequence)? If yes, skip prefill for those tokens and load their cached KV vectors directly. For a 2,000-token system prompt on LLaMA-3 70B, this saves roughly 100ms of prefill time on every single request. Anthropic exposes this as a first-class API feature.

Routing-aware scheduling — the distributed systems problem

In a multi-worker cluster (many GPU machines), prompt caching creates a cache locality problem. Worker A has cached the KV vectors for your system prompt. Worker B has not. If a new request is routed to Worker B, it must compute the prefix from scratch. If routed to Worker A, it gets the cache hit for free.

The load balancer must therefore be cache-aware. The naive approach — consistent hashing on the prompt prefix — routes all requests with the same prefix to the same worker. Excellent for cache hits. Terrible if that prefix is very popular: one worker gets all traffic while others sit idle.

The opposite extreme — pure load balancing (least connections, round robin) — distributes load perfectly but completely ignores cache locality. Cache hit rate approaches zero.

Production systems navigate this with hybrid policies: consistent hash routing as the primary signal, with overflow to other workers when the primary is above some utilization threshold. The exact threshold and fallback logic are tuned per deployment. This is an active research area with no settled best approach.

Caching typeWhere the decision happensWhat triggers itScope
KV cache (within-session)Automatically inside the attention kernel — no scheduling decision neededEvery decode step, always, without exceptionSingle request, single GPU worker
Prompt caching (cross-request)The scheduler / prefix cache manager, at request admission timeHash of request prefix matches a stored cache entryAcross requests on one worker
Routing-aware schedulingThe load balancer, before the request is dispatched to any workerRequest arrival — route to the worker most likely to have a cache hitCluster-level, across all workers
Radix attention — tree-based prefix caching (SGLang's approach)

Hash-based prefix caching (vLLM's approach) works by hashing blocks of 16 tokens and looking up matches. This works well for linear shared prefixes — the same system prompt across all requests. But real workloads often have branching prefix patterns:

  • A chatbot with 5 different system prompts, each with 3 different few-shot example sets = 15 prefix branches
  • A RAG pipeline where different documents are prepended to the same base prompt
  • Multi-turn conversations that share early turns but diverge at different points

Radix attention (SGLang) stores cached KV blocks in a radix tree (also called a prefix tree or trie) — a tree data structure where each node represents a segment of tokens, and branches represent divergence points. To look up a cache match, walk the tree from root, following the token sequence. You match as far as the tree goes, then prefill only the remaining tokens.

Radix tree with 4 cached conversations:
[system prompt: "You are a helpful assistant..."] ← root (shared by all)
├── [few-shot: math examples] ← branch A
├── [user: "solve x²+3x=0"] → KV cached (request 1)
└── [user: "what is 17×23?"] → KV cached (request 2)
├── [few-shot: code examples] ← branch B
└── [user: "write a sort fn"] → KV cached (request 3)
└── [few-shot: writing examples] ← branch C
└── [user: "draft an email"] → KV cached (request 4)

When a new request arrives with math few-shot + a new question, the tree match finds the system prompt (root) + math examples (branch A) in the cache. Only the new user question needs prefill. With hash-based caching, branch A and branch B would be separate unrelated cache entries — the shared system prompt root would be duplicated in each.

Hash-based (vLLM)Radix tree (SGLang)
Match granularityFixed 16-token blocksArbitrary-length segments at tree nodes
Branching prefixesEach branch stored independently — shared root duplicatedShared root stored once, branches fork naturally
Multi-turn reuseHash chain: change at token 50 invalidates block 4+Tree walk: match up to the divergence point, reuse everything before it
Memory efficiencyGood for linear prefixesBetter for branching workloads (shared root not duplicated)
Lookup costO(1) per block hashO(prefix_length) tree walk — but amortized across shared segments
EvictionLRU per blockLRU per leaf, with reference counting on shared internal nodes

SGLang's sgl-router exploits this by routing requests with similar prefixes to the same worker, maximizing radix tree hit rates. This reportedly gives 1.9× throughput improvement from cache-aware routing alone.

Deep Dive — Prefix cache matching granularity

A single-token change mid-prefix does NOT invalidate the entire cache in block-based systems — it invalidates from the block boundary containing the changed token onward.

vLLM: Uses content-addressed block hashing. KV cache is organized into blocks of 16 tokens. Each block is hashed based on its token content and its prefix hash (a hash chain). If a 1024-token prompt changes at token 50 with block size 16, the first 3 blocks (tokens 0-47) still cache-hit; block 4 (tokens 48-63) misses because its content changed; all subsequent blocks miss because their prefix hashes depend on block 4. Partial reuse at block granularity.

SGLang: Uses a radix tree (RadixAttention) with token-level granularity at the tree node level, storing segments of arbitrary length as nodes. This matches at finer granularity than vLLM's 16-token blocks — better for workloads with variable-length shared prefixes (few-shot examples that differ in length). Both systems default to LRU eviction for cache management.

Where KV cache lives — the 4-tier storage hierarchy

Everything above assumes KV cache lives in GPU HBM. In production at scale, KV cache spills across a four-tier hierarchy that trades bandwidth for capacity. Naming the tiers makes serving discussions much sharper:

TierWhereBandwidthCapacity per nodeWhat lives here
G1GPU HBM1–8 TB/s10s–100s of GBActive sequences, hot prefixes
G2Host CPU RAM10s–100s of GB/s (PCIe / NVLink-C2C)100s of GB to multi-TBIdle conversations, evicted hot caches, swap targets
G3Local NVMe SSD5–10 GB/sTBsPer-node cold cache, large RAG document KVs
G4Networked SSD / object store~GB/s10s of TBCluster-wide shared KV — any GPU can resume any session

Each tier is roughly an order of magnitude slower and larger than the one above. Engines decide where to demote a KV block based on access frequency, conversation idle time, and remaining HBM budget. Restoration cost on cache miss is the dominant tradeoff: a G1 hit is free, a G2 fetch costs PCIe latency, G3 costs SSD I/O, and G4 costs network I/O — but at G4 capacities you can keep KV alive for hours of conversation history without evicting all the way to "re-prefill from text."

Beyond strict prefix matching — LMCache and CacheBlend

vLLM's hash cache and SGLang's radix tree both require an exact prefix match to reuse KV. Real RAG and chat workloads frequently have non-prefix overlap: the same document chunk appears at different positions in different prompts. Two recent systems extend reuse beyond strict prefix:

  • LMCache — a multi-tier KV store (G2/G3/G4) that detaches KV cache from a specific engine instance. Caches survive restarts, can be shared across vLLM/SGLang replicas, and ship across the cluster on demand. Effectively makes KV cache portable.
  • CacheBlend — reuses KV vectors for repeated content even when it appears at different positional offsets, by selectively recomputing only the small set of tokens whose attention statistics actually change. Trades a small recompute cost for much higher cache hit rate on RAG workloads where the same passages appear in different prompt positions.
The KV cache as a managed resource — Dynamo KVBM and the "global KV cache"

NVIDIA Dynamo's KV Block Manager (KVBM) is the productized form of this hierarchy: a single manager owns G1/G2/G3/G4 placement across a cluster, exposes "global KV cache" semantics (any worker can resume any session), and pipelines block transfers with NIXL-based RDMA. The conceptual shift is treating KV cache as a first-class storage tier — like an object store for token state — rather than ephemeral per-replica memory. This is the architectural endpoint of the trajectory that started with PagedAttention: from "no caching" → "per-request KV" → "per-replica prefix cache" → "cluster-wide global KV cache" with explicit storage management.

Optimization › Chunked Prefill

Chunked prefill

Solving the interference problem between prefill and decode in a shared serving system

Why prefill and decode interfere — the core problem

Prefill and decode have fundamentally incompatible resource profiles running on the same GPU:

Prefill is compute-bound. Processing 10,000 input tokens in parallel saturates the GPU's arithmetic units. A long prefill job can take 200-500ms and fully occupies the GPU during that time.

Decode is memory-bandwidth-bound. Each decode step loads model weights from HBM and generates one token. Each step takes ~10-20ms and barely uses the GPU's arithmetic units.

The problem: in a continuous batching system, these two types of work share the same GPU and the same forward pass scheduler. When a new request arrives with a 10,000-token prompt, its prefill must run before it can join the decode stream. But while that prefill runs — for 200-500ms — every single ongoing decode sequence in the batch is completely blocked. They cannot generate their next token. Every user currently receiving a streaming response sees a complete freeze in the stream.

Why you can't just run them simultaneously

Your first instinct might be: why not run prefill and decode in parallel, as separate forward passes? The GPU executes one forward pass at a time. You cannot interleave two separate forward passes — the hardware doesn't support it. Whatever is in the current batch is what gets computed, and nothing else proceeds until that pass is done. The only lever you have is: what goes into each forward pass?

Visual: decode stall without vs with chunked prefill

Without chunked prefill — 10K-token prefill blocks all ongoing decodes for 500ms

New request
prefill 10,000 tokens (500ms) — GPU monopolized
User A decode
STALLED — no tokens generated for 500ms
User B decode
STALLED — streaming freezes completely

With chunked prefill (512-token chunks) — prefill interleaved, decodes never stall

New request
512
512
512
512
...
~20 chunks over 400ms
User A decode
smooth — 1 token every 25ms
User B decode
smooth — no freeze
How chunked prefill works

Instead of processing an entire long prompt in one forward pass, break it into fixed-size chunks (e.g., 512 tokens per chunk). Each forward pass then becomes a mixed batch:

  • 512 tokens from the new request's prompt (one prefill chunk)
  • 1 new token from each of the ongoing decode sequences

All of this runs in one forward pass together. Prefill and decode tokens coexist in the same batch, processed simultaneously. After that forward pass (~20-25ms), decode sequences got their next token (no freeze), and the new request has prefilled 512 of its 10,000 tokens. Next step: another 512 tokens of prefill plus another round of decode. After ~20 steps, the full prompt is prefilled.

The latency tradeoffs
MetricWithout chunked prefillWith chunked prefillWhy
TTFT for the new long-prompt requestLower if no concurrent long prefills; unpredictable otherwiseHigher but predictable — spread across N steps regardless of other trafficPrefill interleaved with decode takes more wall-clock time to complete, but is bounded and deterministic
TBT for concurrent decode usersSpikes 500ms+ whenever a long prefill runsSlight consistent increase (e.g., 20ms → 26ms per step)Prefill chunk shares GPU time each step rather than monopolizing it
P99 TBT (tail latency)Unbounded — entirely depends on whether a long prefill hits concurrentlyPredictable and bounded to at most one chunk duration (e.g., always ≤ 30ms per step)No single request can stall others for more than one chunk's worth of time
For interactive chat applications — which is most of Anthropic's use cases — TBT consistency matters more than TTFT. A user streaming a response cares deeply about smooth token flow. They're less sensitive to a slightly longer wait before the first token. Chunked prefill optimizes for the right metric: smooth streaming over minimum absolute TTFT.
Deep Dive — How does the attention mask work in a mixed batch?

Each token type follows its own visibility rules via a structured block-diagonal attention mask. Prefill tokens within the current chunk attend to: (a) all tokens in preceding chunks (already in the paged KV cache), and (b) each other causally within the current chunk. Decode tokens attend only to their own per-sequence KV cache. The sequences never see each other. Frameworks like FlashInfer implement this with variable-length attention kernels (varlen attention) that handle heterogeneous mask shapes efficiently.

Compute penalty: Yes, there is interference. Prefill is compute-bound (matrix-matrix multiplications); decode is memory-bandwidth-bound (matrix-vector). Running them together means the GPU can't fully optimize for either regime. Sarathi-Serve showed this explicitly — there's a TTFT vs TBT Pareto curve controlled by chunk size. In vLLM, the chunk size (--max-num-batched-tokens) is a tunable parameter precisely because the right tradeoff is workload-dependent.

Chunk size as a tunable knob

The chunk size (e.g., 512 tokens) is a configurable parameter that directly trades TTFT against TBT smoothness. Larger chunks mean fewer steps to complete prefill (lower TTFT) but each step is more disruptive to decode (higher per-step TBT increase). Smaller chunks mean smoother decode but longer time to complete prefill. Production systems tune this based on their SLA priorities.

Chunked prefill in disaggregated inference

In a disaggregated setup (separate prefill and decode fleets), the original motivation for chunked prefill disappears — there are no decode sequences on the prefill worker to interfere with. But chunked prefill remains useful for two other reasons:

  • Head-of-line blocking within the prefill fleet: Without chunking, a single 50,000-token prompt monopolizes a prefill worker for several seconds while shorter prompts queue behind it. Chunking lets the scheduler interleave short and long prompts, improving average TTFT across the prefill fleet.
  • KV cache pipelining to decode workers: Completed chunks of KV cache can be shipped to decode workers incrementally while prefill is still running on remaining chunks. A decode worker can potentially start generating the first output tokens before the full prefill has finished — overlapping prefill computation with KV transfer and early decode. This is an active research area.
📖 Sarathi-Serve — the canonical chunked prefill paper

Optimization › Prefix Caching

Prefix caching new

Skip the prefill you've already done — reuse the KV cache across any request that shares a leading token sequence.

Why prefill is the work you most want to skip

Prefill is dominated by self-attention, which is O(N²) in sequence length. A 10,000-token system prompt that is identical across every request to your service is, in the naive setup, 100 million attention ops the GPU recomputes from scratch on every request. Decode is cheap by comparison — one new K/V per step. So the highest-leverage cache in any LLM serving stack is not "the answer cache." It is the KV cache of the prompt prefix: if request 2 starts with the same tokens as request 1, you should not be running attention over those tokens again.

This is what prefix caching does. It indexes the KV blocks produced during prefill and, when a new request arrives, walks its tokens against the index to find the longest matching prefix. Whatever it finds, the engine skips — only the suffix tokens need attention computed against the cached K and V. The savings are entirely on TTFT and prefill throughput; decode cost is unchanged.

The win is enormous on three real workloads: long shared system prompts (think "the 4,000-token policy preamble every request inherits"), multi-turn chat (each turn extends a stable prefix), and agent loops where tool outputs append to a growing scratchpad. In all three the cached prefix can be 80%+ of the prompt, and TTFT drops accordingly.
Two implementations: hash-chained blocks vs radix tree

vLLM and TensorRT-LLM use a hash-chained block cache. The KV cache is already paged into fixed-size blocks (vLLM default 16 tokens, TRT-LLM default 128). Each block gets an identity hash(parent_hash, block_tokens, extras), where extras include the LoRA ID, multimodal hash, and an optional per-tenant cache_salt. A child block's hash chains its parent's, so prefix matching is just a chain of O(1) hash lookups — fast and simple. The catch: only full blocks are reusable. A request whose prompt is 1,025 tokens long with a 16-token block size shares 64 blocks; the trailing 1 token is wasted because partial blocks don't get a stable hash.

SGLang uses RadixAttention. All active KV cache entries live in a radix tree whose edges are token spans and whose leaves point to paged KV. Insert, match, and evict are tree operations. The win shows up when generations branch: parallel sampling, beam search, tree-of-thought, agent fan-out — every divergence is automatically a tree split, and every convergent ancestor is shared in GPU memory exactly once. A flat hash table either misses these or duplicates them.

PropertyHash-chained (vLLM, TRT-LLM)Radix tree (SGLang)
LookupO(1) per blockO(prefix length)
GranularityBlock-aligned onlyToken-level
Branching workloadsTreats branches as independent promptsNative subtree sharing
Bookkeeping costHash table + free queueTree maintenance + pointer chasing
Best forHigh-QPS chat with stable system promptsAgent / RAG / ToT / structured-output traffic
Eviction: refcount plus LRU, sharing one paged pool

Both families combine reference counting with LRU. A block in use by an active request cannot be evicted, full stop. Among free blocks, the least-recently-used goes first. vLLM keeps a doubly-linked free queue and evicts from the head; when a request finishes, its blocks are queued in reverse order so the deepest (most-reusable) prefix blocks survive longest. SGLang recursively evicts leaf nodes of the radix tree under the same LRU rule.

Operationally the prefix cache and the live decode KV pool share the same paged memory. There is no separate "cache RAM." A hot prefix cache shrinks the maximum batch you can decode concurrently — the throughput-vs-TTFT knob the operator turns is "how aggressively are we willing to keep cold prefixes around at the cost of fewer concurrent decodes."

What drives hit rate — and what destroys it

Hit rate climbs with workload structure: a long stable system prompt across all tenants, multi-turn chats where each user keeps extending the same prefix, few-shot templates, agent loops with a stable scratchpad, RAG when the same retrieved context is reused across follow-ups. It collapses when prompts are unique per request, or when the system prompt is regenerated per call, or when traffic is sharded such that consecutive requests for the same prefix land on different replicas.

That last one — sharding — is why cache-aware routing matters as much as the cache itself. A round-robin load balancer trashes the hit rate by design: it sends each request to whichever replica is "next" rather than to the one that already holds its prefix. Cache-aware routers (SGLang's scheduler, vLLM's Production Stack, NVIDIA Dynamo's Smart Router, AIBrix) hash the prompt prefix and consistent-hash replicas. Same prefix, same replica, near-zero TTFT. Random routing, and you might as well have no prefix cache.

Failure modes
  • Exact-prefix only. A single different token at position 0 invalidates the whole chain. Whitespace differences, BOS handling, chat-template version drift, and tokenizer changes all silently kill reuse.
  • Tokenizer determinism. Two replicas running slightly different tokenizer versions (or NFC vs NFKC normalization) will produce different IDs and zero hits across them.
  • LoRA and multimodal. The hash must include the LoRA ID and image hash or two completely different requests collide on the same block.
  • Timing window. TRT-LLM only reuses a block "if the second request launches after the first request completed." Bursty traffic with all-new prefixes evicts before reuse can land.
  • Cross-tenant collisions. Hash-based caches can in principle collide. Use sha256 and a per-tenant cache_salt so only requests with the same salt can reuse cached KV.
Numbers worth remembering
  • SGLang's RadixAttention paper reports up to 6.4× higher throughput across nine workloads vs prior SOTA, and the LMSYS launch blog cites up to 5× vs vLLM and Guidance on agent / ToT / JSON / RAG traffic.
  • In agent loops, public benchmarks routinely show 60–90% TTFT reduction on the shared-prefix portion of the prompt.
  • Block size is the granularity floor for partial-prompt reuse: 16 tokens (vLLM) is finer-grained than 128 (TRT-LLM default) but means more bookkeeping per cached prompt.
📖 vLLM prefix caching design doc SGLang RadixAttention announcement TensorRT-LLM KV cache reuse

Optimization › FlashAttention

FlashAttention

Why standard attention is slow and memory-hungry — and how tiling in SRAM fixes both problems at once

The two problems with standard attention

Memory problem: Standard attention computes a score matrix of shape (sequence_length × sequence_length). For a 32,768-token context, that's over a billion entries. In FP16 that's ~2 GB just for this one intermediate result per layer. This matrix must be written to HBM, read back for softmax, written again, read again for the weighted sum of Values. Four HBM round-trips per attention layer. For a 96-layer model with long contexts: hundreds of GBs of HBM traffic just for attention intermediates.

Speed problem: All this HBM traffic is the bottleneck, not arithmetic. The GPU's Tensor Cores are fast enough to compute attention quickly, but they're starving — they finish their computation and wait for the next chunk of data to arrive from HBM. Standard attention is memory-bandwidth-bound, meaning you cannot make it faster by adding more TFLOPS.

These two problems compound: the O(n²) memory requirement also imposes a hard limit on context length — once the score matrix exceeds available HBM, you cannot run attention at all.

Visual: standard attention vs FlashAttention memory access
Standard Attention
HBM Load Q, K → compute QKT
HBM Write full N×N score matrix
HBM Read back for softmax
HBM Write softmax result
HBM Read again × V
4+ HBM round-trips. Memory: O(n²)
32K context → 2 GB score matrix per layer
FlashAttention (tiled in SRAM)
HBM Load Q tile → SRAM
SRAM For each K,V tile: compute scores, online softmax, accumulate
SRAM Score matrix only exists in SRAM tiles — never in HBM
HBM Write final output — once
1 HBM read + 1 write. Memory: O(n)
32K context → only SRAM tiles needed (~KB)
The FlashAttention solution — tiling in SRAM

FlashAttention's insight: you don't need to materialize the full N×N score matrix to compute the final attention output. You can compute it in small tiles that fit in SRAM (on-chip memory, ~20 TB/s) using a technique called "online softmax."

Normal softmax requires seeing all scores before normalizing: softmax(x_i) = exp(x_i) / sum(exp(x_j) for all j). To compute this you need all the x_j values at once — hence the full N×N matrix. Online softmax computes the same result incrementally: as you process each tile, you maintain a running maximum and running normalization factor, updating them tile by tile. The final result is mathematically identical to standard softmax but never requires the full matrix in memory at once.

  • Load a tile of Q (from HBM to SRAM)
  • For each tile of K and V: load them into SRAM, compute the partial attention scores for this Q×K tile, update the running max and normalization factor, accumulate the partial weighted V sum
  • Write the final attention output for this Q tile back to HBM — exactly once
  • Repeat for the next Q tile

The N×N score matrix is computed in tiles of SRAM and discarded immediately — it never exists in HBM. Q, K, V are each read from HBM exactly once. The output is written to HBM exactly once.

What changes concretely
Standard attentionFlashAttention
Memory footprintO(n²) — full score matrix in HBM; grows quadratically with context lengthO(n) — only SRAM tiles in flight at any time; linear in context length
HBM reads/writesMultiple full passes over Q, K, V plus score matrixQ, K, V each read once; output written once; score matrix never in HBM
SpeedMemory-bandwidth-bound — starved waiting for HBM dataApproaches compute-bound — data stays in SRAM; 2-4× faster in practice
Max context lengthLimited by HBM capacity (score matrix must fit)Limited only by total compute budget; enables very long contexts
Versions and what each improved

FlashAttention-1 (2022, Tri Dao et al.): The original tiled SRAM algorithm. Proved the concept with 2-4× wall-clock speedup over standard PyTorch attention.

FlashAttention-2 (2023): Better parallelism — FA1 parallelized only across batch and heads. FA2 also parallelizes across the sequence dimension, better utilizing all GPU thread blocks. ~2× faster than FA1 on modern hardware.

FlashAttention-3 (2024): Exploits H100-specific hardware features: asynchronous memory movement (Tensor Memory Accelerator instructions), FP8 Tensor Core support, and better pipelining between WGMMA (warpgroup matrix-multiply-accumulate) operations and softmax. Approaches the hardware SOL for attention on H100.

Why it enables everything else

FlashAttention isn't just a speed optimization — it's an enabler. Without it, 128k token contexts would require tens of GBs just for attention score matrices per layer, making them economically infeasible. With FlashAttention, the attention memory cost is O(n) in sequence length rather than O(n²). This shifts the limiting constraint from attention memory to KV cache memory — which is then addressed by GQA, MQA, MLA, and KV cache quantization.

Deep Dive — FlashAttention did NOT reduce compute complexity

Critical distinction: FlashAttention reduced attention memory from O(n2) to O(n). But attention compute is still O(n2) — the QKT matmul is O(n2 x d) FLOPs, and the AV matmul is O(n2 x d) FLOPs. These are fundamental to the attention mechanism and FlashAttention doesn't change them — it just performs them in SRAM tiles instead of materializing the full n x n matrix in HBM.

This is why long-context inference is still slow even with FlashAttention: the compute is unavoidably quadratic. FlashAttention removed the memory explosion (making 100k+ contexts physically feasible), but the O(n2) compute remains. This is exactly why co-design (reducing attention heads, replacing attention with linear-scaling mechanisms like MoE experts or state-space layers) is the next frontier: reducing the number of O(n2) operations rather than making each one more memory-efficient.

Almost every long-context serving system in production today depends on FlashAttention. It exemplifies the unhobbler pattern perfectly: it changed attention memory from O(n2) to O(n), which isn't just a constant-factor improvement — it changed what context lengths are physically possible to serve.
📖 FlashAttention (Dao et al. 2022)FlashAttention-2FlashAttention-3

Optimization › Quantization Deep Dive

Quantization deep dive

AWQ, GPTQ, FP8, INT8, INT4, GGUF — what each does and when to use it

Quantization reduces the numerical precision of model weights (and sometimes activations) to use less memory and run faster. A 70B model in FP16 = 140 GB. In INT4 = ~35 GB. The memory difference determines whether a model is servable on a given hardware configuration at all. See the Number Formats section for why each format behaves differently — that foundation explains why the tradeoffs below exist.

Two fundamentally different approaches

Weight-only quantization (AWQ, GPTQ, GGUF): Only the stored weights are low-precision. Before the actual matrix multiplication — called a GEMM (General Matrix Multiply, pronounced "gem" — the single most important operation in neural network inference; every attention projection and every FFN layer is a GEMM) — weights are dequantized back to FP16. The arithmetic happens at FP16 precision. You save on memory and on HBM bandwidth (loading 4× less data per decode step), but you get no compute speedup — the GEMM itself runs at FP16.

Weight + activation quantization (FP8, INT8/SmoothQuant): Both weights AND the intermediate activation values flowing between layers are quantized. The matrix multiplication itself runs at low precision end-to-end. You get memory savings AND compute speedup — FP8 GEMMs are natively ~2× faster than FP16 GEMMs on H100's Tensor Cores. The challenge: activation distributions have outliers that destroy naive quantization quality (see Number Formats section for why).

Granularity — where the scale factor lives

A quantized tensor is stored as low-precision values plus a higher-precision scale factor that maps them back to real numbers (e.g., real = int_value × scale). The granularity question is: how many values share one scale?

GranularityHow many values per scaleCostQualityWhere used
Per-tensorOne scale for the whole weight matrix (millions of values)Cheapest — one extra number per tensorWorst — a single outlier forces the scale to stretch and crushes everything elseNaive INT8, early FP16→INT8 attempts
Per-channelOne scale per output channel (one per row or column)Small — a vector of scales per tensorMuch better — outliers in one channel don't pollute others. Standard for INT8 weights.SmoothQuant, classical INT8 weight quant
Per-block (microscaling)One scale per fixed block of values (e.g., every 32 elements)~3% storage overheadBest — outliers are isolated to a single block; quality approaches FP16MXFP8/MXFP4, NVFP4, GGUF "K-quants"

Finer granularity = better quality but more scale-factor overhead. The industry trajectory is steadily down: per-tensor → per-channel → per-block, and block sizes have dropped from 128 to 32 to 16. Two block formats matter for current hardware:

  • MXFP8 / MXFP4 (Open Compute "Microscaling" standard) — block of 32 elements, one shared FP8 scale. Native on Blackwell. MXFP4 doubles compute throughput vs FP8 and roughly halves weight memory.
  • NVFP4 (NVIDIA, Blackwell) — block of 16 elements with a per-block FP8 scale, plus a global FP32 scale per tensor. The smaller block size and two-level scaling preserve quality on attention layers where MXFP4 alone struggles.
QAT vs PTQ — and the new wave of quantization-native models

All the methods above (AWQ, GPTQ, SmoothQuant, FP8 PTQ) are post-training quantization (PTQ) — start from an FP16 checkpoint, calibrate on a small dataset, ship the quantized weights. Cheap, fast, no retraining. Quality loss is small but nonzero.

Quantization-aware training (QAT) bakes the quantization noise into training itself: the forward pass simulates low-precision arithmetic, gradients still flow at FP32, and the model learns weights that are robust to the quantization grid. Far more expensive (full training run), but the resulting model has effectively no quality gap vs the FP16 reference.

Frontier models are increasingly shipped with quantization built in:

  • OpenAI GPT-OSS — released natively in MXFP4. The published weights are the quantized weights; there is no FP16 reference checkpoint to fall back to.
  • Kimi K2 Thinking (Moonshot) — INT4 with QAT.
  • DeepSeek V3 / R1 — trained with FP8 mixed precision end-to-end; the FP8 weights are the model.

Operationally this changes a serving question from "how aggressively can I quantize this model?" to "what's the lowest-precision format the model already supports natively?" — your engine and hardware just need to match.

GPTQ — layer-wise weight quantization with Hessian correction

GPTQ quantizes weights to INT4 or INT3 one layer at a time. For each layer, it solves a least-squares optimization: find the quantized weights that minimize the change in that layer's output on a calibration dataset (a small, representative sample of real data — typically 128-512 text passages — that the quantization algorithm runs through the model to observe how each weight affects outputs; the model is not being trained on this data, just measured). GPTQ uses second-order gradient information (the Hessian matrix — a mathematical structure that captures how sensitive the model's output is to changes in each individual weight; weights with large Hessian values are "important" and should be quantized more carefully, while weights with small values can be rounded aggressively with minimal quality loss) to identify which weights are most sensitive and compensate them first. This produces better quality than naively rounding to the nearest integer, especially at aggressive bit-widths like INT3.

Requirements: a calibration dataset (typically a few hundred samples from the training distribution), an offline quantization process that takes hours for large models, and a specialized fast GEMM kernel (like Marlin) to actually realize the inference speedup.

AWQ — activation-aware weight quantization

AWQ's key insight: not all weight channels are equally important. Channels that correspond to large activation magnitudes carry more signal and should be quantized more carefully. AWQ identifies these "salient" channels (by running a calibration pass to observe actual activation magnitudes), scales those channels before quantizing (making their values more spread out within the INT4 range), and applies the inverse scaling at inference time (which is absorbed into an adjacent operation at zero cost).

Result: generally better accuracy than GPTQ at INT4, especially on reasoning tasks. Also requires calibration data. Both AWQ and GPTQ are weight-only — they need the Marlin kernel (or equivalent) to achieve fast inference despite the INT4 storage.

FP8 — the current production standard on H100

FP8 quantizes both weights and activations, keeping floating-point format (sign + exponent + mantissa). The H100's Tensor Cores natively compute FP8 GEMMs at approximately 2× the throughput of FP16 GEMMs. FP8's exponent bits handle activation outliers automatically — the format can represent both 0.001 and 500 in the same tensor without precision collapse (unlike INT8).

FP8 E4M3 (4 exponent bits, 3 mantissa) is the standard for model weights and most activations. FP8 E5M2 (5 exponent bits, 2 mantissa) trades precision for higher dynamic range, sometimes used for gradient computations during training. No calibration dataset needed for many FP8 quantization approaches — the floating-point format handles the distribution automatically. Quality loss versus FP16 is minimal, often undetectable on standard benchmarks. FP8 is converging on being the default inference format for H100 serving in 2024-2025.

SmoothQuant INT8 — the A100 approach

SmoothQuant uses INT8 for both weights and activations to get the ~1.5× compute speedup from INT8 Tensor Cores on A100. The problem: activation outliers make naive INT8 collapse (see Number Formats). SmoothQuant's solution: multiply activations by a per-channel scaling factor s (making outlier channels smaller) and divide the corresponding weight channel by s (making those weights larger). The product is mathematically identical. But now both distributions are more uniform and INT8-friendly. The scaling factor is computed offline from a calibration pass. This "smoothing" operation migrates quantization difficulty from activations to weights, and weights are easier to quantize because their distribution is more regular.

GGUF — CPU and edge inference

GGUF is a file format from llama.cpp optimized for CPU inference. It supports mixed precision within a single model — sensitive layers (attention, early/late layers) get higher precision (Q6_K, Q8_0), while FFN layers get more aggressive compression (Q2_K, Q4_K). This layer-level heterogeneity preserves quality better than applying the same bit-width uniformly. GGUF models use CPU SIMD instructions (AVX-512, ARM NEON) for fast dequantization on laptops and edge devices. Not used in GPU production serving — AWQ/FP8 are faster on GPUs.

MethodFormatWhat's quantizedCompute speedupQualityBest use case
GPTQINT4/INT3Weights onlyMemory + bandwidth onlyGood at INT4; degrades at INT3Memory-constrained GPU serving when AWQ not available
AWQINT4Weights onlyMemory + bandwidth onlyBest INT4 qualityDefault INT4 GPU serving — best quality/compression tradeoff
FP8FP8 E4M3/E5M2Weights + activations~2× on H100Near FP16 — often indistinguishableDefault H100 production inference in 2024-2025
SmoothQuant INT8INT8Weights + activations~1.5× on A100GoodA100 production inference where FP8 is unavailable
GGUFQ2_K–Q8_0 mixedWeights onlyCPU SIMD-optimizedVaries; Q4_K_M is sweet spotLocal CPU inference (laptops, edge devices)
BnB NF4INT4 (NF4 format)Weights onlyMemory onlyGood for distribution-aware use casesQLoRA fine-tuning; not optimized for fast inference
The sensitivity ladder — what to quantize aggressively, what to keep precise

Not all parts of a model tolerate quantization equally. The empirical sensitivity ladder, from easiest to quantize down to most fragile:

ComponentAggressive precision toleratedWhy
Weights (FFN, MLP)INT4 / MXFP4 with negligible quality lossDistributions are smooth and well-behaved; AWQ/GPTQ exploit per-channel structure.
ActivationsFP8 generally fine; INT8 needs SmoothQuant; INT4 rarely viableOutlier channels create extreme dynamic range. FP8's exponent absorbs them; integer formats need rescaling tricks.
KV cacheFP8 or INT8 widely deployed; INT4 emergingQuantizing the cache halves its memory footprint, doubling effective batch size or context length. Sensitive in long contexts because errors compound across decode steps.
Attention internals (softmax, scores)Keep FP16/BF16Softmax is exquisitely sensitive to small score differences — small quantization noise cascades into wrong token choices. Universally exempted from low-precision arithmetic.

Consequence: a "FP8 model" in production usually means FP8 weights, FP8 activations, FP8 (or higher) KV cache, and BF16 attention math. NVIDIA's TensorRT Model Optimizer and similar tools automate the layer-by-layer sensitivity analysis to decide the per-tensor precision automatically.

Tooling — where production quantization is converging
  • NVIDIA TensorRT Model Optimizer (ModelOpt) — unified toolkit for PTQ, QAT, sparsity, and pruning, output targets TRT-LLM and vLLM. Handles MXFP4/NVFP4 calibration on Blackwell.
  • Neural Magic llm-compressor — vLLM-native quantization pipeline (FP8, INT8, INT4 with GPTQ/AWQ).
  • AutoAWQ / AutoGPTQ — open-source reference implementations of AWQ and GPTQ; output formats consumed directly by vLLM/SGLang.
  • Unsloth dynamic quantization — heterogeneous bit-widths within one model (e.g., 1.58-bit for some layers, 4-bit for others), tuned per-layer for quality. Popular for fitting frontier models on consumer hardware.
  • llama.cpp / GGUF — CPU and edge inference with mixed-precision K-quants.
Deep Dive — Calibration data sensitivity for AWQ/GPTQ

How sensitive? Moderately. The calibration set shapes the quantization error distribution — a mismatch between calibration and serving distribution causes higher perplexity on out-of-distribution inputs. If calibrated on English (WikiText-2, C4) but serving multilingual traffic, quality degrades 2-5 perplexity points on low-resource languages, with morphologically complex languages (Arabic, Korean, Finnish) most affected. Fix: mix ~10-20% of target-language samples into calibration data to substantially recover quality without hurting English.

How much data? GPTQ: 128-512 samples of 2048 tokens each (~262k tokens standard). AWQ: even less — 32-128 samples since it only identifies salient weight channels, not full Hessians. Quality is surprisingly robust to calibration size once you hit ~100 samples; the distribution matters more than quantity. AWQ calibration for 70B takes ~10-30 minutes on a single GPU; GPTQ takes 1-4 hours.

Optimization › Marlin Kernel

Marlin kernel

Why INT4 models were slower than FP16 — and how a fused kernel fixed it by never leaving SRAM

The problem — naive INT4 inference is slower than FP16

After quantizing a model's weights to INT4 (via AWQ or GPTQ), you've reduced memory by 4×. But to actually run inference, you need to do matrix multiplications (GEMMs). The GPU's Tensor Cores operate in FP16 (or FP8 on H100), not INT4. Before the GEMM can run, INT4 weights must be dequantized back to FP16.

The naive implementation does this in two sequential kernel calls: (1) a dequantize kernel that loads INT4 weights from HBM, converts them to FP16, and writes the FP16 result back to HBM; (2) a GEMM kernel that loads the FP16 weights from HBM and runs the matrix multiply. You've read HBM twice for the weights — once in INT4 form, once in FP16. Since HBM bandwidth is the bottleneck at decode, you've negated most of the benefit of quantization.

The result: INT4 models with naive dequantization can actually run slower than FP16 models at decode, despite using 4× less memory at rest. This was a major practical obstacle to INT4 deployment until fused kernels like Marlin solved it.

What Marlin does — fused dequantize + GEMM in one kernel

Marlin is a custom CUDA kernel that fuses dequantization and GEMM into a single operation. The key insight: SRAM (on-chip memory, ~20 TB/s) is 6× faster than HBM (3.35 TB/s). If you can keep the dequantized FP16 weights in SRAM/registers rather than writing them back to HBM, you avoid the second HBM read entirely.

Marlin's tiling strategy for each tile of the weight matrix:

  • Load from HBM to SRAM: Load a small tile of INT4 weights from HBM into shared memory. This is the only HBM read for these weights.
  • Prefetch while computing: While computing with the current tile, asynchronously prefetch the next INT4 tile from HBM into SRAM. Computation and memory transfer are pipelined — no stalls.
  • Dequantize in registers: Convert the INT4 values to FP16 directly in registers. The FP16 form exists only momentarily in registers — it never touches HBM or even SRAM.
  • GEMM with FP16: Run the matrix multiply using the FP16 weights now in registers against the activations. Fast Tensor Core operations.
  • Repeat: The next tile is already in SRAM due to prefetching. No stall between tiles.

Net result: INT4 weights are read from HBM exactly once (as INT4, 4× smaller than FP16). The dequantized FP16 values exist only transiently in registers and are consumed immediately. You've gone from 2 HBM reads per weight (naive) to 0.25 HBM reads per equivalent FP16 weight (fused INT4).

The actual speedup

Marlin achieves near-theoretical-peak GEMM throughput for INT4 quantized models on A100 and H100. In practice: 3-4× faster than naive dequantize-then-multiply. An AWQ INT4 model with the Marlin kernel can actually outperform the same model in FP16 at decode time, because you're loading 4× less data from HBM per decode step and the dequantization is essentially free.

The general principle — minimize HBM reads, keep work in SRAM

Marlin implements the same fundamental principle as FlashAttention: minimize HBM reads, do all computation in SRAM/registers, pipeline memory loads with computation to hide latency. This principle is the foundation of all high-performance GPU kernel engineering. Whenever a paper or blog post claims a "fused kernel" provides speedup, this is what they mean: two sequential operations with an HBM round-trip between them have been collapsed into one operation where intermediate results stay on-chip.

The bandwidth gap between SRAM (~20 TB/s) and HBM (~3.35 TB/s) is 6×. Every HBM read you eliminate, or replace with an SRAM read, unlocks 6× faster access to that data. This is why kernel engineering — which seems like low-level plumbing — produces such large end-to-end speedups in LLM inference.
📖 Marlin paperMarlin GitHub

Optimization › LoRA & Tensor Parallelism at Inference

LoRA and tensor parallelism — two knobs, two very different cost shapes new

How adapter serving and TP degree each move the throughput / latency / memory dials — and how they interact

Why these two together

LoRA and tensor parallelism are both "deployment-time" knobs — you don't retrain the base model to use them. But they push on completely different bottlenecks. LoRA changes what's in the batch (which adapter each request needs); TP changes how the model is laid out across GPUs (which layer math runs where). Tuning one without thinking about the other is how teams end up with a 4-GPU deployment that's slower than a 2-GPU one, or a multi-tenant LoRA server that mysteriously falls off a cliff at 30 adapters.

LoRA at inference — the multi-tenant story

A LoRA adapter is a pair of low-rank matrices (A: d×r, B: r×d, r typically 8–64) that get added to specific base-model weight matrices: W' = W + B·A. Two ways to serve them at inference time:

  • Merged weights. Compute W + B·A once at load time, throw the adapter away, serve the merged model exactly like a normal model. Zero per-request overhead. But: one model = one adapter. You can't batch requests targeting different adapters together.
  • Dynamic / unmerged. Keep the base model resident once, keep N adapters in GPU memory as separate small tensors, apply x·W + (x·A)·B per request based on which adapter that request needs. Adds two small matmuls per LoRA-touched layer per request. Lets you batch many tenants' requests together against one base model.

Production multi-tenant servers (vLLM, TGI, SGLang) all use the unmerged path with specialized batched-LoRA kernels — the most cited is Punica (the SGMV / BGMV kernels), which group requests in the batch by their adapter so each adapter's matmul is itself a batched GEMM rather than N tiny ones. S-LoRA extended this with a unified paging scheme so adapters and KV cache share the same memory pool.

What LoRA actually costs you at inference
  • Compute: Small. The adapter math is two GEMMs of size (batch × d × r) and (batch × r × d). With r=16 and d=4096, that's ~0.5% extra FLOPs per attention/FFN layer. On a memory-bandwidth-bound decode step, this is essentially free.
  • Memory: ~10–50 MB per adapter (depends on rank and how many layers it targets). 100 adapters ≈ 1–5 GB — fits comfortably alongside a 70B FP8 base model on an 8×H100 node. The cap is usually GPU memory minus model minus KV cache headroom, not adapter count per se.
  • Bandwidth: The hidden cost. Each LoRA-aware kernel has to load A and B matrices for every request in the batch — and with heterogeneous adapters across the batch, you lose the "load weights once, reuse across batch" amortization that makes batched inference fast in the first place. Punica's whole point is to recover that amortization by grouping same-adapter requests.
  • Throughput shape: A batch of 32 requests all using adapter A serves nearly as fast as a batch of 32 with no LoRA. A batch of 32 requests across 32 different adapters can be 2–4× slower because every adapter's matmul runs on a batch of 1. Adapter diversity in the batch matters more than adapter count in the system.
Rule of thumb: if your traffic concentrates on a handful of "hot" adapters, dynamic LoRA is a near-free way to serve dozens-to-hundreds of fine-tunes from one base model. If every request hits a different adapter, you're paying close to the cost of running N separate models, just without the memory blowup.
Tensor parallelism at inference — what TP=N actually buys you

Tensor parallelism shards individual weight matrices across N GPUs. For attention: split heads across GPUs (each GPU computes its share of heads, then all-gather). For FFN: split the up-projection column-wise and the down-projection row-wise (column-parallel + row-parallel pattern), then all-reduce. Every layer ends with a collective communication step.

  • Memory: Each GPU holds 1/N of the weights and 1/N of the KV cache. This is the only reason to use TP for many models — a 70B FP16 model is ~140 GB and won't fit on one 80 GB H100, so TP=2 is forced. For a 7B model on an H100, TP=1 is almost always right.
  • Compute per GPU: Drops to roughly 1/N. In compute-bound regimes (long prefill, large batch), this gives near-linear speedup.
  • Bandwidth per GPU: Also drops to ~1/N for weight loading. This is where decode benefits — a 70B model on TP=8 means each GPU only loads ~9 GB of weights per decode step instead of 70 GB.
  • Communication overhead: Two all-reduces per transformer layer. On NVLink (~600 GB/s on H100 NVL), this is a few hundred microseconds per layer. Across 80 layers per decode step, that's tens of milliseconds. Off NVLink (PCIe, or cross-node without NVSwitch) it's an order of magnitude worse and you can easily lose the speedup entirely.
When TP helps decode latency vs. when it hurts throughput

The clean mental model: TP trades per-token latency for aggregate throughput, with the conversion rate set by how much the all-reduce eats.

  • Latency-bound, large model, NVLink-connected: TP wins clearly. A 70B model decoding at TP=4 can be ~2.5–3× faster per-token than TP=1 (which doesn't even fit). Each GPU has less HBM traffic per step; the all-reduce is small.
  • Throughput-bound, large batch, model fits on one GPU: TP usually loses. You've doubled the GPU count but each step now includes communication. Two TP=1 replicas serving in data-parallel will out-throughput one TP=2 instance on the same hardware.
  • Sweet spot — model barely fits at TP=N: Often best to pick the smallest TP that fits the model + KV cache + batch headroom, then scale out with data parallelism. Going to TP=N+1 "for safety" usually costs throughput.
  • Cross-node TP: Avoid unless you have NVSwitch / NVLink across nodes. Otherwise, use pipeline or expert parallelism for the cross-node hop and keep TP within a single NVLink domain (typically 8 GPUs).
A worked sketch — Llama-3 70B on 8×H100

Same hardware, same model, three layouts:

  • TP=8, DP=1 (one giant instance). Lowest per-token latency. Best choice for chat with strict TTFT/ITL SLOs at moderate concurrency. Throughput per GPU is the lowest of the three because of all-reduce overhead.
  • TP=4, DP=2 (two instances). Common production sweet spot. Each instance still benefits from 4-way weight sharding; you double effective concurrency by running two replicas. Often 1.4–1.7× higher total tokens/s than TP=8 at slightly worse per-token latency.
  • TP=2, DP=4 (four instances). Highest aggregate throughput if the model + KV cache + max batch fits at TP=2. Per-token latency is worst. Best for offline batch inference.
Where the two knobs meet

LoRA serving and TP interact in a few specific places:

  • Adapters get sharded too. Under TP, each LoRA's A and B matrices are split the same way as the base layer they target (column-parallel A, row-parallel B for FFN-style adapters). Punica and the vLLM LoRA kernels are TP-aware and handle this automatically — but custom LoRA code often isn't.
  • Cross-adapter batching is harder under TP. Each TP rank now needs the right shard of the right adapter for each request in the batch. Memory pressure on each rank goes up, and the per-adapter matmul is now a smaller GEMM (less amortization headroom). Multi-tenant LoRA servers usually prefer lower TP than single-tenant servers of the same model, all else equal.
  • Practical guidance: If you're serving 50+ adapters on one base model, picking TP=4 over TP=8 (when both fit) often gives meaningfully better throughput because the LoRA matmuls become better-amortized per rank.
📖 Punica — multi-tenant LoRA servingS-LoRA — paged adapter servingMegatron-LM — original TP recipe
← Marlin KernelSpeculative Decoding →
Optimization › Speculative Decoding

Speculative decoding

Using spare GPU compute during decode to propose tokens in parallel — potentially 2-3× faster without quality loss

The opportunity — decode is compute-underutilized

Decode is memory-bandwidth-bound. Each decode step loads all model weights from HBM to generate one token. The arithmetic units spend most of their time waiting for data to arrive — they are not the bottleneck. This means there is spare arithmetic compute capacity during decode that is simply going unused.

For a large model (70B+), each decode step might take 20-50ms. Generating 500 output tokens takes 10-25 seconds of wall-clock time. The GPU is genuinely busy — HBM is running at high utilization — but the arithmetic units are underutilized because data loading is the limiting factor. Speculative decoding exploits this spare arithmetic capacity.

The core idea — draft then verify in parallel

A small, fast "draft" model generates N candidate tokens very quickly — each step takes maybe 1ms because it's a small model. Then the large "verifier" model processes all N candidate tokens in a single parallel forward pass.

Why can the verifier process N candidates in parallel? Normally, tokens must be generated sequentially because each token depends on the previous. But for verification, you already have all N proposed tokens — you can feed them all in simultaneously, just like prefill. The verifier runs one forward pass and at each of the N positions asks: "does my probability distribution agree with the proposed token?" If yes, accept. If no, reject and use the verifier's prediction instead. You continue from the first rejection point.

If the draft model proposes 4 tokens and the verifier accepts 3, you've advanced 3 tokens in roughly the time of 1 large model decode step. The key insight: the verifier's single parallel pass over N tokens costs approximately the same as 1 sequential decode step, because the work is the same — you're just doing it in one shot instead of N separate shots.

Worked example: speculative decoding with 4 draft tokens
1Draft model (1B) generates 4 candidate tokens in ~4ms:
tokens: ["the", "quick", "brown", "dog"]
2Verifier (70B) processes all 4 in one parallel forward pass (~20ms):
checks each position against its own probability distribution
3Verify results:
"the" → verifier agrees (P=0.85) → ✓ accept
"quick" → verifier agrees (P=0.72) → ✓ accept
"brown" → verifier agrees (P=0.61) → ✓ accept
"dog" → verifier disagrees (P=0.15, prefers "fox") → ✗ reject, use "fox"
4Result: Advanced 4 tokens (3 accepted + 1 corrected) in ~24ms instead of 4 × 20ms = 80ms
Speedup: 80ms / 24ms = 3.3× faster decode. Quality: mathematically identical to running the 70B model alone (rejection sampling preserves the distribution).
The acceptance rate determines everything
Draft acceptance rateExpected tokens per large-model step (with 4 draft tokens)Practical throughput multiplier
90%+~3.5 tokens per step~3× decode throughput
70%~2.4 tokens per step~2× decode throughput
50%~1.7 tokens per step~1.5× — marginal benefit
<40%Near 1 token per stepOverhead exceeds benefit

Acceptance rate is determined by how well the draft model's distribution matches the verifier's distribution on the specific types of text being generated. Predictable text (code, templates, repetitive patterns) gets high acceptance rates. Highly creative or domain-specific text gets low acceptance rates.

Draft model options
  • Separate smaller model: A 7B model drafting for a 70B verifier. Must share the same tokenizer (the algorithm that splits text into tokens — different tokenizers produce different token sequences from the same text, so draft and verifier must agree on how text is split). Works well when the smaller model is in the same model family. Memory cost: both models must fit in GPU memory simultaneously (7B + 70B in FP16 = 14 + 140 = 154 GB — already needs multiple GPUs, now with less headroom).
  • Same model, fewer layers (Medusa, hydra heads): Use the first N layers of the verifier as the draft, or add extra prediction heads to the main model. Guaranteed distribution alignment — higher acceptance rates because draft and verifier share weights.
  • MTP heads (built-in draft): If the model was trained with multi-token prediction, the auxiliary heads are already built-in draft heads with no memory overhead. See MTP section.
  • n-gram lookup: An n-gram is a sequence of n consecutive tokens (bigram = 2, trigram = 3, etc.). For text with lots of repetition (RAG documents, code with repeated patterns), simple n-gram matching against the already-generated context can propose tokens with zero model overhead and surprisingly high acceptance rates for specific domains.
Algorithm taxonomy — five named approaches

The "draft + verify" idea has crystallized into five distinct algorithms, each making a different choice about where the draft tokens come from:

AlgorithmDraft sourceExtra params / memoryAcceptance rateBest for
Draft-Target (classical)A separately trained smaller model from the same family (e.g., Llama 3 8B drafting for Llama 3 70B). Rule of thumb: draft ~10× smaller than target.Full second model in memory60–80%Strong, predictable workloads with abundant GPU memory
MedusaN extra decoder heads grafted onto the target model. Each head predicts one future token in parallel; no separate model.~1–5% extra params (the heads)50–70%When you cannot fit a second model, but can fine-tune the target
EAGLE / EAGLE-2 / EAGLE-3A small purpose-built draft module conditioned on the target's hidden states (not its tokens). Drafts up to ~8 tokens per step in a tree.Small auxiliary network (~0.3B for a 70B target)70–90% — currently the strongest in productionDefault high-performance choice in vLLM, SGLang, TRT-LLM
N-gram speculationA simple dictionary built from the prefill tokens themselves: when the model emits a sequence, look up what tokens followed it last time and propose them.Zero — no modelHighly variable: very high on code/RAG, low on novel proseCode completion, document Q&A, repetitive structured output
Lookahead DecodingGenerates n-grams on the fly during decode using a Jacobi-iteration scheme; refines guesses each step. No external draft model or training.Zero — no model, no training30–60%Drop-in speedup when you can't train or ship a second model

Two trends are visible in the table. First, EAGLE has displaced classical draft-target as the production default because it gets higher acceptance with a fraction of the memory cost — its hidden-state conditioning makes the draft genuinely close to the target's distribution. Second, training-free methods (n-gram, Lookahead) are popular operationally because they require no model surgery, no fine-tuning, and no calibration — useful when you're serving a vendor model you cannot retrain.

Two cross-cutting controls apply to all of these:

  • Draft length — proposing more tokens per step amplifies wins on high-acceptance workloads but wastes work when acceptance is low. EAGLE caps at ~8 tokens; classical draft-target typically uses 4–6.
  • Temperature interaction — higher sampling temperature spreads the verifier's distribution and lowers acceptance. Speculative decoding gives the strongest gains at temperature 0–0.7 and is often disabled at high batch size (where decode is no longer memory-bound and the spare arithmetic capacity has evaporated).
Quality guarantee — the most important property

The output distribution of speculative decoding is mathematically identical to sampling from the verifier model alone. This is a formal guarantee, not an approximation. The rejection sampling scheme used in verification preserves the exact probability distribution of the verifier. You get the quality of the large model at the throughput of something approaching the draft model's speed (when acceptance rates are high). There is no quality-throughput tradeoff — just throughput versus system complexity and memory cost.

Deep Dive — Speculative decoding in batched continuous serving

Batched spec decode exists and is the standard production form. A single shared draft model generates candidate tokens for all requests in the batch simultaneously via a batched forward pass. The target model then verifies all candidates across all requests in a single batched forward pass.

Variable acceptance rates across requests: This is the core challenge. If request A has 90% acceptance and request B has 30%, the system handles this via request-level rollback — each sequence independently tracks accepted tokens. The batch proceeds to the next step with different effective sequence lengths per request. The system still gains if the average acceptance rate across the batch is high enough. Research shows batched speculative decoding needs acceptance rates > ~60-70% to break even against standard decode. Predictable text (code, templates) benefits most; creative/domain-specific text may not break even.

Speculative decoding is rare among inference optimizations: it offers free throughput gains (when acceptance rates are high) without any quality degradation. The costs are system complexity (running two models, managing verification logic) and memory overhead (holding both models in GPU memory). For production deployments with long outputs, this tradeoff is usually very favorable.
📖 Speculative Decoding (Chen et al. 2023)Draft & Verify — Accelerated Inference (Leviathan et al.)
