Optimization › Full Optimization List

Full inference optimization techniques

Every major technique, what bottleneck it attacks, and the key mechanism

Every inference optimization attacks one or more of three fundamental bottlenecks: compute (TFLOPS — arithmetic throughput), memory bandwidth (HBM GB/s — how fast you can move data between storage and compute), or memory capacity (HBM GB — how much you can store). Understanding which bottleneck each technique addresses is more useful than memorizing the list.

TechniqueBottleneck attackedMechanismKey benefit
Continuous batchingCompute utilizationDynamically admit new requests and evict finished ones at every decode step. Never wait for a full batch to complete.GPU utilization 30% → 85%+
PagedAttentionMemory capacityManage KV cache in non-contiguous virtual memory pages like OS virtual memory. No upfront allocation.KV cache fragmentation ~60% → near-zero
Chunked prefillLatency consistency (TBT)Break long prompts into fixed-size chunks. Process one chunk per step alongside decode tokens in a mixed batch.Eliminates 500ms+ TBT spikes from long prefills
Prefix caching / Radix attentionCompute (TTFT)Cache and reuse KV vectors for shared prompt prefixes across requests. Hash-based prefix matching.Eliminates prefill cost for repeated system prompts / RAG docs
Speculative decodingLatency (decode steps)Small draft model proposes N tokens. Large verifier confirms all N in one parallel pass. Accept prefix, reject and correct at first mismatch.2-3× decode throughput with no quality loss
FlashAttention (1/2/3)Memory bandwidth + memory capacityTile attention computation to keep data in SRAM. Online softmax avoids materializing full N×N attention matrix in HBM.2-4× faster attention; O(n) memory vs O(n²); unlocks long contexts
GQA / MQAMemory capacity (KV cache)Share K/V attention heads across multiple Q heads. GQA uses groups; MQA uses one global K/V head.KV cache reduced by H/G (GQA) or H (MQA)
KV cache quantizationMemory capacityStore KV cache vectors in INT8 or FP8 instead of FP16. Dequantize on-the-fly during attention.2× more active sequences in same HBM
Weight quantization (AWQ/GPTQ)Memory capacity + bandwidthStore weights in INT4. Load 4× less from HBM per decode step. Dequantize before GEMM (weight-only). Requires Marlin kernel for speed.4× model size reduction; faster decode
FP8 inferenceMemory + bandwidth + computeStore weights and activations in FP8. Run GEMMs at FP8 precision using H100 native FP8 Tensor Cores.2× memory reduction + ~2× compute speedup on H100
Kernel fusionMemory bandwidthCombine multiple sequential GPU operations into one kernel. Intermediate results stay in SRAM/registers instead of going through HBM.Eliminates HBM round-trips for intermediates; meaningful throughput gain
Marlin kernelMemory bandwidth (quantized models)Fused INT4 dequantize + GEMM in one kernel. INT4 weights loaded to SRAM, dequantized in registers, multiplied — FP16 form never hits HBM.3-4× throughput for INT4 vs naive dequantize-then-GEMM
Tensor parallelismLatencyShard weight matrices across GPUs within one NVLink node. All GPUs cooperate on every token in parallel.~8× latency reduction at 8-way TP
Context parallelismMemory capacity (long context)Split sequence across GPUs. Ring attention passes K/V around the ring so all GPUs accumulate full attention results.Enables 128k–1M token contexts
Prefill/decode disaggregationBoth compute and bandwidthSeparate optimized fleets for each phase. KV cache transferred via GPUDirect RDMA over InfiniBand after prefill.Independent scaling; optimal hardware for each phase
MLA (Multi-head Latent Attention)Memory capacity (KV cache)Compress K/V into a single low-rank latent vector per token. Project back up at compute time. No per-head storage.~93% KV cache reduction vs MHA (DeepSeek-V2)
Multi-token prediction (MTP)Latency (decode throughput)Train auxiliary prediction heads jointly with the main model. Use as built-in draft model for speculative decoding.~1.8× decode throughput (DeepSeek-V3)
CPU offloadingMemory capacityKeep model layers or KV cache in CPU RAM. Load to GPU on demand.Serve larger models on fewer GPUs at latency cost
Disaggregated KV cache (Mooncake)Memory flexibilityTreat KV cache as a first-class storage tier separate from compute. GPU becomes stateless for scheduling.Flexible placement and better scheduling in large clusters

FlashAttention and Speculative Decoding have dedicated sections in the nav. MLA and MTP are covered in Architecture (Advanced).

Optimization › Quiz

Chapter 4 Quiz — Optimization

Test your understanding of batching, KV cache, FlashAttention, quantization, and speculative decoding

Chapter 4 Quiz — Optimization
1 Continuous batching improved GPU utilization from ~30-40% to 80-90%+. Why is the improvement so dramatic?
In naive batching, all 32 requests must wait for the slowest to finish. If 31 requests need 20 tokens and 1 needs 500, the GPU serves 1 request for ~96% of the batch time. Continuous batching frees each slot immediately on completion and backfills with waiting requests — the batch composition changes at every decode step. The bus analogy: a city bus that drops passengers at each stop and picks up new riders, vs a charter bus that drives everyone to the farthest destination first.
2 FlashAttention reduced attention memory from O(n²) to O(n). Did it also reduce attention compute?
No. Attention compute is still O(n²) — the QK^T matmul is fundamental. FlashAttention only solved the memory wall: the N×N score matrix never materializes in HBM (computed in SRAM tiles instead). This is why long contexts are feasible but still slow — FlashAttention removed the memory explosion, but the quadratic compute remains.
3 An AWQ INT4 model with the Marlin kernel can outperform the same model in FP16 at decode. How is this possible when INT4 has lower precision?
Decode is memory-bandwidth-bound. INT4 loads 4× less data from HBM per step. Marlin fuses dequantization with the GEMM in one kernel — INT4 weights go from HBM → SRAM, are dequantized in registers (never written back to HBM in FP16 form), and immediately multiplied. The FP16 form only exists transiently in registers. Net: 4× less HBM traffic at the cost of tiny register-level dequant work, which is free because arithmetic units are underutilized during decode anyway.
4 In speculative decoding, the output distribution is mathematically identical to the large model alone. How can you get speedup without quality loss?
The trick is rejection sampling. When the draft model proposes token T with probability p_draft, and the verifier assigns probability p_verify: if p_verify ≥ p_draft, always accept. If p_verify < p_draft, accept with probability p_verify/p_draft. This scheme provably preserves the verifier's exact distribution. The speedup comes from verifying N draft tokens in a single parallel forward pass (same cost as 1 decode step), not from approximating the distribution.
5 Why does KV cache quantization to FP8 have minimal quality impact, while naive INT8 activation quantization fails badly?
KV cache values are already-computed outputs with more regular distributions than raw intermediate activations. The extreme outlier problem that plagues activation quantization (a few values 100× larger than typical) is less severe in cached K/V vectors. FP8's exponent bits handle remaining dynamic range automatically, while INT8's fixed linear scale would still struggle with any outliers.
← Full Optimization ListRequest Length Strategies →
Serving at Scale › Request Length Strategies

Request length strategies

Short/short, short/long, long/short, long/long — four completely different serving problems

The fundamental asymmetry: prefill vs. decode bottlenecks

Prefill is compute-bound. Processing N input tokens in parallel is an arithmetic-heavy operation. The bottleneck is TFLOPS (Tera Floating-Point Operations Per Second — a measure of how many trillion arithmetic operations the GPU can perform per second; it's the GPU equivalent of "how fast can the CPU do math"). Faster GPUs (more TFLOPS) make prefill faster. The H100's 989 TFLOPS (with sparsity) vs the A100's 312 TFLOPS makes a big difference in prefill speed.

Decode is memory-bandwidth-bound. Each decode step loads all model weights from HBM to generate one token. The bottleneck is HBM bandwidth — how fast you can move bytes from memory to compute units. The H100 SXM (3.35 TB/s HBM bandwidth) is significantly faster at decode than the H100 PCIe (2 TB/s), for exactly this reason. More TFLOPS beyond a certain point doesn't help decode at all — the arithmetic units are already waiting for data.

This asymmetry means prefill and decode have different ideal hardware profiles. Disaggregated inference exploits this explicitly.

Request typePrimary bottleneckKey challengeStrategies
Short prompt / short outputScheduler overhead + TTFT at high request ratesThousands of requests per second. Per-request overhead becomes significant. Need maximum throughput with low TTFT.Large dynamic batches. Continuous batching as foundation. Prefix caching for common system prompts. Optimize scheduler efficiency.
Short prompt / long outputKV cache memory growth during decodePrefill is cheap (short prompt). But decode runs for hundreds or thousands of steps. KV cache grows linearly per step. One long-running request can monopolize HBM for minutes.Memory-aware admission control — cap active sequences by KV cache budget. Preemption and CPU-RAM swap for overflow. KV cache quantization (INT8) to double capacity. GQA/MQA to reduce per-token KV size.
Long prompt / short outputPrefill compute time; TTFT measured in secondsProcessing a 50,000-token document takes multiple seconds of prefill. During this time, all ongoing decode sequences stall. TTFT is terrible and unpredictable.Chunked prefill to prevent decode stalls. Prefix caching if prompts share a known structure. Dedicated prefill workers (disaggregation). Consider whether the full context is actually needed.
Long prompt / long outputBoth phases expensive; memory-intensive throughoutMost resource-intensive combination. Long prefill blocks decode. Long decode grows KV cache massively. Cannot be batched efficiently with short requests without severe interference.Prefill/decode disaggregation — separate fleets. KV cache quantization. Sequence/context parallelism. Priority isolation from short interactive requests.
Prefill/decode disaggregation — the advanced solution for mixed workloads

The fundamental insight: prefill and decode have such different hardware requirements that running them on the same machines means you're always making hardware compromises for both. Disaggregation resolves this by splitting the serving fleet into two specialized pools.

Prefill workers receive new requests, run the full prefill (compute-bound, want high TFLOPS), and generate the KV cache for the prompt. They're done after prefill — they never run decode.

Decode workers receive KV caches from prefill workers (transferred via InfiniBand — a high-speed network fabric connecting GPU servers in a cluster, typically at 400 Gbps / ~50 GB/s — using RDMA, Remote Direct Memory Access, which lets GPUs read/write each other's memory directly without CPU involvement; see NVLink section for details), then run decode until the response is complete (memory-bandwidth-bound, want high HBM bandwidth). They never run prefill.

After prefill finishes on a prefill worker, the KV cache (which can be gigabytes for long prompts) is transferred over InfiniBand RDMA directly to the decode worker's GPU HBM, bypassing the CPU. The decode worker then begins generating tokens immediately. Each fleet scales independently — if decode is the bottleneck, add more decode workers without touching the prefill fleet. If a surge of long prompts arrives, scale up prefill workers.

Deep Dive — KV transfer is the emerging bottleneck in disaggregation

Concrete numbers for LLaMA-3 70B (GQA): KV cache = 320 KB/token. A 100k-token prompt = ~31.25 GB. At InfiniBand 400 Gbps (~50 GB/s): transfer takes ~625 ms. Even at 800 Gbps: ~312 ms. Both blow most TTFT budgets before a single decode token is generated. For long contexts (32k-128k tokens), KV transfer latency now dominates over decode compute time itself. This is why Mooncake uses RDMA with zero-copy, and Dynamo includes a KV transfer scheduler that pipelines transfers with decode. Chunked prefill can partially mitigate this by shipping completed KV chunks incrementally while prefill continues — an active research area.

Disaggregation is Anthropic's production serving architecture direction. The papers that formalize this: DistServe (OSDI 2024) and Splitwise (ISCA 2024). NVIDIA Dynamo implements this in their open-source inference framework. Being able to describe disaggregation clearly — including the networking requirements and the KV transfer mechanism — is high signal in any Anthropic infrastructure conversation.
📖 DistServe (OSDI 2024) — prefill/decode disaggregationSplitwise (ISCA 2024) — heterogeneous disaggregation
← Full Optimization ListScheduling & Admission →
Serving at Scale › Scheduling & Admission

Scheduling, admission control & prioritization new

SLO-aware scheduling, early rejection, priority tiers, and goodput-optimized serving

SLO-aware admission control — the Mooncake pattern

Before accepting a request, estimate whether you can complete it within the SLO. If not, reject immediately with a 503 rather than accepting, partially processing, and then failing — wasting GPU compute on tokens that never reach the user.

total_time = queue_wait + prefill_time + decode_time
prefill_time = prompt_tokens / prefill_throughput (e.g., 10,000 / 5,000 = 2s)
decode_time = predicted_output_tokens / decode_throughput (e.g., 500 / 50 = 10s)
queue_wait = f(queue_depth, current_throughput)

If total_time > SLO_deadline: reject immediately with 503 + Retry-After.

The hard part: estimating predicted_output_tokens before generation. See the Output Length Prediction section.

AWS analogy: This is like an NLB health check combined with a connection limit. Rather than accepting connections you can't serve, you reject at the load balancer. The difference: "can I serve this?" depends on the specific request and current system state.

Goodput-optimized scheduling

Goodput = requests meeting SLO / total requests attempted. This is different from throughput (tokens/sec) — a system can have high throughput but low goodput if many requests miss their SLO. A system doing 100 req/s with 95% SLO compliance has goodput of 95 req/s.

Key techniques from goodput research:

  • SLO-aware admission: Reject requests likely to miss SLO rather than degrading everyone's latency.
  • Preemption ordering: When memory pressure forces eviction, evict requests already likely to miss SLO, or those with least progress (minimizing wasted compute), or lowest priority tier.
  • Waiting policy: Sometimes delaying a new request (increasing its TTFT) is better than starting it immediately and causing TBT SLO violations for in-flight requests.
Priority tiers in production

Weighted Fair Queuing (WFQ): Assign weights to priority classes — e.g., Enterprise=10, Paid=5, Free=1. Each class gets bandwidth proportional to its weight. Under overload, Free gets 1/16 of capacity, not zero. Prevents complete starvation while maintaining priority ordering.

TierExampleWeightBehavior under overload
Tier 0 (critical)Internal health checks, enterprise8Nearly always admitted
Tier 1 (high)Paid-standard4Reduced but guaranteed bandwidth
Tier 2 (normal)Free-authenticated2Throttled significantly
Tier 3 (best-effort)Free-anonymous, batch jobs1First to be rejected

This maps directly to AWS capacity pools: Reserved (Tier 0-1), On-Demand (Tier 2), Spot (Tier 3).

Preemption policies — who gets evicted first
  • FCFS (default in vLLM): Last-arrived evicted first. Simple but can evict nearly-complete requests.
  • Least-progress-first: Evict requests that generated fewest tokens. Minimizes wasted compute.
  • Lowest-priority-first: Evict from lowest tier. Best for multi-tenant systems.
  • Longest-remaining-first: If output length is predicted, evict requests with most remaining work. Frees the most memory per eviction.
Engine-specific scheduler differences (2025-2026)
EngineScheduler typeKey feature
vLLM V1Priority-based FCFS with token budgetsChunked prefill default on; recompute-only preemption (no swap)
SGLangPredictive reservation (new_token_ratio)Estimates future decode memory needs before admitting — prevents "admit then preempt" cycle
TRT-LLMCapacity-based with static schedulingCompiled engines know exact memory requirements — more precise admission
DynamoTwo-level (global planner + local engine)KV-cache-aware routing; assigns to prefill or decode workers
Queue disciplines underneath the priority tiers

WFQ (above) decides which class a request belongs to. Within a class, you still need a queue discipline. Two primitives cover most production deployments:

  • FIFO — first in, first out per class. Default in vLLM. Simple, fair, head-of-line blocking is the failure mode: a 50K-token prompt at the head of the queue stalls everything behind it for seconds.
  • Priority queue — sorted by a per-request key (predicted output length for SJF, deadline for EDF, business priority for WFQ tie-breaks). Adds aging to prevent starvation.

Production systems usually run a stack: WFQ across classes → priority queue within each class → token-budget admission gate before entering the engine batch. The earlier sections of this chapter explain each layer.

Routing — KV-aware and LoRA-aware

Beyond scheduling within a single replica, the cluster-level routing decision can be cache-aware in two distinct ways. Both are forms of session affinity dressed up as load balancing:

Routing flavorWhat's "sticky"Hash keyFailure mode if naive
KV-cache-aware routingWhich replica has the prefix's KV cache warm in HBMHash of the request prefix tokens (or radix-tree match score)Hot prefixes saturate one replica while others sit idle
LoRA-aware routingWhich replica has the requested adapter weights loadedAdapter ID (e.g., tenant-42-customer-support-v3)Many tenants → adapter thrash: every request loads weights from CPU/SSD, killing throughput

Both routings need the same overflow/spill mechanism — pin to the cache-warm replica until utilization crosses a threshold, then fan out. Production routers (SGLang's sgl-router, NVIDIA Dynamo, vLLM's production stack) implement both as a single decision: pick the replica that maximizes the joint cache-hit score across prefix and adapter. Multi-tenant LoRA serving is meaningless without LoRA-aware routing — tenants would see hour-long P99s as their adapter gets swapped in and out.

Cold starts — why admission control has to know about replica state

Admission control isn't only about "is the engine full?" — it's also "is the replica even ready?" When autoscaling spins up a new replica, the time from "scheduler decided to add capacity" to "replica accepts requests" breaks down into four stages:

StageTypical durationMitigation
1. GPU procurement (cloud-provider provisioning, scheduler placement)10s of seconds to several minutesReserved capacity, warm pools, multi-cloud bin packing
2. Container image pull (CUDA/cuDNN/engine binaries)10s of seconds (image can be 10+ GB)Smaller images; in-region registry mirrors; pre-baked AMIs
3. Model weight load (HBM hydration from object store)10s of seconds for 70B+; minutes for 405BIn-datacenter weight cache; tensor streaming via mmap'd safetensors; high-bandwidth object store (S3 Express, Mountpoint)
4. Engine startup / compile (CUDA graph capture, kernel autotune, first prefill)Seconds to minutes (TRT-LLM compile is slowest)Cached compiled engines; PyTorch torch.compile artifact reuse; warmup batch on a representative prompt

Aggregate cold start is therefore commonly 1–5 minutes. Two consequences for admission control: (1) the autoscaler must trigger before queue depth crosses SLO so a new replica is ready by the time it's needed; (2) during scale-up, new replicas should be drained from the routing pool until they pass a synthetic warmup probe — otherwise their first user-facing request pays compile latency and trips the SLO. Production-grade serving stacks treat replica state as a routing dimension on par with cache locality.

← Request Length StrategiesOutput Length Prediction →
Serving at Scale › Output Length Prediction

Output length prediction new

Why predicting output length matters, how to do it, and what happens when you're wrong

Why it matters

You don't know how many tokens the model will generate until it emits EOS. This uncertainty breaks scheduling:

  • Bin-packing: A 2000-token request blocks a batch slot 20× longer than a 100-token request. Without prediction, short requests get stuck behind long ones.
  • SLO feasibility: Can't reject infeasible requests proactively if you don't know decode time.
  • Preemption: If a request is 95% done (predicted 100 tokens, generated 95), don't evict it.
  • KV cache budgeting: Each request's KV cache grows linearly. Predicting output length lets you pre-allocate correctly.
Approaches and their accuracy
ApproachAccuracyLatency costWhen to use
Use max_tokens from requestUpper bound only (conservative)NoneDefault fallback
Classifier (short/medium/long buckets)70-80% for 3-bucket~1msCoarse scheduling decisions
Regression model (predict exact count)MAE of 30-100 tokens~1msSLO feasibility checks
LLM self-prediction ("how long will your answer be?")60-70% within ±30%Extra prefill passHigh-value requests
Distribution-based (QLM/CLT)95% coverage interval~1msBatch-level capacity planning
QLM — using distributions, not point estimates

QLM (from the Andes paper, UC Berkeley/Microsoft 2024) uses the Central Limit Theorem: even if individual predictions are noisy, across a batch of N requests, the total tokens predicted converges to the true total with variance shrinking as 1/√N. Per-request prediction can be mediocre, but batch-level capacity planning can be accurate.

Learning-to-Rank for SJF-like scheduling

Instead of predicting absolute lengths, predict relative lengths — "request A is shorter than request B." This is easier to learn and sufficient for Shortest-Job-First scheduling. Add aging to prevent starvation: effective_priority = shortness_score + aging_factor × wait_time. This is directly analogous to Linux's CFS scheduler or AWS weighted round-robin with priority aging.

What happens when predictions are wrong
  • Over-prediction (predicted long, actually short): Wasted KV cache allocation, but request finishes early. Minor inefficiency.
  • Under-prediction (predicted short, actually long): Dangerous. Triggers preemption, violates SLO, blocks "express lane." Mitigate by using the upper bound of the confidence interval for admission control.
Key insight: prediction accuracy matters less than you'd think. Even a noisy predictor that correctly identifies the top-25% shortest and bottom-25% longest requests enables 2-3× reduction in average latency for short requests.

Serving at Scale › Request vs Sequence vs Batch

Request, sequence, and batch

Three terms used inconsistently across papers and codebases — here is what each actually means

These three terms describe the same unit of work at three different layers of the serving stack. Getting them straight is essential for understanding scheduler behavior, metrics, and debugging.

TermPerspectiveWhat it isChanges over time?
RequestExternal callerOne API call. Contains an input prompt, produces an output completion. Has a lifecycle: arrives → queues → processes → responds.No — it's a snapshot of what arrived.
SequenceServing system internalsA request that has been admitted into the engine and has an active KV cache on the GPU. A growing list of tokens: prompt tokens (after prefill) + output tokens (as generated).Yes — grows with each decode step. Can be preempted, swapped, resumed.
BatchGPU executionThe set of sequences processed together in one forward pass. The GPU's unit of work — it loads weights once and processes all batch members simultaneously.Yes — composition changes every decode step with continuous batching.

Concrete example from your Phase 3 experiments

With prefetch_count=5 and 1.2 req/sec load over 90 seconds:

LayerCountWhereCost
Requests in-flight~72System-wide (load generator waiting for responses)None — just a counter
Requests in RabbitMQ queue~60+RabbitMQ memoryCheap — bytes per message
Prefetch buffer (delivered to worker)≤5Worker process memoryCheap — waiting to be submitted
Active sequences in vLLM~5-7GPU HBM (KV cache allocated)Expensive — 401 KB per token per sequence
Batch size per forward pass~5-7GPU computeAll weight loading amortized across this many tokens

Your prefetch_count controlled the request → sequence boundary. It throttled how many requests could become active sequences (with GPU KV cache cost) at any time. The 72 "concurrent" requests were mostly queued in the cheap place (RabbitMQ) rather than the expensive place (GPU memory).

Why batch size drives throughput

Your Stage 2 Test 5 showed this directly:

Concurrency (≈ batch size)ThroughputMultiplier vs batch=1What's happening
144 tok/s1×Load all weights, generate 1 token. GPU starved.
8254 tok/s5.8×Same weight load, 8 tokens out. Much better amortization.
32836 tok/s19×Approaching GPU memory bandwidth saturation.
641,104 tok/s25×Peak — KV cache is the limiting factor now.

The 25× gain from batch=1 to batch=64 is the same HBM weight load producing 64 tokens instead of 1. This is why continuous batching matters so much — it keeps the batch full.

Prefill vs decode: two kinds of "batch"

The word "batch" means different things in prefill vs decode, which adds to the confusion:

  • Prefill batch: A single sequence with a 2,000-token prompt runs a "batch" of 2,000 tokens in one forward pass. All prompt tokens processed in parallel. The batch dimension here is tokens within one sequence.
  • Decode batch: 64 sequences each contribute 1 new token = a "batch" of 64 tokens. The batch dimension here is sequences, with 1 token each.

When someone says "batch size 64 with sequence length 2,048" — the 64 is how many sequences, the 2,048 is the per-sequence token count. During decode, each sequence contributes exactly 1 token, so the batch has 64 tokens total across 64 sequences.

← Request vs Sequence vs BatchAgentic Inference →
Serving at Scale › Multi-turn Inference

Multi-turn inference new

How conversations work mechanically, why they're expensive, and how to design for them at scale

What makes multi-turn different from single-turn

In single-turn inference, every request is fully independent. A prompt arrives, gets prefilled, decode runs, the response is returned. Nothing persists. The server can forget everything the moment the response is sent.

In multi-turn (a conversation), each new user message depends on the full history of everything that came before — every prior user message and every prior assistant response. The model needs that entire history to generate a coherent next response. The naive approach: concatenate the full conversation history into the prompt on every turn.

Turn 1: 500 tokens. Turn 2: 500 + 400 (turn 1 response) + 200 (turn 2 message) = 1,100 tokens. Turn 5: ~3,500 tokens. Turn 10: potentially 8,000–15,000 tokens. The prompt grows with every turn. Multi-turn inference is fundamentally a long-context problem that gets progressively worse throughout the session.

Two fundamentally different architectures
Two architectures compared
Architecture 1 — StatelessArchitecture 2 — KV Persistence
KV cache lifecycleEvicted when request finishes. Full re-prefill on next turn.Kept alive between turns (GPU HBM, CPU RAM, or KV store).
Prefill cost per turnFull context every turn. Turn 10 re-prefills all 10 turns of history.Only new tokens since last turn. Turn 10 prefills just the new message.
RoutingAny server can handle any turn. No affinity needed.Must route to the server holding the KV cache (session affinity).
Failure handlingTrivial — retry on any server, re-prefill from text.KV cache lost on server failure. Must re-prefill as fallback.
ScalingHorizontally trivial. Stateless servers.Complex — must balance KV locality vs load distribution.
Memory pressureNone between turns. KV freed immediately.Persistent KV competes with new requests for HBM.
Production useMost systems today, with prefix caching as optimization.Where investment is going — Mooncake, Anthropic long-context.

Architecture 1 — Stateless (most systems today): The KV cache is evicted the moment each request finishes. On the next turn, the client sends the full conversation history as a new prompt. The server reprefills everything from scratch — including all tokens from previous turns it already processed. Wasteful, but operationally simple. Every server is stateless. Any server in the fleet can handle any turn of any conversation. Load balancing is trivial. No session affinity required.

Architecture 2 — KV persistence (where the industry is heading): After each turn finishes, the KV cache is kept alive — in GPU HBM if capacity allows, in CPU RAM as a secondary tier, or in dedicated KV storage (Mooncake architecture). The next turn only prefills new tokens and appends to the existing KV cache. A 10-turn conversation costs 10 × (average new tokens per turn) in prefill rather than 10 × (growing sum).

Many production systems still use Architecture 1 as the default, because it's operationally simple. However, prefix caching (Anthropic's prompt caching, vLLM's automatic prefix caching, SGLang's RadixAttention) is now widespread, creating a practical middle ground — the shared prefix portion of KV cache is reused across turns without full session persistence. Architecture 2 (full KV persistence) is where investment is going — Anthropic's extended thinking, long-context models, and agentic use cases all create strong economic pressure to solve KV persistence properly.
Why Architecture 2 is hard — the four challenges

1. Routing affinity. If the KV cache for a conversation lives on Server A, all subsequent turns must go to Server A. Your load balancer needs session affinity — consistent hashing on conversation ID. Straightforward to implement, but creates the same hot-spot problem as prefix caching: popular conversations or long-running sessions can saturate specific workers while others sit idle.

2. KV cache memory pressure. In-progress conversation KV caches are live — you can't evict them without losing the ability to continue cheaply. As conversations grow longer and more conversations are active simultaneously, KV cache competes with new request processing for HBM. You need an eviction policy: LRU across conversations, priority by recency, or commercial charging models (paying for "warm context"). If you evict a conversation's KV cache, the next turn falls back to full reprefill — functionally transparent to the user, just slower and more expensive.

3. Disaggregated architecture tension. In a disaggregated setup (prefill workers + decode workers), where does the KV cache live between turns? If it lives on the decode worker that handled the last turn, you need affinity to that specific decode worker — but decode workers are supposed to be stateless and interchangeable for scheduling flexibility. If you persist KV in a separate storage tier (Mooncake), you add latency loading it back on each turn. There is no clean solution today.

4. Dual representation management. Conversation text and conversation KV cache are different things with different lifecycles. The text must be persisted durably (it's the source of truth). The KV cache is a derived, expensive-to-compute artifact that can be regenerated from the text if needed. You're managing two representations with very different storage economics: text is kilobytes, KV cache is gigabytes.

System design — layer by layer
LayerResponsibilityKey decision
ClientSends conversation ID + full text history as fallbackAlways send full text — server decides whether to use cached KV or re-prefill
RoutingConsistent hash on conversation ID → same worker groupPrefer worker with warmest cache; overflow to others above utilization threshold
ServingMaintains KV cache pool with LRU eviction per conversationTrack cache hit rate by depth bucket (turns 1-3, 4-10, 10+) to tune eviction
StorageMulti-tier KV cache: GPU HBM (hot) → CPU RAM (warm) → text-only (cold)Each tier has different restoration cost. Cold = full re-prefill from text.

Client layer: Every message includes a conversation ID. The client always sends the full conversation history text (not just the new message) as the fallback for when KV cache has been evicted. The server decides whether to use cached KV or reprefill from text on each turn.

Routing layer: Consistent hashing on conversation ID routes to the same worker group. Within the group, prefer the specific worker with the warmest cache. Add overflow routing when the primary worker is above utilization threshold — the same hybrid locality/load-balance tradeoff as prompt caching, applied at conversation granularity.

Serving layer: Each worker maintains a KV cache pool with LRU eviction across active conversations. Track cache hit rate per conversation depth bucket (turns 1–3, 4–10, 10+) to tune eviction aggressiveness. When evicted, text history still available for fallback reprefill — transparent to the user.

Storage layer: For valuable long-running sessions: two-tier KV cache. Hot conversations stay in GPU HBM. Warm-but-idle conversations demote to CPU RAM. Cold conversations evict to text-only. Each tier has different restoration cost on cache miss.

Capacity planning — where multi-turn differs sharply from single-turn

Single-turn KV cache usage is predictable: it scales with request concurrency and output length. Multi-turn KV cache usage is not — it compounds with conversation depth over the session lifetime. A 20-turn conversation with 500 tokens per turn holds 10,000 tokens of KV cache indefinitely while active.

KV cache size per token is model-dependent — it scales with num_KV_heads × d_head × num_layers × bytes_per_element. For a dense GPT-3 style model in FP16 this is ~4.7 MB per token. For LLaMA-3 70B with GQA (8 KV heads), it's roughly 0.3 MB per token — but 10,000 tokens still means 3 GB of HBM per active long conversation, on top of 140 GB of model weights. Sizing your KV cache pool requires traffic data on conversation depth distribution — you cannot derive it from first principles alone.

Connection to prompt caching

Anthropic's prompt caching feature is a specific slice of this — it caches KV vectors for stable, static prefixes (system prompts, long context documents) across requests. Multi-turn conversation caching is the natural dynamic extension: instead of caching a static prefix, you cache the growing conversation prefix across turns. The engineering challenges are identical in kind, but harder in practice because the cache key is dynamic (growing with each turn) rather than static.

Context engineering — write prompts to maximize cache hits

Prefix caches only fire on shared prefix bytes. The composition of your prompt directly controls how much of each turn hits cache and how much has to be re-prefilled. The single biggest practical lever:

Keep stable content early. Keep novel content late. The earlier the divergence point in your prompt, the smaller the cached prefix and the more compute you waste re-prefilling material that hasn't actually changed.

Concrete consequences for prompt structure:

  • Order: instructions → tools → retrieved docs → conversation history → user's new message. System prompt and tool definitions almost never change across turns; put them first. Retrieved RAG chunks change per request but are stable within a session; put them next. The only truly novel content per turn is the new user message — push it to the end.
  • Avoid early dynamic content. Injecting "current time: 2026-04-26 14:32:11" or a per-request UUID into the system prompt invalidates every turn's cache hit because the divergence point moves to byte 0. If you need timestamps or request IDs, put them in the last user message, not the system block.
  • Stable formatting. Don't rewrite the same instruction with slightly different whitespace or wording across turns — the hash diverges and you re-prefill from that point. Pick a canonical form and stick to it.
  • Tool definitions are part of the prefix. Adding or reordering a tool mid-conversation invalidates everything from the tool block onward. Define the full tool set up front; add tools by appending, not by editing earlier entries.

This is the inference-side mirror of normal-form database design: factor out the stable prefix, isolate the dynamic suffix. It's free throughput — same model, same engine, just a prompt layout that lets the cache do its job.

Deep Dive — KV cache validity across model versions and sampling changes

Model version update: KV cache vectors are outputs of specific W_K, W_V weights. If the model is updated — even a minor checkpoint update, adapter swap, or quantization change — all cached KV tensors are numerically incorrect for the new model's attention and must be discarded. There is no mechanism to "migrate" KV cache across model versions because the latent space geometry changes. Production systems handle this via versioned cache namespacing: each model version gets an isolated cache pool, and on rollout the old version's cache drains naturally via LRU. Common pattern: blue-green deployment where new replicas spin up, traffic shifts gradually, and old replicas serve existing warm sessions until they complete.

Sampling parameter changes: Temperature, top-p, top-k are controls that govern randomness when picking the next token from the model's probability distribution (temperature=0 always picks the most likely token; higher temperature spreads probability more evenly, increasing creativity/randomness; top-p and top-k limit which tokens are even considered). These are applied after the forward pass — they modify the logit distribution at sampling time but do not affect KV vectors. Changing temperature between turns does not invalidate the KV cache. However, there's a subtle correctness case: if earlier turns generated different tokens due to different sampling settings, the KV cache for those generated tokens would be wrong. vLLM handles this by including generated token IDs in the cache key hash chain — cache hits require matching both prompt and generated tokens.

📖 Mooncake — disaggregated KV cache as a first-class storage tierAnthropic prompt caching docs

Serving at Scale › Agentic Inference

Agentic inference patterns new

How tool calling, multi-step reasoning, and extended thinking change the serving problem

What makes agentic workloads fundamentally different

Standard chat: request arrives, prefill, decode, done. Agentic workloads (Claude Code, computer use, multi-step reasoning) break every assumption:

DimensionStandard chatAgentic
Session duration5-30 secondsMinutes to hours
Turns per session120-50 tool calls
Context growthFixedGrows monotonically (each tool result appends)
Output varianceModerateExtreme (5 tokens to 10K+ within same session)
GPU idle timeNone30-62% (during tool execution)
KV cache lifetimeSecondsMinutes to hours

AWS analogy: Standard chat is a stateless web server behind an ALB. Agentic inference is a WebSocket-based real-time application with long-lived connections, variable message sizes, and idle periods — much harder to load balance and scale.

How tool calls actually work — it's just tokens

A tool call is not a special system event. The model generates a tool invocation as regular output tokens in a structured format (JSON, XML, or a special markup). The serving system detects these tokens and acts on them. The entire mechanism is built on top of the same autoregressive decode — no special GPU operations, no side channels.

The stop sequence mechanism

The model is trained to emit a stop sequence when it wants to invoke a tool — a specific token pattern (like </tool_call> or "}\n" at the end of a JSON block). The serving system's sampler checks every generated token against a list of configured stop sequences. When a match is found, decode halts immediately.

This creates a subtle infrastructure problem: partial stop matching. If the stop sequence is </tool> and the model has generated </too, the sampler can't yet tell if this is the beginning of the stop sequence or just the word "too" in normal text. It must buffer these ambiguous tokens rather than streaming them to the client. If the next token is l>, it was a stop — discard the buffered tokens and halt. If the next token is th (making "tooth"), flush the buffered tokens to the client and continue.

This partial-stop buffering adds a small latency hiccup at every potential stop sequence prefix — typically negligible, but it means the serving system is doing string matching on every single generated token, and the streaming output can stutter briefly at partial matches.

The suspend-and-resume scheduling problem

When a tool call is detected, the serving system must:

  • Pause decode for this sequence — it's waiting for external tool results
  • Decide what to do with the KV cache — hold in GPU (wastes memory), swap to CPU (resume latency), or discard (re-prefill cost)
  • Signal the client — send the tool call JSON, wait for the client to execute the tool and return results
  • Resume — when tool results arrive (could be 100ms to 60+ seconds later), append them to the context, prefill the new tokens, and continue decode

The tricky part: the scheduler has a sequence that is "alive" (has state, has a KV cache, is part of a session) but "not active" (not contributing tokens to the batch, not doing useful GPU work). Every other serving optimization assumes sequences are either active or done — tool calls create a third state: suspended.

Compounding prefill cost across tool calls

Each tool call round appends context: the model's tool invocation + the tool result. The context grows monotonically. If using stateless serving (Architecture 1 from the Multi-turn section — send full context each turn), the prefill cost compounds:

TurnWhat's in the promptTotal tokensPrefill time (5K tok/s)KV cache (320 KB/tok, 70B GQA)
1 (initial)System prompt + user request2,0000.4s0.6 GB
2 (after read_file)Turn 1 + tool call + file contents (3K tokens)5,2001.0s1.6 GB
3 (after search)Turn 2 + tool call + search results (2K tokens)7,5001.5s2.3 GB
5 (after 4 tool calls)Accumulated context15,0003.0s4.7 GB
10 (after 9 tool calls)Accumulated context35,0007.0s10.9 GB
20 (deep agent session)Accumulated context80,00016.0s25.0 GB
By turn 20, each tool call round requires 16 seconds just for prefill — before generating a single output token. And 25 GB of KV cache for one session. This is why prefix caching is essential for agentic workloads: with prompt caching, only the new tokens since the last turn need actual prefill compute. The shared prefix (turns 1-19) hits the cache and costs near-zero.
Visual: agentic session timeline (Claude Code example)
Time →

pf

Notice: the KV cache grows monotonically and is held during tool execution. During the 2s file read, the GPU is doing no useful work but the KV cache memory is occupied. This is the core tension of agentic serving.

The KV cache as a long-lived resource

In standard serving, KV cache is allocated at prefill, used for seconds, then freed. In agentic serving, it becomes a long-lived resource that must persist across tool execution gaps. A 100K-token conversation on a 70B model uses ~32 GB of KV cache (with GQA) — a single session consuming a large fraction of available GPU memory.

Options for idle KV cache:

  • Hold in GPU memory: Simplest, wastes the most expensive resource during tool execution.
  • Swap to CPU memory: Free GPU for other requests. Swap-in adds ~100-500ms latency on resume.
  • Disaggregated KV store (Mooncake): KV cache in a separate storage tier. Any GPU can resume the session. Maximum flexibility, ~50-200ms retrieval latency.
  • Discard and re-prefill with prefix caching: Drop session KV cache. Rely on prefix caching for shared portions (system prompt). Only re-prefill session-specific tokens. Works when session context is small relative to shared prefix.
Extended thinking from a serving perspective

Thinking tokens are real tokens — each goes through the same decode forward pass, consumes the same compute, and extends the KV cache. A response with 10K thinking + 2K visible tokens has a KV cache footprint of 12K, not 2K.

This changes TTFT semantics: there are now two metrics — time to first thinking token (fast, like standard TTFT) and time to first visible token (can be 30+ seconds). Traditional latency-based autoscaling would incorrectly interpret 45 seconds of thinking as degradation.

Inference-time compute scaling

The broader principle: spending more compute at inference time to improve quality. Multiple approaches exist:

  • Best-of-N: Generate N candidates, score with a reward model, return the best. Cost: linear in N. Quality: logarithmic (diminishing returns). KV cache: all N share the same prefill.
  • Tree search with verification: Explore reasoning paths, score intermediate steps with a process reward model, prune bad branches. How o1/o3, DeepSeek-R1, Gemini 2.5 work. KV cache branching uses copy-on-write (PagedAttention's reference counting).

Research shows a 7B model with 100× inference compute can outperform a 70B model at 1× on reasoning tasks. The serving system must support 100× variance in per-request compute cost.

The emerging two-tier pattern

The industry is converging on:

  • API-served agents: Stateless serving with aggressive prefix caching. Send full context each turn. Let the serving system handle KV reuse transparently. Simpler, more robust, works well with existing infrastructure. This is how Claude Code and ChatGPT with tools work today.
  • Latency-critical / long-context agents: Session-aware serving with KV persistence or disaggregated KV stores. More complex but necessary when re-prefill cost becomes prohibitive (>100K token contexts).
An agent is a system, not a model — independent component scaling

The earlier sections of this chapter treat "the LLM" as the unit of serving. An agent loop touches several other backing services on every turn, and each is its own scaling unit with its own load profile:

ComponentLoad profileScales withScaling pattern
LLM servingGPU-bound; KV cache memory dominates at scaleTokens/sec, concurrent sessionsGPU replicas, KV-aware routing
Retriever / vector DBCPU + memory; embedding lookup latency-sensitiveQueries/sec; index sizeSharded by index; cache popular queries
RerankerSmall GPU; small batches; predictable latencyTop-K candidates per requestIndependent GPU pool — usually shared across many agent flows
Code execution sandboxCPU + container churn; startup cost dominatesTool-call ratePre-warmed sandbox pool; per-tenant isolation
External tool servers (search, file I/O, MCP)I/O-bound; rate-limited by upstreamTool-call rate per toolIndependent worker pools; circuit breakers around flaky tools

Each of these scales on its own metric and fails for its own reasons. Treat them as separate fleets with independent autoscaling — sizing them all proportional to "agent sessions" wastes money on whichever component happens to be under-utilized for your workload.

Same-cluster placement — the ~40ms rule

Inter-component latency adds up fast in agent loops. A typical turn does retrieval → rerank → LLM prefill → tool call → LLM decode, with several hops between services. Cross-cluster network round-trips cost ~30–50ms each (varies with cloud region, VPC peering, mesh overlay). At ten hops per turn that's up to half a second of pure network latency in a turn that the user expects to feel instant.

Co-locate the agent's components in the same cluster (ideally the same availability zone) whenever possible. The ~40ms cross-cluster cost compounds across hops and is the single most common source of "but each component is fast?" performance bugs in agent systems.

Practical placement rules: keep retriever, reranker, sandbox pool, and LLM in the same VPC and same AZ. Tool servers that hit external APIs (web search, third-party SaaS) are inherently cross-region — budget for that explicitly and cache aggressively at the agent boundary. If you must span regions, do it once per session, not once per turn.

Serving at Scale › Tool Calling & Reasoning

Tool calling and reasoning models in the engine new

What the inference engine actually does when an agentic loop or a thinking model runs through it.

The agentic patterns chapter covered the architecture; this section is one level deeper. When a request comes in with tools=[…], what code paths fire inside the engine? When the model emits a reasoning trace, where does it go? The serving layer treats tool calls and reasoning tokens as first-class concepts, with dedicated parsers, finish reasons, and accounting paths.

The mechanics of a tool call

The model has been fine-tuned to emit a model-family-specific token sequence when it wants to invoke a function. A few examples:

  • Hermes/Qwen: <tool_call>{"name": "...", "arguments": {...}}</tool_call>
  • Llama 3.1: a JSON envelope wrapped in special tokens
  • Mistral: [TOOL_CALLS] followed by JSON
  • DeepSeek-V3: <｜tool▁calls▁begin｜>…<｜tool▁calls▁end｜>
  • Llama 4: pythonic syntax, e.g. [get_weather(city="NYC")]

The engine watches the decode stream for these markers as tokens are produced, parses out the JSON or Python args, sets finish_reason="tool_calls" (OpenAI-shape) or stop_reason="tool_use" (Anthropic-shape), and returns control to the orchestrator. The orchestrator runs the tool, appends the result to the conversation, and submits a new request. Anthropic documents this loop explicitly: assistant returns tool_use blocks, the client executes, the next user turn carries a matching tool_result.

Tool-call parsers

Because every model family encodes tool calls differently, vLLM exposes a --tool-call-parser flag with a per-family registry: hermes, mistral, llama3_json, llama4_pythonic, pythonic, granite/granite4, internlm, jamba, deepseek_v3/deepseek_v31, kimi_k2, glm45, plus an auto mode. Each parser implements both extract_tool_calls() for non-streaming responses and extract_tool_calls_streaming() so partial JSON can be surfaced as it decodes. SGLang has the same pattern (Llama, Qwen, Mistral, DeepSeek, Pythonic detectors).

Pick the wrong parser and tool calls show up as plain text content to the client. The model is producing valid tool-call tokens, but without the family-matched parser the engine has no way to know what to do with them.
Multi-turn KV reuse — why prefix caching is load-bearing

An agent loop is [system → user → assistant(tool_call) → tool_result → assistant(tool_call) → tool_result → … → final answer]. The conversation prefix grows monotonically: every previous turn is part of every subsequent turn's prompt. Without prefix caching, every turn re-prefills the entire history, which is O(N²) total work over N turns. With prefix caching, it is O(N).

This is why session affinity at the gateway matters more for agent traffic than for any other workload. Routing turn 8 of a 10-turn agent loop to a different replica than turns 1–7 is the difference between a 50ms TTFT and a 2-second TTFT — every turn pays a full re-prefill of the conversation history, on hardware that is otherwise idle. Cache-aware routing (consistent hashing on prompt prefix) is the operational answer.

Reasoning models — the parser and the budget

Reasoning models (DeepSeek-R1, QwQ-32B, Qwen3, Granite 3.2, Claude extended-thinking, the o1/o3 family) emit a reasoning segment before the final answer, usually delimited — for R1 it's <think>…</think>. vLLM exposes --reasoning-parser with values like deepseek_r1, qwen3, granite, glm45, ernie45, hunyuan_a13b, minimax_m2_append_think. The parser splits the stream into reasoning_content vs content and streams them as delta.reasoning vs delta.content. A thinking_token_budget can force-close reasoning by injecting the end-of-thinking token.

Operationally this matters in three ways:

  • Reasoning tokens are billed and consume KV. Even when the product UI hides them from the user, they are real decode steps with real cost. A request that returns 200 visible tokens after 8,000 reasoning tokens costs you 8,200 tokens of compute.
  • Reasoning is firmly decode-bound. R1 routinely emits 2,000–10,000 thinking tokens per request. Throughput on these workloads is gated by memory bandwidth and KV size, not FLOPs. MLA and MTP are direct architectural responses to this.
  • Reasoning leakage is a real bug. A misconfigured parser lets <think> content through to content, breaking downstream JSON parsers and leaking chain-of-thought to end users.
Constrained decoding — when tool calls must be valid JSON

If tool_choice="auto", the model is free to emit prose or a tool call, and the parser disambiguates. If tool_choice="required" or a specific tool is named, engines switch on constrained decoding: xgrammar (vLLM default), Outlines, lm-format-enforcer, or SGLang's compressed FSM. The schema is compiled to a token-level finite-state machine; at each decode step, logits for tokens that would violate the grammar are masked to -inf. The output is provably valid by construction — no parse-retry loop. Cost: a one-time FSM compile (cached per schema) plus a per-token mask op.

SGLang's compressed FSM goes further: when the grammar forces a deterministic span (e.g. the literal "name": in a JSON schema), it collapses the chain of singular FSM transitions and emits multiple tokens in a single step instead of running the model. LMSYS reports up to 2× lower latency and 2.5× higher throughput on JSON character generation versus Outlines + vLLM and Guidance + llama.cpp.

Operator failure modes
  • Parser drift. A model minor version bumps its tool-call template; the named parser silently returns content instead of tool_calls. vLLM's docs flag this for small Llama variants, Mistral 7B (no parallel calls), and InternLM2 ("not stable").
  • Partial JSON on stream cutoff. Client disconnect or hitting max_tokens mid-call yields an unclosed brace. The streaming extractor must surface a clean error rather than silently truncating.
  • KV blow-up on long agent histories. A 50-turn coding agent with a 10,000-token system prompt can exceed per-session KV budgets. Without eviction-aware routing, the prefix cache thrashes and you lose the win you were trying to capture.
  • Schema vs parser disagreement. Constrained decoding validates content; tool-call JSON is validated by the parser path. The two can disagree at edge cases (escapes, unicode), producing tool calls that are valid by the parser but rejected by downstream schema validation.

Serving at Scale › Disaggregation

Prefill/decode disaggregation architecture new

Complete architecture walkthrough — fleets, KV transfer, independent scaling, and when it's worth the complexity

Why colocated serving has a fundamental problem
DimensionPrefillDecode
BottleneckCompute-bound (matrix multiplications)Memory-bandwidth-bound (weight loading)
GPU SM utilization90-95%20-40%
Memory bandwidth utilization30-50%90-95%
Latency contributionTTFTTBT / total decode time

When colocated, they interfere destructively. A 32K-token prefill blocks all ongoing decodes, causing TBT spikes. DistServe measured 2-30× decode latency inflation during large prefills. Chunked prefill mitigates but doesn't eliminate — each chunk still competes for compute.

AWS analogy: Running a CPU-intensive batch job and a latency-sensitive API server on the same instance. Solution: separate instance groups with independent scaling policies.

Visual: disaggregated serving architecture
PREFILL FLEET
High TFLOPS GPUs
Process prompts in parallel
Produce KV cache
Scale on: TTFT, queue depth
KV Cache Transfer
⟶
GPUDirect RDMA
~50 GB/s (InfiniBand)
DECODE FLEET
High HBM bandwidth GPUs
Generate tokens one at a time
Stream to client
Scale on: TBT, KV cache util
Each fleet scales independently — like separate ASGs for web tier and worker tier
Architecture and request flow

1. Request arrives at router/scheduler. 2. Router selects a prefill worker (least-loaded or prefix-cache-aware). 3. Prefill worker processes full prompt, produces KV cache + first token. 4. KV cache transfer to a decode worker (via GPUDirect RDMA, NVLink, or NCCL — potentially gigabytes for long contexts). 5. Decode worker continues generation using received KV cache. 6. Tokens streamed back to client. 7. On EOS, decode worker frees KV cache.

Independent scaling: Prefill fleet scales on TTFT and input throughput. Decode fleet scales on TBT and KV cache utilization. A burst of long-context requests scales prefill without touching decode. This is the web tier / worker tier pattern — the prefill fleet handles incoming work, decode fleet processes it asynchronously.

Framework comparison
FrameworkApproachKV transferKey insight
DistServe (OSDI 2024)Strict physical separation. Layer-by-layer pipelining.NCCL (prefers NVLink)7.4× more requests served while meeting SLO. Transfer pipelining hides latency.
Splitwise (ISCA 2024)Flexible — can be temporal on same GPU. Heterogeneous hardware.Datacenter network (100Gbps+)Different GPU types for prefill vs. decode. 1.4× throughput at equivalent SLO.
Mooncake (Moonshot AI)KV cache as a separate storage tier (CXL/DRAM/NVMe). Neither fleet owns the cache.CXL memory poolEliminates the stateless-vs-locality tension entirely. Prefix deduplication across 1000s of requests.
NVIDIA Dynamo (2025)Production framework built on TRT-LLM/vLLM. Rust control plane.Nixl + NCCLSupports both disaggregated and colocated modes. K8s-native deployment.
KV transfer — the Achilles' heel

Systems spend 10-30% of total request time on KV transfer. A 32K-token KV cache for a 70B model (~32 GB with GQA, FP16) takes ~640ms at InfiniBand 400Gbps (50 GB/s). Mitigations: layer-by-layer pipelining (DistServe — start transferring layer 0 while computing layer 1), FP8 KV cache (halves transfer volume), and NVLink for intra-node disaggregation (~44ms for the same transfer at 900 GB/s).

When disaggregation is worth the complexity
Disaggregate when...Stay colocated when...
Mixed workloads with highly variable prompt lengths (100 to 100K tokens)Uniform workloads (similar prompt and output lengths)
Strict TBT SLOs (<50ms) — can't tolerate any prefill interferenceSmall scale (<8 GPUs) — complexity not justified
Large scale (50+ GPUs) with independent scaling needsNo InfiniBand/NVLink — TCP adds 100ms-1s KV transfer latency
Long contexts (>32K) where prefill takes secondsSimplicity is a priority — one fleet, one scaling policy
Berkeley's retrospective finding: chunked prefill closes 60-80% of disaggregation's latency benefit at much lower complexity. The real win of disaggregation is independent scaling, not per-request latency.
Quantitative thresholds — when does disagg actually pay off?

The qualitative table above gets you direction; the Baseten/Dynamo deployment data gives concrete thresholds where disaggregation stops being theoretical and starts winning in practice:

  • Volume: ≥100M–1B tokens/day. Below that, the complexity tax (extra fleets, KV transfer plumbing, two scaling policies) outweighs the latency win.
  • Model size: ≥100B parameters. Smaller models prefill quickly enough that chunked prefill on a single fleet handles the interference fine.
  • Workload shape: prefill-heavy with long inputs (RAG over big docs, code editing, agent loops with growing context). For short-input/long-output chat, disagg gains very little.
xPyD notation — naming the fleet ratio

Production deployments name disaggregated topologies as xPyD — x prefill workers and y decode workers. 5P3D is five prefill replicas paired with three decode replicas, a typical ratio for prefill-heavy traffic. The notation is shorthand for the autoscaling policy: prefill and decode fleets are sized independently to match their respective bottlenecks.

Common ratios:

  • 1P1D — equal split. Default starting point for mixed workloads.
  • 2P1D / 3P1D — prefill-heavy. Long-input RAG, document Q&A, code-aware agents.
  • 1P2D / 1P3D — decode-heavy. Short prompts that produce long outputs (creative writing, reasoning chains).
Conditional disaggregation — don't pay the transfer cost when you don't need to

Naive disagg routes every request prefill→decode, paying KV transfer latency every time. Conditional disaggregation introduces a fast path: requests that are short or fully prefix-cached are handled by the decode worker locally — no transfer, no second fleet hop. Only requests with substantial uncached prefill cross the fleet boundary.

The decision typically lives in the router: estimate "how much new prefill compute is needed?" using prefix-cache hit rate plus prompt length; if below a threshold (e.g., <2K tokens of net prefill), keep the request on the decode worker. This avoids paying ~100ms of KV transfer for trivial completions where prefill itself takes <50ms.

Two failure modes — what alerts you should be running
Failure modeSignalCauseResponse
Prefill queue saturationTTFT spikes; prefill-fleet queue depth grows; decode workers idle waiting for KV cache deliveriesLong-input traffic exceeds prefill fleet's compute. Decode is over-provisioned relative to prefill.Scale prefill fleet up (raise x in xPyD); shed long-input traffic via admission control until capacity arrives.
Decode-side KV cache exhaustionDecode workers preempt or reject; KV cache utilization at 100%; TBT degrades; new requests stall after prefill completesLong output traffic / large concurrent sessions exceed decode HBM capacity. Prefill is over-provisioned relative to decode.Scale decode fleet up (raise y); enable KV quantization or G2/G3 KV demotion; reduce max_num_seqs.

The two fleets fail in different ways for different reasons, which is exactly the point of disaggregating in the first place — but it doubles the alerting and capacity-planning surface area. Run independent dashboards and SLO burn-rate alerts for each fleet.

Differing TP between fleets — and the transpose-kernel detail

Prefill is compute-heavy and benefits from low TP (each replica owns more parameters but uses fewer GPUs, freeing capacity for more replicas). Decode is bandwidth-heavy and benefits from higher TP (more aggregate HBM bandwidth per token). Production deployments commonly run, say, TP4 prefill paired with TP8 decode.

The complication: the KV cache produced by TP4 prefill is sharded differently than what TP8 decode expects. NVIDIA Dynamo solves this by including a transpose kernel in the KV transfer path — the cache is reshaped on the fly between fleets so each side keeps its native sharding. The reshape is overlapped with the NIXL-based RDMA transfer so it doesn't add measurable latency. Without the transpose, prefill and decode would have to share a TP value, eliminating one of disaggregation's biggest wins.

← Tool Calling & ReasoningNVIDIA Dynamo →
Serving at Scale › NVIDIA Dynamo

NVIDIA Dynamo new

A datacenter-scale inference orchestrator that wraps engines like vLLM, TensorRT-LLM, and SGLang.

The mental model: Kubernetes for inference engines

Dynamo is not a model engine. It does not own kernels, it does not implement attention, and it does not replace vLLM, TensorRT-LLM, or SGLang. It sits one layer above them — the orchestration plane that routes requests, scales worker pools, shuttles KV cache between nodes, and enforces SLOs across an entire fleet. The mental model: Dynamo is to inference engines what Kubernetes is to containers.

Announced at GTC 2025 and shipped to 1.0 the same year, it is the open-source successor to Triton's distributed-serving role, written ~55% in Rust (the data plane) and ~30% Python (extensibility). It is purpose-built for multi-node deployments — frontier MoEs, reasoning models with long thinking traces, agent fleets — where one engine instance is fundamentally not enough.

The five components that matter

Frontend — OpenAI-compatible API gateway, with service discovery via etcd + NATS or Kubernetes-native via CRDs and EndpointSlices. The boring part. Important because every other component depends on a fast, low-latency control plane.

Smart Router (KV-aware) — Tracks every cached KV block across the worker fleet using a radix tree (the "Flash Indexer," ~170M ops/s). Routes incoming requests to the worker with the highest prefix-cache overlap, not round-robin. NVIDIA reports 2× faster TTFT on Qwen3-Coder 480B from this single change. The whole point of prefix caching is wasted if the router doesn't know who has what.

Disaggregated prefill/decode — Splits the two phases onto separate worker pools because they have opposite resource shapes. Prefill is compute-bound, short-lived, large matmuls. Decode is memory-bandwidth-bound, long-lived, KV reads. Each pool can run a different parallelism strategy (e.g., prefill TP2, decode TP4) and scale independently. NVIDIA's reference burst ratio is 2 prefill workers (TP2) per 1 decode worker (TP4).

KVBM — KV Block Manager — Tiered KV-cache memory: HBM → CPU DDR → local SSD → remote/object storage. Hot blocks stay on GPU; cold blocks spill out and are pulled back on hit, avoiding re-prefill of long contexts. This is what lets you hold many concurrent long-context sessions without dedicating 80GB of HBM per session. Exactly the EBS-vs-instance-store-vs-S3 tiering pattern, applied to KV.

Planner — SLA-driven autoscaler that understands prefill vs decode separately. Watches TTFT and inter-token-latency (ITL); decides whether to add prefill workers, decode workers, or fall back to aggregated mode. Alibaba's APSARA 2025 result: 80% fewer SLA breaches at 5% lower TCO from running Planner over a static replica set.

NIXL (NVIDIA Inference Xfer Library) — Hardware-agnostic transport for moving KV blocks between workers and tiers. Backends include UCX (RDMA over InfiniBand or NVLink), GPUDirect Storage, and S3. This is the bus that makes disaggregation cheap enough to be worth doing — without RDMA, the prefill→decode KV handoff becomes the bottleneck and the whole architecture loses.

Where it fits in the stack
A typical Dynamo deployment shape:
[Frontend] → [Smart Router] → N decode workers (vLLM / TRT-LLM / SGLang)
                               → M prefill workers (M < N typically)
KVBM tier (HBM / DDR / SSD / RDMA) shared via NIXL
Planner watches SLOs and resizes M, N
Numbers worth remembering
  • Up to 30× request throughput on DeepSeek-R1 671B, GB200 NVL72.
  • 7× throughput per GPU on DeepSeek-R1, GB200 NVL72 vs B200 baseline.
  • >2× throughput on Llama 70B on Hopper.
  • 2× faster TTFT with KV-aware routing on Qwen3-Coder 480B.
  • 80% fewer SLA breaches at 5% lower TCO — Alibaba APSARA Planner result.
  • 7× faster cold-start via ModelExpress (GPU-to-GPU weight streaming) on DeepSeek-V3 / H200.
Hardware coupling and operational reality

Dynamo is designed around NVIDIA's reference platforms — Hopper (H100/H200), Blackwell (B200, GB200 NVL72, GB300 NVL72). NIXL leans on NVLink for intra-node and InfiniBand/RDMA for inter-node KV transfer. You can run disaggregation without RDMA, but the prefill→decode handoff becomes the slowest hop in your stack and the architectural premise breaks.

Operational complexity is real. You are running etcd + NATS (or Kubernetes + the Grove operator), an RDMA fabric, KVBM storage tiers, and a Planner whose autoscaling decisions you must trust in production. Dynamo conceptually competes with Ray Serve, KServe, Triton's older ensemble mode, and whatever bespoke router/orchestrator stack a frontier lab has built in-house. Its differentiator is KV-cache-awareness end to end — router + KVBM + NIXL — which generic orchestrators don't have.

When Dynamo is the wrong choice. If your workload is short, uncached, uniform-shape requests with no shared system prompts and no long contexts, a single well-tuned vLLM replica behind a plain load balancer is hard to beat. The maximum value comes from workloads with long shared prefixes (system prompts, agents, RAG) and long contexts (reasoning models, code agents).

← DisaggregationChapter 5 Quiz →
Serving at Scale › Quiz

Chapter 5 Quiz — Serving at Scale

Test your understanding of request routing, scheduling, admission control, and disaggregation

Chapter 5 Quiz — Serving at Scale
1 A request with a 10K-token prompt arrives. Your prefill throughput is 5,000 tok/sec and decode throughput is 50 tok/sec. The predicted output is 500 tokens. Your SLO is 15 seconds. Should you accept or reject?
Calculate: Prefill = 10,000/5,000 = 2s. Decode = 500/50 = 10s. Total = 12s. 12s < 15s SLO → Accept. If queue wait adds >3s, reject. This is the Mooncake early rejection pattern — estimate total time at admission, reject immediately with 503 if infeasible, rather than accepting, partially processing, and failing the SLO (wasting GPU compute on tokens that never reach the user).
2 In an agentic session, the model generates a tool call and waits 5 seconds for tool results. During those 5 seconds, what's happening to GPU resources?
The KV cache is held in GPU HBM (consuming memory but generating zero tokens). The GPU's compute units are idle for this session. If average tool execution is 5s and average decode between tools is 3s, the GPU is idle 5/(5+3) = 62.5% of the time for agentic sessions. This is the core tension: hold the KV cache (waste memory) or swap it out (add resume latency). Production systems increasingly use prefix caching with full context re-send as a simpler alternative.
3 Why is chunked prefill's real benefit about TBT consistency rather than TTFT?
Without chunking, a 10K-token prefill causes a 500ms TBT spike for all streaming users — their token flow freezes completely. With chunking (512-token chunks), TBT increases from 20ms to ~25ms per step — barely noticeable. TTFT actually gets slightly worse with chunking (prefill spreads across more steps), but for interactive chat, smooth streaming matters more than minimum absolute TTFT. Users tolerate waiting slightly longer for the first token; they don't tolerate their stream freezing mid-response.
4 In disaggregated inference, the Berkeley retrospective found that chunked prefill closes 60-80% of disaggregation's latency benefit. When is disaggregation still worth it?
Independent scaling is the real win. When your workload is a mix of long-context RAG (prefill-heavy) and short chat (decode-heavy), disaggregation lets you scale each fleet independently — like separate ASGs for web tier and worker tier. Also worth it when: strict TBT SLOs (<50ms) can't tolerate any prefill interference, scale exceeds 50+ GPUs, or you have heterogeneous GPUs (high-FLOPS for prefill, high-bandwidth for decode).
5 Your output length predictor classifies requests as short (1-100 tokens), medium (100-500), or long (500+) with 75% accuracy. Is this useful for scheduling?
Yes, very useful. Even a noisy predictor that correctly identifies the shortest 25% and longest 25% enables SJF-like scheduling with 2-3× reduction in average latency for short requests. You don't need exact token counts — you need relative ordering. Add aging (priority boost with wait time) to prevent long-request starvation: effective_priority = shortness_score + aging_factor × wait_time.

Operations › Training-to-Serving

Training-to-serving pipeline new

From training checkpoint to production endpoint — weight conversion, quantization, compilation, validation, and deployment

The pipeline stages

Training Checkpoint (.pt/.bin) → SafeTensors conversion → Quantization (optional) → Graph optimization (optional) → Validation → Packaging → Deployment

Weight format conversion
FormatSecurityLoadingUsed by
PyTorch .pt/.binUses pickle — can execute arbitrary code on load. Security risk.Must load entire file into memoryLegacy, training output
SafeTensors (HuggingFace)No code execution. Simple header + raw data.Memory-mapped (mmap) — load on demand. Supports sharding for parallel GPU loading.Default format (2025+). vLLM, TRT-LLM, SGLang all load natively.
GGUF (llama.cpp)Safe. Single file with metadata + weights.Supports in-file quantization.CPU/edge inference (Ollama, llama.cpp). Not GPU production.
Quantization as a pipeline step

Three sub-stages: 1. Calibrate (run 128-512 representative samples through the full-precision model, collect activation statistics per layer). 2. Quantize (apply scaling factors to convert weights/activations to target precision). 3. Validate (verify quality hasn't degraded unacceptably).

Tooling (2025-2026): AutoAWQ (AWQ for vLLM), AutoGPTQ, Neural Magic's llm-compressor (unified quantization → vLLM-optimized output), NVIDIA TensorRT Model Optimizer (quantization + sparsity for TRT-LLM), and NVIDIA NIM (pre-quantized, pre-compiled containers — download and run).

TensorRT compilation — what it does

TensorRT compiles a neural network into an optimized inference engine through:

  • Layer/operator fusion: Linear → Bias → ReLU becomes a single CUDA kernel. Eliminates HBM round-trips between operations.
  • Kernel auto-tuning: Benchmarks multiple kernel implementations and selects the fastest for the specific GPU, batch size, and tensor dimensions. GPU-specific — an A100 engine won't be optimal on H100.
  • Mixed precision: Selectively quantizes insensitive layers to INT8/FP8, keeps sensitive layers in FP16.

Trade-off: TRT-LLM engines achieve highest throughput but are inflexible — changing max_batch_size or adding a LoRA adapter requires recompilation (30-60 min). torch.compile (PyTorch 2.x) is a lighter-weight alternative with more flexibility.

Model validation
  • Numerical: Compare output logits between original and quantized models. Measure KL divergence (<0.01 for INT8/FP8, <0.05 for INT4) and perplexity degradation (<0.5% for INT8, <2% for INT4).
  • Task-specific: Run MMLU, HumanEval, GSM8K, MT-Bench. Flag any drop >1 point.
  • Production shadow: Run quantized model alongside production in shadow mode. Use LLM-as-judge for automated quality comparison at scale.
Deployment strategies
StrategyProsConsBest for
Bake into containerSelf-contained, fast cold start if cachedEnormous images (70B = 140 GB). Changing model = rebuild.Small models (<10 GB)
Download at startupSmall images (~2-3 GB). Easy model swaps.Cold start includes download (2-5 min for 70B from S3).Large models, multi-model
Shared filesystem (EFS/FSx/PVC)Near-instant access. Multiple pods share same files. mmap with SafeTensors.Infrastructure complexity. Network FS throughput can bottleneck.Production at scale (most common 2025+)
2025-2026 trends
  • Model CI/CD: New checkpoint → quantize to target precisions → eval suite (gate on quality) → load test → canary deploy → monitor quality → rollback if degraded.
  • Multi-LoRA serving: One base model in GPU memory, hundreds of LoRA adapters hot-loaded per-request. Pipeline: fine-tune → validate → register → deploy (hot-load into running vLLM).
  • NVIDIA NIM: Pre-quantized, pre-compiled model containers. Download and run — no pipeline needed. Trade-off: vendor lock-in to NVIDIA stack.
  • OCI model registries: Storing weights as OCI artifacts in ECR/GCR, unifying container and model distribution infrastructure.

Architectural philosophies

vLLM"The Linux of LLM inference" — general-purpose, community-driven, flexibility over peak perfPython + C++/CUDA kernelsUC Berkeley (2023). PagedAttention.
