Performance characteristics

All numbers approximate for LLaMA 70B on H100, FP16, high batch:

  • vLLM: ~2,000-4,000 output tok/sec. Broadest model support. 20-40% faster in V1 vs V0.
  • TRT-LLM: ~2,500-5,000 output tok/sec. 10-30% faster than vLLM on same hardware due to compiled kernels. Build step takes 30-60 min.
  • SGLang: ~2,000-4,500 output tok/sec. Within 5-10% of vLLM on standard; 2-5× faster for structured generation. RadixAttention gives 30-80% TTFT reduction for shared-prefix workloads.
  • Dynamo: Depends on underlying engine. Core benefit is disaggregation, not raw single-GPU perf.
When to choose each
vLLM: Default choice. Broadest model support, largest community, fastest feature iteration, multi-hardware. Start here.

TRT-LLM: Maximum NVIDIA throughput justifies operational complexity. NVIDIA NIM. Dedicated MLOps team available.

SGLang: Structured generation is primary use case. Multi-turn workloads where prefix caching is critical. Easiest path to high performance.

Dynamo: Need disaggregation at 50+ GPU scale. Strict TBT SLOs. High-bandwidth interconnect available.

Pragmatic default: Start with vLLM. Once you've identified specific bottlenecks, switch to the specialized engine. Same principle as AWS: don't start with a custom Spot placement strategy. Start with on-demand, prove the workload, then optimize.

← Training-to-ServingSGLang Deep Dive →
Operations › SGLang Deep Dive

SGLang new

A serving runtime built around RadixAttention and structured-output decoding — the inference engine of choice for prefix-heavy and agentic workloads.

Two things in one repo

SGLang (originated at LMSYS, UC Berkeley) is two related projects shipped together, and the distinction matters when picking what to deploy:

  • A serving runtime: a high-performance inference engine in the same category as vLLM and TensorRT-LLM. OpenAI-API compatible. Supports Llama, Qwen, DeepSeek, Kimi, GLM, Gemma, Mistral, plus embedding/reward/diffusion models. This is what most people deploy.
  • A frontend DSL: a Python-embedded language with primitives like gen, select, and fork for expressing multi-call LLM programs (agents, tree-of-thought, self-consistency, RAG pipelines). gen is non-blocking, fork clones a prompt into parallel branches, select constrains generation to a fixed set of choices.

The runtime works standalone with the OpenAI API. The DSL is what unlocks the most dramatic wins because it tells the runtime which calls share prefixes, so the cache can do its job. According to SGLang's own docs, the project now powers >400,000 GPUs and trillions of tokens per day.

RadixAttention — the headline trick

Standard prefix caches use a flat hash on (model, prompt). RadixAttention stores all active KV cache entries in a radix tree keyed by token sequence. Shared prefixes become shared subtrees, so a system prompt + few-shot exemplars + branching completions are physically shared in GPU memory exactly once. Operations are O(prefix length) for search/insert; eviction is LRU with reference-counting so an in-flight request can't evict its own cache.

This is strictly stronger than a hash table when generations branch — parallel sampling (n > 1), beam-like agent fan-out, tree-of-thought, self-consistency — because the divergence point is automatically a tree split. A flat hash would either miss those siblings or duplicate them. The 9-workload paper benchmark (MMLU, HellaSwag, ReAct, ToT, JSON, multi-turn chat, DSPy RAG, LLaVA) reports up to 5× throughput vs vLLM and Guidance on Llama-7B (A10G) and Mixtral-8×7B with TP, and the abstract claims up to 6.4× vs prior SOTA across the suite.

Compressed FSM and jump-forward decoding

For structured output (JSON Schema, regex, EBNF), SGLang doesn't just mask logits per token. When the grammar forces a deterministic span — e.g. the literal "name": in a JSON schema — it collapses the chain of singular FSM transitions and emits multiple tokens in one step, skipping the model entirely for the forced span. Re-tokenization handles the boundary conflicts where the forced text doesn't align with model token boundaries.

Reported gains: up to 2× lower latency and 2.5× higher throughput vs Outlines+vLLM and Guidance+llama.cpp on JSON character generation and document-extraction (LLaVA). The xgrammar backend is now the default for general CFG / JSON-Schema / regex constraints, and it composes with RadixAttention so the constrained portion of the prefix is still cache-shared across requests.

Net effect for function-calling and structured-output workloads — the bread and butter of agent infra — is that you get both the prefix-cache win and the FSM-skip win in the same request.
What it shares with vLLM

Continuous batching, paged attention, chunked prefill, speculative decoding (EAGLE / MTP heads), prefill-decode disaggregation, tensor / pipeline / expert / data parallelism, multi-LoRA batching, FP8 / FP4 / INT4 / AWQ / GPTQ quantization, a zero-overhead CPU scheduler. MoE handling is well-tuned for DeepSeek-V3/R1 (DeepSeek themselves recommend SGLang for production); MLA, EP, and recently "Elastic EP" for partial-failure tolerance in large MoE deployments are all upstream.

When SGLang wins, when it doesn't
ScenarioBest engineWhy
Plain high-QPS chat, single prompt, no shared prefixvLLM (parity), TRT-LLMSGLang's tree pays bookkeeping overhead it can't amortize without prefix sharing.
Agent loops with shared system prompts, multi-turn, RAGSGLangRadixAttention + cache-aware scheduler dominate when prefixes are long and reused.
JSON / function-calling / structured output throughputSGLangCompressed FSM + xgrammar; tokens for forced spans cost zero model time.
Tree-of-thought, parallel sampling, self-consistencySGLangBranching becomes tree splits; siblings share ancestors in cache automatically.
Peak single-stream latency on NVIDIA-only deploymentTRT-LLMHand-tuned CUDA kernels, FP8 attention kernels, in-flight batching with TRT plugins are most mature there.
Mature productionization story on NVIDIA TritonTRT-LLMTightest integration with the existing NVIDIA inference stack.
Recent capabilities
  • Prefill–decode disaggregation in production (separate pools for compute-bound prefill vs memory-bound decode).
  • Multi-node serving for frontier MoEs (DeepSeek-V3 / R1, Kimi).
  • Multimodal pipelines (LLaVA, Qwen-VL) with structured extraction.
  • Function-calling parsers for Llama, Qwen, Mistral, DeepSeek tool-use formats.
  • Hardware breadth: NVIDIA GB200 / B300 / H100 / A100, AMD MI300 / MI355, Intel Xeon CPUs, Google TPU, Ascend NPU.
  • Day-0 support for new models (e.g., Nemotron 3 Super) and Elastic EP for MoE failure tolerance.
Numbers worth remembering
  • Up to 6.4× throughput vs prior SOTA across the paper's nine-workload benchmark.
  • Up to 5× throughput vs vLLM and Guidance on agent / ToT / JSON / RAG workloads.
  • 2× lower latency, 2.5× higher throughput on JSON-constrained decoding via compressed FSM.
  • Operational scale per the docs: >400,000 GPUs, trillions of tokens per day.

← Engine ComparisonBenchmarking →
Operations › Benchmarking

Benchmarking methodology new

Open-loop vs closed-loop, coordinated omission, graduated ramps, and how to avoid misleading results

What to measure
MetricWhat it tells youReport as
TTFTPrefill latency (responsiveness)P50, P95, P99
TBT / ITLDecode latency per step (streaming smoothness)P50, P95, P99
TPOTAverage time per output token = (E2E - TTFT) / (output_tokens - 1)Mean
E2E latencyTotal request timeP50, P95, P99
Output throughputTotal output tokens/sec across all requestsMean at steady state
GoodputSLO-compliant throughput (the metric that matters most)requests meeting SLO / total

Always report the full distribution, not just averages. A system with 50ms average but 5s P99 is much worse than one with 80ms average and 120ms P99.

Why percentiles, not averages — latency is right-skewed

LLM serving latency distributions are heavily right-skewed: a long tail of slow requests pulls the mean upward, but most requests are clustered near the median. Reporting the mean (or a P50) hides the worst behavior; reporting only P99 hides typical behavior. The standard reporting set:

  • P50 (median) — what a typical user experiences. Should be near your "fast" target.
  • P90 / P95 — the experience of a busy power user who sends many requests. If P95 is 5× P50 you have a queueing or batching problem.
  • P99 — the worst your largest customers' tenth-of-a-percent of traffic ever sees. Drives the SLO that determines whether you're paged.
  • P99.9 — only meaningful at very high QPS; otherwise it's noise from a handful of requests.

SLOs almost always live at P95/P99, not the mean. A system whose P99 is 10× its P50 will burn pager budget even if its mean looks great. Run all comparison tables (engines, hardware, configs) at the same percentile and the same load level — comparing P99 of system A at 10 RPS to P99 of system B at 50 RPS is meaningless.

Open-loop vs closed-loop — the critical distinction

Closed-loop: Send request, wait for completion, send next. With N clients, always exactly N in-flight. When the system slows down, request rate drops automatically. This hides the real overload behavior.

Open-loop: Send requests at a fixed rate (Poisson process), regardless of system speed. When the system slows, requests pile up. You see the real queueing latency. This is how real production traffic works — users don't wait for other users' requests to finish.

A system benchmarked closed-loop at 50 concurrent clients might show 200ms P99. The same system open-loop at equivalent request rate might show 2000ms P99 — 10× worse. The difference is queueing delay, which closed-loop hides by construction. Always use open-loop for production capacity planning.
Visual: open-loop vs closed-loop under load
Closed-loop (misleading)

50 clients, each waits for response before sending next request.

t=0
50ms
t=50ms
50ms
t=100ms
5000ms (stall!)
t=5100ms

During the 5s stall, no new requests sent.
Measured P99 = ~50ms. Looks fine!
Open-loop (realistic)

Requests arrive at fixed 100/sec rate regardless of system speed.

t=0
50ms
t=50ms
50ms
t=100ms
5000ms stall → 500 requests queue!
queued
500 requests wait 1-5 seconds each
500 requests experience multi-second latency.
Measured P99 = ~5000ms. Real behavior!
Coordinated omission

In a closed-loop benchmark, when a request takes 5 seconds instead of 50ms, the next request is delayed 5 seconds. During those 5 seconds, no measurements are recorded. But in reality, ~100 users would have sent requests during those 5 seconds, all experiencing multi-second latency.

The benchmark records one 5s measurement. Reality would record ~100 measurements of 5+ seconds. P99 is drastically under-reported.

Fix: Use open-loop benchmarking (vLLM's benchmark_serving.py in Poisson mode avoids this by design), or apply Gil Tene's HdrHistogram correction.

Load test design — graduated ramps

Don't benchmark at a single flat rate. Use a graduated ramp: start at 5 req/s, increase by 5 every 2 minutes. Plot latency vs. throughput at each step. This reveals:

  • Linear region: Latency is stable, system has headroom.
  • The knee: Latency starts climbing — saturation begins. This is your practical capacity.
  • Overload region: Latency explodes, throughput may decrease.

AWS analogy: This is exactly a step-scaling stress test. You wouldn't set target tracking to 80% CPU without first knowing what load corresponds to 80%. The graduated ramp gives you the load-to-utilization curve.

Common pitfalls
  • Cold cache: First requests are always slower (CUDA compilation, KV block allocation). Send 50+ warmup requests, discard from measurement.
  • Synthetic prompts: "Write a 500-word essay" produces predictable lengths. Use ShareGPT or production replays.
  • Fixed max_tokens: Makes every request the same "size." Real traffic has heavy-tailed output distributions.
  • Ignoring GPU thermal throttling: GPUs throttle at high temperatures. Long benchmarks show decreasing throughput over time. Monitor temperature.
  • Network contamination: Benchmarking a remote API includes network RTT in TTFT. Benchmark locally for engine perf, remotely for user-perceived latency.
Shadow traffic — the only way to test something that isn't in production yet

Synthetic loads (ShareGPT, fixed lengths, uniform Poisson) tell you something about the engine but very little about your workload. The most reliable benchmark is shadowing: replay real production traffic against a candidate config and compare against the live system. To produce results that actually transfer to production, the shadow workload has to match production on five dimensions:

DimensionWhy it mattersHow to match it
ISL/OSL distribution (input/output sequence length)Average lengths hide the workload — a 50/50 mix of 100-token and 50K-token prompts behaves nothing like a uniform 25K-token prompt at the same mean.Sample real prompts; preserve the histogram, not just the mean.
Traffic volume + jitterSteady-state throughput is the easy case. Real traffic has bursts (login storms, viral content, cron jobs) that exercise queueing and admission paths.Replay timestamps, not Poisson. Or layer Poisson onto a daily traffic curve.
Request contentsDifferent content distributions hit different cache hit rates, route differently, and trigger different speculative-decode acceptance rates.Use real (or scrubbed) production prompts, not generated ones.
Input parametersTemperature, top-p, max_tokens, stop sequences, tool definitions all change scheduler behavior — fixed values mask whole classes of bugs.Replay the parameter mix as users actually send it.
Tenant / model mixMulti-tenant systems with LoRA adapters, multiple base models, or per-customer system prompts behave very differently from a single-model benchmark.Preserve the tenant distribution; route requests as production routes them.

Operationally, run shadow traffic as mirrored requests (the candidate sees real prompts, the response is discarded; the live system handles the user). This catches regressions that a synthetic benchmark would miss and lets you compare candidate vs production on the exact same input distribution.

Evals vs benchmarks — different questions, different tools

Two words that are routinely conflated and shouldn't be:

BenchmarkEval
Question answered"How fast / cheap / efficient is this serving stack on this hardware?""How good are the model's outputs on this task?"
What you changeEngine, hardware, batching, quantization config, parallelism planModel, prompt, sampling, fine-tune
OutputLatency / throughput / cost numbersAccuracy / pass rate / win rate
ExamplesvLLM benchmark_serving, GenAI-Perf, MLPerf InferenceMMLU, GSM8K, HumanEval, SWE-bench, internal task suites, A/B preference

Both are necessary and they answer orthogonal questions. A typical mistake is reporting "Engine X is faster" without checking that quality is held constant — quantization or speculative decoding regressions can show up only on the eval side. Always pair a benchmarking change with at least a smoke eval on a representative task.

Goodhart's Law: "When a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure." Optimizing single-metric benchmarks (e.g., raw tokens/sec) can degrade the things you actually care about — cache hit rate, P99 latency, output quality. Goodput (SLO-compliant throughput) is one well-known antidote because it bakes the SLO into the metric. The deeper antidote: track a small portfolio of metrics together (latency percentiles, throughput, quality, cost) and refuse to ship a change that improves one at the expense of another without explicit justification.

Standard tools
  • vLLM benchmark_serving.py: De facto community standard. Open-loop Poisson mode. ShareGPT dataset. Reports TTFT, TPOT, E2E, throughput.
  • GenAI-Perf (NVIDIA): Purpose-built for LLM benchmarking. Generates latency-throughput curves automatically. TRT-LLM / Triton native.
  • SGLang Genai-bench: SGLang's first-party benchmarking harness — covers prefix-caching scenarios and disaggregated topologies natively.
  • MLPerf Inference (MLCommons): Industry standard. LLM scenarios added in v4.0 (2024). Server scenario uses open-loop + SLO compliance — addresses coordinated omission by design.
  • Locust / k6: general-purpose HTTP load generators useful when you want to mix LLM traffic with other backend calls in an end-to-end test.
← SGLang Deep DiveCost Economics →
Operations › Cost Economics

Inference cost economics new

$/token math — how optimization decisions translate to dollars

The fundamental formula

Every inference cost calculation reduces to one equation:

$/Mtok = GPU_cost_per_hour / (tokens_per_second × 0.0036)

Or equivalently: $/Mtok = GPU_cost_per_hour × 1,000,000 / (tokens_per_second × 3,600)

Concrete example — LLaMA 3 70B on H100 (FP16, continuous batching):

  • GPU cost: $2.50/hr (reserved). Throughput at batch ~64: ~2,000 output tokens/sec → $0.35/Mtok output
  • Same model, single request (no batching): ~35 tokens/sec → $19.84/Mtok output

That's a 57× cost difference between batched and unbatched — illustrating why continuous batching is the single most important optimization for inference economics.

Worked example: cost per million tokens
1Setup: 2× H100 GPUs serving LLaMA 70B with tensor parallelism. GPU cost: $2.50/hr per GPU = $5.00/hr total.
2Throughput (batched): Continuous batching at batch ~64 achieves ~2,500 output tokens/sec.
3Apply formula: $/Mtok = $5.00 / (2,500 × 0.0036) = $5.00 / $9.00 = $0.56/Mtok
4Adjust for utilization (70%): $0.56 / 0.70 = $0.80/Mtok
5Adjust for goodput (85% SLO compliance): $0.80 / 0.85 = $0.94/Mtok
Effective cost: $0.94/Mtok output. Compare: Claude Sonnet 4 charges $15/Mtok output. The ~16× margin covers R&D, safety, RLHF, API infrastructure, and peak capacity headroom.
Including utilization and goodput

Utilization adjusts for idle GPUs: Effective $/Mtok = base $/Mtok / utilization_fraction. At 60% utilization: $0.35 / 0.60 = $0.58/Mtok. Idle time is the silent killer — at $2.50/hr per H100, every idle hour is pure waste. This is why autoscaling (your Phase 3-4 work) is so critical.

Goodput adjusts for wasted work (preempted requests, speculative decoding rejections, timeouts, thinking tokens). A system running 3,000 raw tok/sec with 15% eviction and 5% timeout: effective goodput = 3,000 × 0.85 × 0.95 = 2,423 useful tok/sec. Effective $/Mtok = $0.35 / 0.808 = $0.43/Mtok.

Input vs output cost asymmetry

Input (prefill) tokens are ~10-30× cheaper to process than output (decode) tokens. Prefill is compute-bound and processes thousands of tokens in parallel. Decode is memory-bandwidth-bound, generating one token at a time per sequence. This is why API providers charge 3-5× more for output tokens.

How optimizations affect the cost curve
OptimizationThroughput multiplierCost reductionQuality impact
FP16 → FP8 (H100)1.5-1.8×-40% to -45%Negligible (<0.5% degradation)
FP16 → INT4 (AWQ/GPTQ)1.8-2.5×-45% to -60%1-3% quality loss
Static → continuous batching2-5×-50% to -80%None
Prefix caching (shared prompts)—50-90% reduction in input costNone
Dense → MoE (equivalent quality)2-4×-50% to -75%Comparable
Disaggregation (prefill/decode)—-20% to -40%None
API pricing vs estimated serving cost
Provider / ModelInput $/MtokOutput $/MtokNotes
Claude Sonnet 4$3.00$15.00Cached input: $0.30
Claude Opus 4$15.00$75.00Cached input: $1.50
GPT-4.1$2.00$8.00
GPT-4.1-mini$0.40$1.60
Self-hosted LLaMA 70B (2×H100)—~$0.50-1.00Includes ~30% operational overhead

Estimated serving cost for a frontier model: $0.50-2.00/Mtok output at scale. The 7-30× margin covers R&D amortization, safety infrastructure, RLHF training, API infrastructure, and peak capacity headroom.

Pricing trend: Frontier model output pricing has dropped ~4× per year (GPT-4: $60/Mtok in 2023 → GPT-4o: $15/Mtok in 2024 → GPT-4.1: $8/Mtok in 2025) through better hardware, quantization, distillation, and MoE architectures.

Thinking tokens — the new cost dimension

Reasoning models (o1/o3, Claude extended thinking, DeepSeek-R1) generate internal chain-of-thought tokens — sometimes 10-100× the visible output length — before producing the final answer. A request generating 500 visible tokens might consume 5,000-50,000 thinking tokens internally, making effective compute cost 10-100× higher than non-reasoning inference. Providers typically bill thinking tokens at output token rates, creating strong economic pressure for thinking token optimization (pruning redundant reasoning, caching common reasoning patterns, early termination on high confidence).

The cost formula connects directly to your Phase 3-4 work: autoscaling maximizes utilization (denominator), admission control maximizes goodput (numerator). Both directly reduce effective $/Mtok. Phase 7's scheduling work will optimize at the micro level — given fixed GPUs, maximize the useful work per dollar.
← BenchmarkingRoofline, MFU, SOL & Unhobblers →
Operations › Roofline, MFU, SOL & Unhobblers

Roofline, MFU, SOL, and unhobblers new

The conceptual framework for reasoning about GPU utilization: arithmetic intensity, the roofline model, what limits efficiency, and the breakthroughs that remove ceilings rather than improve constants

The roofline model — every operation is either compute-bound or bandwidth-bound

Before measuring utilization you have to know what kind of work the GPU is doing. The roofline model classifies any operation by a single number — its arithmetic intensity — and tells you which physical limit (compute or memory bandwidth) it hits first.

Arithmetic intensity = FLOPs performed ÷ bytes moved between HBM and the chip. Units: ops/byte. Low intensity = lots of memory traffic per math op (memory-bound). High intensity = lots of math per byte loaded (compute-bound).

Each GPU has a corresponding ops:byte ratio — its peak FLOP/s divided by its peak HBM bandwidth. For an H100 SXM in FP16: 989 TFLOPS / 3.35 TB/s ≈ 295 ops/byte. That's the breakeven point. An operation whose arithmetic intensity is below 295 will hit the bandwidth ceiling and waste FLOPs. An operation above 295 will hit the compute ceiling and waste bandwidth.

GPUFP16 TFLOPSHBM BWops:byte (FP16)
H100 SXM9893.35 TB/s~295
H2009894.8 TB/s~206 — same compute, more bandwidth → easier to feed
B200 (FP8)45008 TB/s~563 — Blackwell raises the breakeven, demanding higher intensity

The "roofline" is a graph: arithmetic intensity on the x-axis, achievable performance on the y-axis. There are two ceilings — a slanted bandwidth ceiling on the left and a flat compute ceiling on the right. Where they meet is the chip's ops:byte ratio. Every operation lives somewhere on that plot.

Worked example — why decode is memory-bound

Standard multi-head attention during decode: each new token loads the entire KV cache, multiplies it by the new query, applies softmax, multiplies by V, and writes back the result. The arithmetic-vs-bandwidth math (Llama-style 70B model, FP16, single decode step):

  • FLOPs per token: the dominant cost is the QKT and softmax×V multiplies — roughly 4 × num_heads × d_head × context_length FLOPs.
  • Bytes loaded per token: the full KV cache for this sequence — roughly 2 × num_kv_heads × d_head × context_length × 2 bytes.
  • Arithmetic intensity: works out to ~62 ops/byte for naive multi-head attention on a typical configuration.

62 is far below the H100's 295 breakeven. Decode attention is squarely on the bandwidth ceiling — the GPU's arithmetic units finish their math and wait for the next chunk of KV cache to arrive. No amount of additional FLOPs helps; you need either more bandwidth (H200), less data movement per FLOP (FlashAttention's tiling, GQA/MLA's smaller KV cache), or higher arithmetic intensity per pass (larger batch sizes amortize the same KV load across more queries). Every optimization in the chapter is, mechanically, an attempt to push some operation's intensity above the chip's ops:byte ratio.

Prefill is different: the full prompt is processed in one matmul-heavy forward pass with high arithmetic intensity (often >500 ops/byte for long prompts) — solidly compute-bound. This compute-bound vs bandwidth-bound asymmetry between prefill and decode is the first-principles reason chunked prefill, disaggregation, and continuous batching all exist.

MFU — Model FLOP Utilization

MFU (Model FLOP Utilization) is the ratio of useful model computation actually being performed to the maximum theoretical computation the hardware could perform. It answers: "Of all the arithmetic operations my GPU is capable of per second, what fraction is actually going toward useful model computation?"

An H100 SXM has a peak of ~494 TFLOPS for BF16 (Brain Float 16 — a 16-bit format with wide dynamic range, standard for training; see Number Formats section for details) (dense, without structured sparsity). If a training run achieves 200 TFLOPS of useful model computation, MFU = 200/494 ≈ 40%. State-of-the-art training runs achieve 35-50% MFU. Getting above 50% is considered excellent. Most naive implementations fall below 30%.

Why MFU is almost never 100% — the sources of waste
  • Memory bandwidth bottleneck (the biggest one for inference): For memory-bandwidth-bound operations like decode, the GPU's arithmetic units complete their computation and then sit idle waiting for the next batch of data to arrive from HBM. The GPU is literally waiting for data, not computing. No amount of TFLOPS helps here — you need more HBM bandwidth.
  • Communication overhead: In tensor parallelism, the all-reduce operations require all GPUs to stop computing and exchange results. More GPUs in the TP group = more communication overhead = lower MFU. There is an optimal TP degree beyond which the communication cost exceeds the parallelism benefit.
  • Kernel launch overhead: Launching a GPU kernel (a unit of computation) has fixed overhead regardless of how much work it does. Many small sequential operations have disproportionate overhead. Kernel fusion (combining multiple operations into one kernel) directly addresses this.
  • Pipeline bubbles: In pipeline parallelism, GPUs sit idle at the start and end of each batch waiting for adjacent stages. Each bubble is wasted compute time. For inference with small batches, bubbles dominate.
  • Attention quadratic scaling at long sequences: Standard attention becomes memory-bandwidth-bound as sequence length grows — you're spending more time reading the attention matrix from HBM than doing arithmetic. FlashAttention's tiled SRAM algorithm directly improves MFU for attention by reducing HBM reads.
SOL — Speed of Light

SOL is Jensen Huang's first-principles performance concept. Before asking "why is this slow?", ask "what is the theoretical fastest this could possibly be given the physics?" That ceiling is the Speed of Light for this system. For a GPU: 100% peak TFLOPS with no overhead. MFU then measures how close to SOL you're achieving.

For an inference system: the SOL would be if every request was instantly prefilled and decoded with zero scheduling overhead, zero memory fragmentation, zero communication latency, and 100% arithmetic throughput. Everything else is friction between the real system and SOL.

The value of SOL thinking: it separates "fundamental physics limits" from "engineering inefficiencies we can fix." A 40% MFU means 60% of your GPU's potential is being consumed by things that aren't useful model computation. Some of that 60% is unavoidable (communication is physically necessary). Much of it can be attacked — and every optimization technique in this guide is an attack on some specific source of that gap.

Deep Dive — Inference MFU is misleading; use MBU instead

Typical inference MFU: Prefill achieves 30-50% MFU (comparable to training — large batch of prompt tokens drives compute). Decode achieves 1-5% MFU — extremely low because each decode step processes one token per request, making weight loads dominate over FLOPs. Combined serving MFU across a mixed workload: typically 5-15%.

Why MFU is the wrong metric for decode: MFU measures how close you are to peak FLOP/s. But decode is bottlenecked by HBM bandwidth, not compute. A GPU at 1% MFU during decode can be at 95% memory bandwidth utilization — which is the actual binding constraint. You're measuring the wrong resource.

Better metrics for inference: (1) MBU (Model Bandwidth Utilization) — actual HBM bandwidth used / peak HBM bandwidth. A well-tuned decode system should achieve 70-90% MBU. (2) Tokens/second/dollar for cost efficiency. (3) TTFT and TBT at P50/P99 for SLO compliance. (4) KV cache utilization % — like capacity pool utilization in Auto Scaling: if KV cache is full, you're blocking new requests regardless of GPU compute availability.

At NVIDIA, SOL thinking is used culturally at every level — frontline engineers use it as naturally as managers. When someone proposes a timeline or a performance number, the immediate question is "what's the SOL here, and why are we not at it?"

Unhobblers — the concept that explains technological step-changes

An "unhobbler" (term from Leopold Aschenbrenner's Situational Awareness essay) is a scientific or engineering discovery that removes a constraint that was previously preventing further scaling or progress — not just improving efficiency by a constant factor, but changing the shape of the scaling curve entirely. It's what differentiates a "10% speedup" from "the entire paradigm is now possible."

UnhobblerConstraint it removedWhat it unlocked
Attention mechanism (2017)RNNs couldn't attend to distant tokens — quality was capped regardless of scale because long-range dependencies were lossyTransformers, modern LLMs, the entire current AI era
FlashAttention (2022)O(n²) memory cost made contexts beyond ~4k tokens economically infeasibleThe long-context era: 4k → 32k → 128k → 1M token contexts
MoE architectureEvery parameter must activate on every token — parameter count was directly coupled to compute costModels with enormous knowledge capacity at practical compute cost (GPT-4, Mixtral, DeepSeek)
MLA (2024)KV cache memory wall made serving very long contexts economically infeasible at scaleLong-context serving at competitive cost — breaking the serving memory wall
Multi-token prediction (maturing)One-token-per-step sequential ceiling on decode throughputPotentially: higher decode throughput without a separate draft model
The pattern to internalize: optimization techniques (quantization, kernel fusion, batching) improve constant factors — they make existing things faster. Unhobblers change the scaling curve — they make previously impossible things possible. When evaluating a new technique, asking "is this a constant-factor improvement or does it change the scaling curve?" tells you how transformative it's likely to be.
📖 Situational Awareness — Leopold Aschenbrenner (introduces "unhobbler")PaLM paper — introduced MFU as a standard metric

Software Stack › CUDA Primer

CUDA — what's actually running on the GPU new

Kernels, graphs, the runtime, and why GEMM is the operation that matters

The stack you're sitting on top of

Every line of vLLM, SGLang, or TRT-LLM ultimately compiles down to CUDA kernels running on NVIDIA GPUs. When something is slow or breaks, the trail of blame usually leads here. Reading inference engine logs becomes much easier once you know what each layer does.

LayerWhat it isYou touch it when…
CUDA kernelA C++ function annotated with __global__ that runs on GPU threads. Compiled by nvcc into PTX/SASS.Writing or hand-tuning a custom op (FlashAttention, Marlin, custom MoE).
CUDA runtimeThe userspace library (libcudart) that dispatches kernels, manages streams, and copies memory.Debugging launch failures, OOMs, illegal memory accesses.
CUDA driverThe kernel-mode driver providing the actual GPU API surface (libcuda). Versioned independently from the runtime.Mismatched driver vs CUDA toolkit version blocks engine startup — first thing to check on a new node.
CUDA GraphsA pre-recorded DAG of kernel launches replayed in one shot, eliminating per-kernel launch overhead (~5–10 μs per kernel).Reducing decode step time when you have many small kernels per layer.
The thread/block/grid hierarchy in one paragraph

A kernel launches a grid of thread blocks. Each block runs on one SM (streaming multiprocessor — see Hardware Depth chapter) and contains up to 1024 threads grouped into warps of 32. Threads in the same warp execute in lockstep (SIMT). Threads in the same block share fast SRAM ("shared memory") and can synchronize. Threads in different blocks cannot. This hierarchy maps directly onto the hardware: warps onto SIMT lanes, blocks onto SMs, grid onto the full GPU. Every CUDA optimization technique is fundamentally about laying out work so this mapping is efficient.

GEMM is the operation that matters

GEMM (General Matrix Multiply, pronounced "gem") is the central operation of LLM inference. Every attention projection, every FFN linear layer, every output projection is a GEMM. ~95% of inference compute lives in GEMMs; everything else (softmax, layer norm, RoPE, activation functions) is small.

That's why the rest of this chapter is mostly about libraries and tools that make GEMMs fast: cuBLAS provides the standard implementation, CUTLASS lets you write custom variants, FlashAttention is a fused attention-as-GEMM kernel, DeepGEMM is FP8-specialized for Hopper. When someone says "the kernel," they almost always mean "the GEMM kernel for this specific shape and precision."

CUDA Graphs — the under-appreciated decode optimization

A single decode step on a 70B model launches dozens to hundreds of small kernels: attention QKV projections, FlashAttention, FFN up/down projections, layer norm, residuals, RoPE. Each launch costs ~5–10 μs of host-side overhead. For a 20 ms decode step, that's potentially 25–50% pure overhead before any actual GPU work.

CUDA Graphs let you record this entire kernel sequence once and replay it as a single dispatch. The replay cost is microseconds. vLLM, TRT-LLM, and SGLang all use CUDA Graphs for the decode hot path — it's why their benchmarks always include a "warmup" phase where the graph is captured for each batch size. The graph is keyed by tensor shapes, so the engine has to keep one per relevant shape combination; this is also why suddenly changing batch size mid-run can cause a latency hiccup.

Why kernel selection matters across hardware generations

A kernel optimized for H100 isn't automatically optimal on B200. Tensor Core instruction widths, supported precisions (FP8 vs FP4), shared memory sizes, and async-copy semantics all change between generations. The same engine running the same model on different hardware will pick different kernels — sometimes from totally different libraries (cuBLAS-on-Ampere vs CUTLASS-on-Hopper vs DeepGEMM-on-Hopper). Engine release notes that say "improved Blackwell support" mean someone spent weeks porting kernels.

📖 CUDA C++ Programming GuideCUDA Graphs blog

Software Stack › Numerical Libraries

cuBLAS, cuDNN, CUTLASS, CuTe, FlashInfer, DeepGEMM new

The library stack between PyTorch and the metal — what each one does and when each one wins

The library tower
LibraryOwnerWhat it doesWhen it's used
cuBLASNVIDIA, closed-sourceHighly tuned GEMM and BLAS-3 primitives. Picks the best precompiled kernel for given shape + precision.The default GEMM backend for PyTorch, vLLM, and most engines on standard precisions.
cuDNNNVIDIA, closed-sourceDeep-learning ops (convolution, attention fusions, normalization). Tightly tuned for each GPU generation.Convolution-heavy workloads, some attention paths, RNNs.
CUTLASSNVIDIA, open-source (Apache 2.0)A C++ template library for writing custom GEMMs. Lets you compose tile sizes, instruction shapes, and epilogues.When cuBLAS doesn't have a fast kernel for your specific shape/precision (custom MoE GEMMs, fused attention variants, INT4 GEMMs).
CuTeNVIDIA (part of CUTLASS 3.x)"CUDA Tensors" — a layout algebra for describing how tile data flows through registers, shared memory, and Tensor Cores.Writing modern Hopper/Blackwell kernels that need precise control over async copy and Tensor Core MMAs.
FlashInferUW + community, open-sourceA specialized library of attention kernels for serving (PagedAttention, varlen attention, sparse attention).vLLM's modern attention path, SGLang's RadixAttention, anywhere mixed prefill+decode batches need varlen kernels.
DeepGEMMDeepSeek, open-sourceFP8 GEMM kernels specialized for Hopper. Very small (~few hundred lines) and very fast on the precise shapes DeepSeek models use.FP8 inference on H100/H200; reference for "what does a focused kernel achieve vs a general library?"
When you'd hand-write a kernel

You don't write CUDA from scratch every day. The realistic pattern: an engine team writes new kernels when (1) a new model architecture has a GEMM shape cuBLAS doesn't tune well for, (2) a new precision (FP8, FP4, NVFP4) lands on a new GPU and needs library support, or (3) profiling shows a custom-fused kernel could replace 3–4 separate ops with one. FlashAttention, Marlin, DeepGEMM, and the per-engine MoE kernels all came from one of these three motivations.

Reading engine release notes through this lens

"Added DeepGEMM support" → faster FP8 inference on Hopper. "Switched attention backend to FlashInfer" → better paged + varlen handling, particularly for mixed prefill/decode batches. "CUTLASS 3.x integration" → engine can now target Hopper's WGMMA and async copy properly. Once you know which library does what, the changelog reads like an architecture diagram.

📖 CUTLASS on GitHubFlashInferDeepGEMM
← CUDA PrimerKernel Fusion →
Software Stack › Kernel Fusion

Kernel fusion new

Why combining ops into one kernel is the most consistent source of inference speedups

The HBM round-trip problem

Most LLM ops alone are simple: layer norm is a few muls and adds, RoPE is a rotation, residual addition is just +. They're individually trivial. The problem is that each op, run as a separate kernel, has to (1) read its inputs from HBM into registers, (2) compute, (3) write the output back to HBM. Then the next kernel reads what was just written. For memory-bound ops, the HBM round-trip dominates the actual arithmetic by an order of magnitude or more.

Concrete example: a residual block does y = x + LayerNorm(MLP(x)). Run naively, that's at least 4 kernels (MLP up, MLP down, LayerNorm, add) and 4 HBM round-trips for the residual stream. Fused into one kernel, the intermediate values stay in registers/SRAM and the residual stream touches HBM exactly once.

Three flavors of fusion
FlavorHowWin
Hand-written fused kernelsCustom CUDA — fuse a known op pattern by hand (FlashAttention, Marlin, fused RoPE+attention, fused LayerNorm+residual)Largest possible speedup on the targeted op; requires significant engineering per kernel.
Compiler fusion (torch.compile, Triton)Trace the model, identify fusible op patterns, emit a single generated kernel automatically.Broad but conservative — captures simple element-wise + reduction fusions without manual work; doesn't beat hand-tuned for complex ops.
Engine-level CUDA GraphsNot fusion strictly, but a sibling — replays a pre-recorded sequence of kernel launches with no host overhead.Eliminates launch overhead but doesn't reduce HBM traffic.
torch.compile in production

PyTorch 2.x introduced torch.compile, which traces a model's forward pass and uses a Triton-based backend to generate fused kernels for the parts it can. For LLM serving its biggest impact is fusing the small ops around attention (LayerNorm, RoPE, residuals) into the attention kernel proper — a 10–25% decode speedup without rewriting anything by hand. vLLM and SGLang both use torch.compile selectively; TRT-LLM does its own equivalent at engine-build time. The first run after a compile is slow (compilation takes seconds to minutes); subsequent runs use the cache.

When fusion fails or hurts
  • Shape changes invalidate compiled kernels. A new batch size or sequence length triggers a recompile. Engines manage this by capturing graphs for a finite set of "warmup" shapes and falling back to eager mode for the rest.
  • Fused kernels are harder to profile. When a 5-op fused kernel is the hot spot, you can't tell which constituent op is the actual bottleneck without disabling fusion.
  • Numerical drift. Fused kernels often re-order operations for performance; FP precision can differ from the eager-mode reference. For evals expecting bit-exact output, that's a problem.
📖 torch.compile docsTriton — the language behind compiler-fused kernels
← Numerical LibrariesModel File Formats →
Software Stack › Model File Formats

Model file formats new

safetensors, ONNX, GGUF, and why one of them is the universal LLM serving default

The four formats you'll see
FormatMaintained byWhat it storesUsed for
safetensorsHugging FaceJust weights. A simple header (JSON) + raw tensor data, memory-mapped on load.The de facto LLM serving format. vLLM, SGLang, TRT-LLM all consume it natively.
PyTorch .pt / .binPyTorchPickled Python objects (potentially weights + optimizer state + arbitrary code).Training output, legacy. Security risk — pickle can execute arbitrary code on load.
ONNXLinux FoundationWeights + the full execution graph in a portable serialization.Cross-framework deployment, ONNX Runtime, some TensorRT paths.
GGUFllama.cppWeights + metadata + in-file quantization (mixed-precision K-quants).CPU and edge inference (Ollama, llama.cpp, LM Studio). Not used in GPU production.
Why safetensors won for GPU serving

Three reasons:

  • Memory-mapped loading. The OS maps the file directly into virtual memory; the engine can lazily fault in only the tensors it needs, in the order it needs them. A 140 GB Llama 70B checkpoint can begin serving before the full file has even been read.
  • Sharded loading. Large models ship as N shards (e.g., 30 × 5 GB shards for a 150 GB model). Each TP rank reads only the shards covering its slice, in parallel. Cuts cold-start I/O time by N.
  • No code execution. The header is JSON; the tensors are raw bytes. Compare to .pt, where loading a checkpoint can run arbitrary Python — a real attack vector for shared model registries.
ONNX — when it shows up

ONNX stores the model's execution graph, not just weights. Useful when you need to deploy across frameworks (PyTorch trained, served on ONNX Runtime), or when a kernel-fusing compiler (TensorRT) wants to consume the graph and generate optimized engines. The catch: novel architectures often don't export cleanly. DeepSeek V3's MLA was famously hard to export to ONNX because the standard ops didn't cover the latent compression, and engine teams had to hand-write the equivalent. ONNX is a better fit for stable, conventional architectures than for the cutting edge.

The release-day file-format dance

When a frontier model launches:

  1. Lab releases safetensors + tokenizer + config.json on Hugging Face.
  2. vLLM/SGLang/TRT-LLM open day-zero PRs adding architecture support (config parsing, tensor mapping, attention kernel choice).
  3. Quantization toolchains (AutoAWQ, llm-compressor, ModelOpt) re-quantize and re-publish FP8/INT4 variants.
  4. llama.cpp converts to GGUF for CPU/edge.
  5. NVIDIA publishes a NIM (containerized pre-quantized + pre-compiled engine) for production.

The whole pipeline now happens inside hours of a release, not weeks. safetensors is the format that makes this speed possible — a single canonical source of truth that every downstream tool consumes the same way.

📖 safetensors specONNXGGUF spec
