Software Stack › NVIDIA Dynamo Orchestration

NVIDIA Dynamo — orchestration above the engine new

Why a layer above vLLM/SGLang/TRT-LLM exists, and what it does that no single engine can

What Dynamo is, and what it isn't

Dynamo is not another inference engine. It sits one layer above engines and orchestrates them. A single Dynamo deployment can have vLLM workers, SGLang workers, and TRT-LLM workers under the same control plane, doing different jobs.

LayerOwnsExamples
Inference enginePer-replica scheduling, KV cache, batching, kernel selectionvLLM, SGLang, TRT-LLM
Dynamo (orchestration)Cross-replica routing, prefill/decode disaggregation, KV-aware load balancing, multi-node parallelism, SLA-based capacity planningNVIDIA Dynamo
Cluster schedulerPod placement, autoscaling, GPU procurementKubernetes (with Dynamo as a controller)
Four things Dynamo does that no single engine can
  • Disaggregated prefill/decode across the cluster. Routes prefill to a high-TFLOPS pool and decode to a high-bandwidth pool, transfers KV cache between them via NIXL-based RDMA, with the transpose kernel handling differing TP between fleets (see Disaggregation section).
  • KV cache as a global resource. The KV Block Manager (KVBM) treats GPU HBM (G1), host RAM (G2), local SSD (G3), and networked storage (G4) as one pool with cache-aware routing — so any GPU in the cluster can resume any session.
  • SLA-based planner. Instead of scaling on raw utilization, Dynamo's planner takes TTFT/TPS targets and decides how many prefill and decode replicas to run, of which engine type, with what TP/EP/PP plan. Capacity planning becomes a target function, not a manual ratio.
  • Cross-engine routing. A short-prompt request can go to a vLLM replica with aggressive prefix caching; a long-context request can go to a TRT-LLM replica with a compiled engine; an MoE request can go to an SGLang replica with EP enabled. The right engine for each request, in one cluster.
Why this layer didn't exist before

For a long time the orchestration concerns above were either folded into the engine itself (vLLM's production stack) or left to whatever you wrote on top of Kubernetes. The 2024–2025 wave of disaggregation, multi-tier KV, and multi-engine deployments made these concerns too cross-cutting to live inside any one engine. Dynamo (and similar orchestrators emerging from other vendors) is the response. If you find yourself building per-tenant KV-aware routing, prefill/decode autoscaling, and SLA-driven capacity planning by hand, that's the signal you've outgrown a single engine and need this layer.

📖 NVIDIA Dynamo on GitHub
← Model File FormatsProfiling Tools →
Software Stack › Profiling Tools

Profiling tools new

Nsight Systems, Nsight Compute, PyTorch Profiler — picking the right tool for "why is this slow?"

Three tools, three altitudes
ToolAltitudeWhat it showsWhen to reach for it
PyTorch ProfilerFramework-levelTime spent per Python op (attention, FFN, layer norm), CUDA stream activity, memory allocation timeline"Where is my engine spending time at the op level?"
Nsight Systems (nsys)System-wide timelineCPU threads, CUDA streams, kernel launches, NCCL collective ops, NVLink/PCIe traffic — all on one timeline"Why is there a gap?" / "Is the GPU starved waiting for the CPU?" / "Are my collectives overlapping with compute?"
Nsight Compute (ncu)Per-kernel deep diveOne kernel's SM occupancy, register pressure, memory bandwidth utilization, Tensor Core throughput, stall reasons"This specific kernel is the bottleneck — why?"
The standard escalation path
  1. Start with PyTorch Profiler to identify which op family dominates. If 70% of time is in attention, that's where to dig.
  2. Drop to Nsight Systems if the issue is "the engine is slow but no single op stands out." Look for gaps between kernels (host-side overhead → CUDA Graphs), uneven stream utilization (load imbalance), or collective ops blocking compute (poor TP overlap).
  3. Pull out Nsight Compute only when you've isolated a specific kernel and need to know whether it's compute-bound, memory-bound, or starved on instruction issue. This is where roofline analysis happens — ncu reports arithmetic intensity, achieved bandwidth, and how close you are to the SOL.
What "the GPU is at 100% utilization" actually means

nvidia-smi's GPU-Util column reports "any kernel ran during the last sampling interval" — it's binary, not throughput. A GPU running one tiny kernel reports 100%. The metric you actually want for inference is SM activity or memory bandwidth utilization, both available from Nsight Systems / DCGM. The number of production incidents that started with "but nvidia-smi says 100%!" is uncomfortably large.

DCGM for production telemetry

Nsight tools are interactive and offline. DCGM (Data Center GPU Manager) is the production telemetry equivalent — exports metrics like SM occupancy, HBM bandwidth, NVLink traffic, and tensor-core utilization to Prometheus/Grafana. A reasonable production observability stack runs DCGM continuously and reaches for Nsight only when DCGM flags an anomaly.

📖 Nsight SystemsNsight ComputeDCGM
← NVIDIA Dynamo OrchestrationChapter 7 Quiz →
Software Stack › Quiz

Chapter 7 Quiz — Software Stack

Test your understanding of CUDA, GEMM, fusion, file formats, Dynamo orchestration, and profiling

Chapter 7 Quiz — Software Stack
1 Why are CUDA Graphs a meaningful decode-step optimization but not a meaningful prefill optimization?
A decode step launches dozens of small kernels (attention sub-ops, FFN, norms) totaling ~25–50% of the step in pure host-side launch overhead. CUDA Graphs replay that whole sequence in one dispatch, eliminating most of that overhead. Prefill, by contrast, is a single big GEMM-heavy forward pass — kernel launches are tiny relative to the work, so eliminating them recovers very little. CUDA Graphs are an optimization for many small kernels, which is exactly decode's profile.
2 An engine team replaces cuBLAS with DeepGEMM for FP8 GEMMs on H100. Why might this be a 30%+ speedup despite cuBLAS being NVIDIA's flagship library?
cuBLAS is general — it must serve every shape, precision, and access pattern. DeepGEMM is specialized: just FP8, just Hopper, just the GEMM shapes DeepSeek's models actually use. Specialization lets it pick instruction sequences and tile sizes that cuBLAS's heuristics would never choose because they wouldn't generalize. The win comes from narrowness, not better engineering. The same pattern is why Marlin beat cuBLAS on INT4 weight-only GEMMs and why FlashAttention beat cuDNN on attention.
3 Why is safetensors safer than PyTorch .pt files for shared model registries?
.pt uses Python pickle, which can execute arbitrary code on load — a malicious checkpoint can run shell commands the moment torch.load() hits it. safetensors stores weights as raw bytes with a JSON header; loading is purely deserialization with no code execution surface. For Hugging Face Hub-style public model sharing, that difference is the whole reason safetensors became the default.
4 Your model serves at "100% GPU utilization" per nvidia-smi but TPS is half what theory predicts. What's most likely going on, and which tool tells you?
nvidia-smi's GPU-Util is binary ("any kernel ran during the sampling window") — it reports 100% even if the kernels were tiny. The actual issue is usually low SM occupancy or low memory bandwidth utilization. Nsight Systems gives the timeline view (gaps between kernels, stream activity); Nsight Compute on the suspect kernel reports occupancy and achieved bandwidth. In production, DCGM exports the same metrics continuously to Prometheus.
5 A request arrives needing 50 ms of prefill and 5 seconds of decode. Why might NVIDIA Dynamo route the prefill and decode to different replicas — possibly running different engines?
Prefill is compute-bound (wants high TFLOPS, high TP); decode is bandwidth-bound (wants high HBM bandwidth, possibly different TP). Dynamo's disaggregated routing sends prefill to a fleet optimized for compute and decode to a fleet optimized for bandwidth, transferring KV cache between them via RDMA. The two fleets can even run different engines (e.g., TRT-LLM compiled engine for prefill, vLLM for decode) — Dynamo treats each fleet as a pool and picks the right one per request. This is the orchestration layer's job; no single engine does it alone.
← Profiling ToolsGPU Internals →
Hardware Depth › GPU Internals

GPU internals — what's actually inside the chip new

SMs, CUDA cores, Tensor Cores, SFUs, and the MMA op that powers every matmul

The basic units
UnitCount on H100What it does
SM (Streaming Multiprocessor)132 (some idle for yield)The basic execution unit. One thread block from a kernel launch runs on one SM. Each SM contains its own scheduler, registers, shared memory, and execution units.
CUDA Cores128 per SM (16,896 total)Scalar FP32/INT32 ALUs. Handle non-matmul math: activation functions, layer norm, RoPE, residual additions.
Tensor Cores4 per SM (528 total) — 4th-gen on HopperMatrix-multiply-accumulate (MMA) units. Each one performs D = A · B + C on small tile shapes per cycle. ~95% of LLM inference compute happens here.
SFUs (Special Function Units)4 per SMHardware-accelerated transcendentals — sin, cos, exp, log, reciprocal, square root. Critical for softmax and activations.
The MMA op — what a Tensor Core actually computes

A Tensor Core executes a single matrix-multiply-accumulate instruction:

D = A · B + C

where A, B, C, D are small fixed-shape tiles. On Hopper FP16, one Tensor Core executes a 16×16×16 MMA per cycle — that's 4096 multiply-adds (8192 FLOPs) every clock tick from one Tensor Core. With 528 Tensor Cores at 1.83 GHz, the H100 hits ~989 TFLOPS theoretical peak in FP16. The whole game of GEMM kernel optimization is feeding these MMAs without stalls.

Newer generations introduce wider/denser MMAs: Hopper added wgmma (warp-group MMA, 64×64×16 in one op), Blackwell adds 5th-gen Tensor Cores supporting FP4/MXFP4/NVFP4 natively at 2× the throughput of FP8.

Sparse Tensor Cores (2:4 structured sparsity)

Modern Tensor Cores can exploit a specific sparsity pattern: 2:4 structured sparsity — in every group of 4 weights, exactly 2 must be zero. With this constraint baked in, the Tensor Core skips half the multiplies, doubling effective throughput. The constraint is strict: random ~50% sparsity does not qualify. Models trained with 2:4 awareness achieve essentially full quality. The advertised "TF32 with sparsity" or "FP8 with sparsity" peak FLOPS in NVIDIA spec sheets are this 2× figure; without sparsity-aware training, you get the dense numbers.

SM occupancy and why it matters

Each SM has finite resources: registers, shared memory, threads-per-block. A kernel that uses too many registers per thread reduces the number of concurrent thread blocks an SM can host (lower occupancy), which means fewer warps available to hide memory latency. Higher occupancy ≠ better performance automatically — but very low occupancy almost always means you're leaving performance on the table. Nsight Compute reports occupancy as a primary metric.

📖 CUDA Hardware ImplementationHopper architecture deep dive
← Chapter 7 QuizCache Hierarchy →
Hardware Depth › Cache Hierarchy

The GPU memory and cache hierarchy new

Registers, L1/SRAM, L2, HBM — bandwidths, sizes, and what optimization techniques each tier enables

The hierarchy on H100
TierSizeBandwidth (approx)ScopeWhat lives here
Registers~256 KB per SMcycle-rate (TBs/s)Per-threadCurrently active values inside a kernel
L1 / Shared memory (SRAM)256 KB per SM (configurable split)~20 TB/sPer-blockTiles staged for Tensor Core MMAs (FlashAttention works here)
L2 cache50 MB (chip-wide)~7 TB/sAll SMsActive KV cache pages, working tensors
HBM380 GB3.35 TB/sFull GPUModel weights, full KV cache, large activations
Host RAM (over PCIe 5.0)TBs~64 GB/sSystemKV cache spillover (G2 in the 4-tier hierarchy)
Each tier enables a specific optimization
  • SRAM tiling → FlashAttention. Keeps the N×N attention score matrix in SRAM tiles so it never touches HBM. Without on-chip SRAM, FlashAttention would not exist.
  • L2 cache → KV cache reuse and continuous batching efficiency. Active sequences' KV pages stay warm in L2 between decode steps; cold sequences fall back to HBM. PagedAttention's block layout is partly tuned to keep recently used blocks L2-resident.
  • HBM bandwidth → the cap on decode throughput. Every decode step loads the full model weights from HBM. A 70B FP16 model = 140 GB; at 3.35 TB/s that's ~42 ms of pure HBM read time per decode step. This is the floor — no kernel optimization gets you below it without reducing model size or increasing batch size.
  • PCIe / host RAM → swap-based preemption, KV offload to G2. Anything that crosses this boundary pays a 50× bandwidth penalty vs HBM.
Why H200 is "the same H100 with more bandwidth"

H200 has identical compute (989 TFLOPS FP16) to H100. The difference: HBM3e instead of HBM3, raising bandwidth from 3.35 TB/s to ~4.8 TB/s, and capacity from 80 GB to 141 GB. For decode-bound workloads (the common case), H200 is ~40% faster per GPU than H100 — entirely from feeding the same Tensor Cores faster. This is the cache hierarchy in action: when you're already memory-bound, more bandwidth is more performance, full stop.

📖 H100 / H200 datasheets
← GPU InternalsArchitecture Roadmap →
Hardware Depth › Architecture Roadmap

NVIDIA architecture roadmap new

Ampere → Hopper → Blackwell → Rubin → Feynman, and what each generation actually changed

The generations that matter for inference
GenerationYearFlagshipWhat it added that matters for inference
Turing2018T4First-gen Tensor Cores. Still common as a cheap inference instance.
Ampere2020A1003rd-gen Tensor Cores; BF16 native; structured sparsity; NVLink 3.
Ada Lovelace2022L40, L4FP8 Tensor Cores; graphics-oriented, no NVLink. PCIe-only — unsuitable for TP across cards.
Hopper2022H100, H2004th-gen Tensor Cores; FP8 native; thread-block clusters; async copy; distributed shared memory; FlashAttention 3 hardware support.
Blackwell2024B100, B200, GB2005th-gen Tensor Cores; FP4 / MXFP4 / NVFP4 native; FlashAttention 4 hardware support; chip-to-chip NVLink-C2C; transformer engine 2.
Rubin2026R-series + Vera CPUHBM4; explicit prefill chip ("CPX") for disaggregated serving; Vera ARM CPU on-package.
Feynman~2028 (announced)F-seriesNext-gen architecture details still under wraps.
The recurring pattern — each generation adds a precision and changes attention

If you squint, every generation since Ampere has done two things:

  1. Add a smaller precision with native Tensor Core support. Ampere added BF16. Hopper added FP8. Blackwell added FP4 and microscaling formats. Each step roughly doubles raw TFLOPS at the new precision relative to the previous one. Quantization research follows hardware: FP8 only became a serving default once H100s shipped; MXFP4/NVFP4 only matters once you have Blackwells.
  2. Add hardware support for the next FlashAttention. FA3 needed Hopper's async copy and warpgroup-MMA. FA4 needs Blackwell's tensor memory accelerator. Each FlashAttention generation lands ~6 months after the GPU ships and unlocks longer contexts at lower cost.
When does generation matter operationally?
  • Capacity planning. An H100 fleet has different economics from a B200 fleet — different precisions, different per-GPU TFLOPS, different memory. Reservations made at one generation expire and must be re-evaluated.
  • Engine support lag. A new generation usually gets partial engine support at launch; advanced features (FP4, async tile, FA4) trail by 3–9 months. New gen ≠ instantly usable in production.
  • Model-architecture co-design. Frontier model labs increasingly target specific generations (DeepSeek V3 was designed around H100 FP8, FLUX assumes Blackwell for video, GPT-OSS ships in MXFP4 because Blackwell exists). Running a Blackwell-targeted model on Hopper means leaving real performance on the table.
📖 Blackwell architecture overviewRubin announcement
← Cache HierarchySuperchips & Racks →
Hardware Depth › Superchips & Racks

Superchips and rack-scale systems new

Grace, GB200, NVL72, NVL144 — when the unit of capacity is a rack, not a server

From server to rack as the unit of deployment

For a decade the standard inference unit was a single 8-GPU node: 8 GPUs connected by NVLink within the box, InfiniBand across boxes. Frontier models are starting to outgrow this. A 405B-parameter dense model in BF16 needs ~800 GB of HBM; a multi-tenant production deployment of an MoE model wants 16+ experts with EP ≥ 16. Both push beyond what an 8-GPU node can host with low-latency interconnect.

NVIDIA's response: package multiple nodes into a single NVLink-coherent rack, with all GPUs reachable over NVLink instead of InfiniBand.

The current and upcoming rack systems
SystemGPUsCPUsTotal HBMWhat it enables
GH200 superchip1 H200 + 1 Grace CPU1 Grace141 GB HBM3e + 480 GB LPDDR5XNVLink-C2C 900 GB/s between GPU and CPU — KV cache spill to host RAM at near-HBM bandwidth.
GB200 superchip2 B200 + 1 Grace CPU1 Grace~384 GB HBM3eThe Blackwell building block. Two GPUs and one CPU on one board with NVLink-C2C.
NVL72 rack72 B200 (36 GB200 boards)36 Grace~13.5 TBAll 72 GPUs in one NVLink domain at 1.8 TB/s. TP up to 72-way; EP across 72 experts with no InfiniBand hop.
Vera Rubin NVL144144 R-seriesVera CPUs~30+ TBDoubles the NVLink domain; introduces dedicated prefill chips ("CPX") for disaggregated serving.
Why this changes serving topology

Inside an NVL72, the all-reduce that used to be "intra-node NVLink fast vs inter-node InfiniBand slow" becomes "all-fast-everywhere, up to 72 GPUs." Concretely:

  • TP scales further. An 8-way TP cap in a single node becomes practical at TP16/TP32/TP72 inside an NVL72 rack. Latency per token drops proportionally.
  • EP across many experts becomes affordable. Mixed TP+EP for MoE models can run EP72 inside the rack with all-to-all over NVLink instead of InfiniBand.
  • Disaggregation stays inside the rack. Prefill→decode KV transfer over NVLink at 1.8 TB/s instead of InfiniBand at 50 GB/s — a 36× speedup on the KV-transfer hop, eliminating disaggregation's biggest tax.
The Grace / Vera CPU pieces

Grace (current, ARM-based) and Vera (next-gen, ARM-based) are not generic CPUs — they're specifically designed to sit next to the GPU with a 900 GB/s coherent interconnect. The point isn't CPU speed; it's that host RAM becomes a usable spillover tier for KV cache (G2 in the 4-tier hierarchy) and LoRA adapters at near-HBM bandwidth. Without NVLink-C2C, host RAM was 50× slower than HBM and hence rarely touched on the hot path. With it, multi-TB G2 caches become serving-relevant.

📖 GB200 NVL72Vera Rubin NVL144
← Architecture RoadmapMIG Slicing →
Hardware Depth › MIG Slicing

MIG — Multi-Instance GPU new

Carving one physical GPU into smaller hardware-isolated GPUs

The problem MIG solves

An H100 has 132 SMs and 80 GB of HBM. For a 7B model in INT4, that's overkill — the model fits in 4 GB and uses a fraction of the SMs. But you can't just run multiple processes on one GPU and expect predictable latency: they share L2, HBM, scheduler, and noisy-neighbor effects are real. MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) partitions the GPU at the hardware level into up to 7 isolated instances, each with its own slice of SMs, L2, and HBM, with hardware-enforced QoS.

How the partitioning works
Profile (H100)SMsHBMUse
1g.10gb~1410 GBSmallest slice — embedding models, small classifiers
2g.20gb~2820 GBQuantized 7B models, Whisper
3g.40gb~4240 GBMid-size models
7g.80gb (full GPU)13280 GBOne job uses the whole device — large LLMs

You configure MIG profiles via nvidia-smi mig at the driver level. Each instance appears to userspace as a separate CUDA device. Kubernetes-level GPU operators (NVIDIA's K8s device plugin, GPU Operator) advertise MIG instances as schedulable resources.

When MIG is the right answer
  • Multi-tenant small-model serving. Seven tenants each running a quantized 7B model on a shared H100 — MIG gives each tenant a real GPU's worth of isolation without dedicating a full chip.
  • Live ASR / specialized models with predictable per-instance load that doesn't need the full chip.
  • Dev/test/CI shared pools where you want isolation but not dedicated hardware per developer.
When MIG is the wrong answer
  • Frontier LLMs. Anything over ~30B parameters wants the full GPU; MIG just reduces what's available.
  • Tensor parallelism. MIG instances cannot communicate over NVLink; TP across MIG slices is not a thing. If you need TP, you need the whole GPU.
  • Bursty load. MIG slices are statically sized — you can't temporarily borrow capacity from an idle neighbor. Continuous batching across the whole GPU usually wins on bursty workloads.
📖 MIG User Guide
← Superchips & RacksNon-NVIDIA Accelerators →
Hardware Depth › Non-NVIDIA Accelerators

Non-NVIDIA accelerators new

TPU, Trainium, Groq, Cerebras, Etched, AMD — what each is good at and where the LLM serving market actually uses them

The landscape
AcceleratorVendorMemory modelWhere it's used in LLM serving
Google TPU (v4, v5e, v5p, Trillium)GoogleHBM, 2D torus interconnectInternal Google (Gemini), GCP-hosted JAX/MaxText workloads, some open-source via OpenXLA. Not common outside Google's ecosystem.
AWS Inferentia / TrainiumAWSHBM, NeuronLinkAWS-hosted inference (Bedrock, SageMaker NeuronCore). Strong cost efficiency for AWS-native customers; weaker open-source ecosystem than CUDA.
Groq LPUGroqSRAM-only (no HBM!)Hosted inference API. Very low latency on small-medium models because everything fits in SRAM; capacity-constrained for big models.
Cerebras WSE-3CerebrasWafer-scale SRAMHosted inference API. Extremely fast TPS on a single replica; few production users compared to GPU clouds.
Etched SohuEtchedTransformer-fixed ASICBet on transformer architecture being stable. Inference-only. Early-stage; not yet in mainstream production.
AMD Instinct MI300X / MI350XAMDHBM3, Infinity FabricProduction GPU alternative. Comparable HBM/TFLOPS to H100/H200; ROCm software stack catching up to CUDA. vLLM and SGLang both have AMD backends.
Qualcomm Cloud AI 100QualcommOn-chip + DDRNiche; cost-optimized inference for specific workloads.
SambaNova RDUSambaNova3-tier memoryHosted inference; small market share.
Why CUDA still dominates

The hardware landscape is more diverse than the software landscape. NVIDIA has ~85%+ of training and inference deployment despite many of these alternatives being competitive on raw silicon. The reasons are stack-deep:

  • Kernels. Years of tuned libraries (cuBLAS, cuDNN, CUTLASS, FlashAttention, DeepGEMM) targeting CUDA. Other vendors are still porting equivalents.
  • Engines. vLLM, SGLang, TRT-LLM all started CUDA-first. AMD ROCm has the strongest non-CUDA support; others are partial.
  • Models. Frontier model labs train and tune on H100/H200/B200. Day-zero engine support for new model architectures is a CUDA story.
  • Talent. The pool of engineers who can hand-write a fast attention kernel on CUDA is ~10× larger than for any other platform.
Where the alternatives are real today

If you're an AWS-native shop with cost pressure, Inferentia/Trainium have real economics for stable workloads. If you're already inside Google, TPUs are the obvious choice. AMD MI300X is the most credible drop-in alternative for H100/H200-class workloads — same engines, similar TFLOPS, often better $/TFLOP, ROCm gap narrowing every quarter. Groq and Cerebras win on specific latency-sensitive APIs. The rest are niche.

📖 AWS TrainiumAMD InstinctGroq
← MIG SlicingPCIe vs SXM →
Hardware Depth › PCIe vs SXM

PCIe vs SXM form factors new

Same chip, different package — and why the form factor sometimes matters more than the chip

The two packages
PCIeSXM
Physical formStandard PCIe card; slots into a server backplaneNVIDIA's mezzanine form factor; mounts directly to a baseboard
Power budget~300–400 W (limited by PCIe slot power)700+ W (custom power delivery)
Memory bandwidthSlightly lower (e.g., H100 PCIe = 2 TB/s vs SXM = 3.35 TB/s)Full advertised bandwidth
NVLinkOptional bridge between adjacent cards (~600 GB/s, 2-card pairs only)NVSwitch fabric — all 8 GPUs in the box reach each other at 900 GB/s
CoolingAir-cooled in standard serversLiquid or high-CFM air; requires purpose-built chassis
CostLower per GPU; cheaper total systemHigher per GPU; only available in HGX/DGX-class systems
Why this matters for inference

For LLM serving at scale, the SXM advantages compound:

  • Tensor parallelism needs NVLink. 8-way TP across an SXM node is normal; 8-way TP across PCIe cards (without a full NVSwitch) chokes on interconnect bandwidth.
  • HBM bandwidth gap moves the roofline. A 40% bandwidth difference (2 TB/s vs 3.35 TB/s on H100) translates almost 1-for-1 to decode throughput on memory-bound workloads.
  • Power headroom enables sustained boost. SXM cards can hold higher clocks longer under load; PCIe cards throttle sooner under sustained inference traffic.

For batch inference, embedding workloads, or single-GPU serving of small models, PCIe is fine and cheaper. For frontier-model serving with TP/EP, SXM is effectively required. Cloud SKUs reflect this: H100 PCIe instances (AWS p5.48xlarge variants, GCP A3) coexist with H100 SXM instances (AWS p5e, DGX) at very different prices.

L4, L40, L40S — the PCIe-only Ada line

The Ada Lovelace generation (L4, L40, L40S) is PCIe-only. No NVLink, no SXM. NVIDIA's positioning is graphics + light inference; in practice, these cards are a real option for embedding services, small-model serving, and single-GPU CV workloads — but they fundamentally cannot do tensor-parallel large-model serving. Buying L40s and trying to run TP4 on Llama 70B is a common, expensive mistake.

📖 H100 PCIe vs SXM specs
← Non-NVIDIA AcceleratorsChapter 8 Quiz →
Hardware Depth › Quiz

Chapter 8 Quiz — Hardware Depth

Test your understanding of GPU internals, cache hierarchy, generations, racks, MIG, and accelerators

Chapter 8 Quiz — Hardware Depth
1 H200 has the same TFLOPS as H100 but is ~40% faster on decode. Why?
Decode is memory-bandwidth-bound, not compute-bound (see roofline section). H200 has the same Tensor Cores as H100, so peak TFLOPS are identical. But its HBM3e gives 4.8 TB/s vs H100's 3.35 TB/s — roughly 40% more bandwidth. Every decode step reads the full model weights from HBM, so 40% more bandwidth ≈ 40% more decode throughput. The Tensor Cores were already idle waiting for data; feeding them faster recovers what was lost to bandwidth-boundedness.
2 An NVL72 rack has 72 B200 GPUs all reachable via NVLink at 1.8 TB/s. What changes operationally compared to nine standard 8-GPU nodes connected by InfiniBand?
The NVLink-only domain expands from 8 to 72 GPUs. Concretely: TP scales beyond 8-way (TP72 inside the rack becomes practical, dropping per-token latency); EP across MoE experts can use NVLink for all-to-all (no InfiniBand hop); prefill/decode disaggregation can transfer KV between fleets at 1.8 TB/s instead of 50 GB/s — a 36× speedup on the KV-transfer hop. Workloads that were "too tightly coupled to span nodes" become single-rack jobs.
3 Why does 2:4 structured sparsity double Tensor Core throughput when random 50% sparsity does not?
Tensor Cores have hardware support for one specific sparsity pattern: in every group of 4 weights, exactly 2 are zero, encoded with metadata indicating which two. The MMA unit reads only the non-zero weights, halving the work per output. Random 50% sparsity has no such structure — the hardware has no way to know which weights to skip without checking each one, which negates any savings. Models trained with 2:4 awareness from the start match dense quality; bolting it on post-hoc usually doesn't.
4 Your team buys L40S cards for "Llama 70B serving" because they're cheaper per GPU than H100. Why is this a mistake?
L40S is Ada Lovelace generation — PCIe-only, no NVLink. Llama 70B in BF16 is 140 GB; it doesn't fit on one card and needs TP across multiple GPUs. TP requires per-layer all-reduce at NVLink bandwidth (900 GB/s); over PCIe (~64 GB/s) the all-reduces dominate decode step time and you lose the entire parallelism benefit. L40S is fine for embedding services, single-GPU 7–13B serving, and CV — not for tensor-parallel large LLMs. The cost saving disappears the moment you need TP.
5 A multi-tenant SaaS runs seven different small fine-tuned 7B models, each with its own customer. How would MIG help, and what would it not help?
Helps: partition one H100 into 7 × 1g.10gb instances; each tenant gets a hardware-isolated slice with predictable latency, immune to noisy-neighbor effects in L2 or HBM. Effectively turns one $30K GPU into 7 independent serving environments. Does not help: if any tenant's load spikes, MIG can't borrow capacity from idle neighbors — slices are statically sized. And if one of the 7B models is large enough to need TP, MIG is wrong because TP across MIG slices isn't possible. For bursty or growing workloads, multi-LoRA on the full GPU usually beats MIG.
← PCIe vs SXMContainers & Cold Starts →
Production › Containers & Cold Starts

Containers and cold starts new

Why a "new replica" takes 1–5 minutes to start serving, and what each minute is doing

What's in an inference container

An LLM serving container is unusually large — easily 10–30 GB before the model weights:

  • Base image (CUDA + cuDNN + the right driver version) — usually pinned to a specific NVIDIA-published tag like nvidia/cuda:12.4.0-devel-ubuntu22.04.
  • Engine binaries — vLLM, SGLang, or TRT-LLM with their compiled kernels.
  • Python deps — torch, transformers, the engine's pinned versions of everything.
  • System packages — ffmpeg for audio paths, networking utilities for RDMA, sometimes the InfiniBand userspace stack.
  • Tokenizer + config — small (~MBs) but the model loader needs them.

Model weights typically aren't in the image — they're mounted from object storage at startup. A 140 GB Llama-70B checkpoint inside the image would make the container unwieldy and force you to rebuild on every weight update.

The four phases of a cold start
PhaseTypical durationDominant cost
1. GPU procurementseconds to minutesCloud scheduler placing a pod on a node with the right GPU type, optionally provisioning a new node
2. Image pull~10–60 s10–30 GB image fetched from registry; depends on registry locality and network
3. Weight load~10s of seconds for 70B; minutes for 405BStreaming safetensors from object storage into HBM
4. Engine warmup~5–60 s (TRT-LLM compile is the slowest)Capturing CUDA Graphs for each batch shape, autotuning kernels, optionally compiling a TRT engine

Aggregate: 1–5 minutes for a typical production replica. Long enough that "scale up when queue grows" usually triggers SLO violations before the new replica is ready.

Mitigations that actually work
  • Smaller images. Multi-stage builds, no dev tools in the runtime image, prune unused PyTorch parts. A well-built image can be 4–6 GB instead of 25.
  • In-region registry mirror. Pull from 123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com, not Docker Hub. Bandwidth jumps from ~100 MB/s to multi-GB/s.
  • In-datacenter weight cache. Pre-stage weights on a local NVMe cache (S3 Express, FSx, Mountpoint-S3) so all replicas in the cluster pull from the cache instead of object storage. Weight load time drops from minutes to ~10 s.
  • Cached compiled engines. TRT-LLM engines, torch.compile artifacts, and CUDA Graph captures are GPU-specific and shape-specific — but they can be cached. Some teams build per-(model, GPU type, batch shape) caches that turn warmup from 60 s into 5 s.
  • Warm pools. Keep N idle replicas already past warmup. Costs money but turns scale-up into "route traffic" instead of "boot pod."
NIM — pre-baked everything

NVIDIA Inference Microservices (NIM) are containers where stages 2–4 are mostly pre-done: pre-quantized weights, pre-compiled TRT-LLM engine, autotuned for the target GPU. Pull, run, serve — no compile, no quantization. Trade-off is reduced flexibility (you serve what NVIDIA shipped, not your custom checkpoint), but for standard model deployments it cuts cold start to under a minute and removes the engineering work of building all of the above yourself.

📖 NVIDIA NIMMountpoint-S3 for in-region weight cache
← Chapter 8 QuizCold Start Bottlenecks →
Production › Cold Start Bottlenecks

Cold start bottlenecks new

A bottleneck-by-bottleneck breakdown of where the 1–5 minutes actually go, and the most-used techniques to claw seconds back from each one

What "cold start" actually means

"Cold start" is shorthand for the wall-clock time between "a new replica is requested" and "that replica can serve its first token under SLO." For a typical 7B–70B model on a modern GPU, that interval is dominated by five stacked bottlenecks, each of which can be attacked independently:

  • GPU procurement. Cloud scheduler placing a pod on a node with a free GPU, optionally provisioning a brand-new node from the cloud capacity pool.
  • Container image pull. Fetching a 5–30 GB image from a registry, decompressing it, and unpacking the layers onto the node's filesystem.
  • Weight load. Streaming model weights (often hundreds of GB) from object storage through host memory and into HBM.
  • Engine warmup. CUDA context init, kernel-library JITs (FlashInfer, Triton, TVM-FFI, FlashAttention CUTE DSL), torch.compile, and CUDA graph capture.
  • Multi-model swap. When the replica must change which model is hot — e.g., per-tenant routing — without a full restart.

Three things are worth noticing before diving in. First: these stages are stacked, not parallel — every second saved earlier still pays. Second: the relative cost mix varies wildly with model size, image size, and instance type. A 7B vLLM image on a g4dn (T4) spends a different fraction of its budget in each phase than a 405B SGLang image on a B200. Third: the "lazy-load tax" is real — even after image pull "completes," many bytes only get faulted in when the engine first reads them, so the cost can leak across stages and look like engine warmup when it's really still image I/O.

A useful rule of thumb measured on production setups: a vanilla cold start is in the 3–10 minute range; a well-optimized one without snapshots is in the 30–90 second range; a snapshot-restore path is sub-10 seconds. The biggest single jump in that ladder is GPU memory snapshots.
Bottleneck 1 — GPU procurement

The replica can't start until it has a GPU to start on. Two sub-costs hide here. The first is scheduling latency: the K8s scheduler matching a pod to an existing node with a free GPU, which is usually milliseconds. The second is node provisioning: when no node has a free GPU, the cluster autoscaler (Karpenter, Cluster Autoscaler, or EKS Auto Mode) calls the cloud provider to spin up a fresh GPU instance. New-node procurement is typically 60–180 seconds, longer for constrained SKUs (H100 SXM, GB200) where you may queue against capacity pressure.

Most-used techniques:

  • Warm pools / standby nodes. Keep N idle GPU nodes pre-provisioned. Karpenter doesn't natively support warm pools, so the common workaround is a NodePool with a minNodes floor of small idle instances. You pay always-on capacity in exchange for cutting node-provision time to zero.
  • min-replicas ≥ 1. Force one fully-warm pod per critical path. This is the cheapest way to never pay procurement cost on the first request — the autoscaler only sees the second burst.
  • Capacity reservations. AWS Capacity Blocks for ML, GCP committed-use, Azure reserved instances. Doesn't reduce procurement latency, but eliminates Spot-style interruptions that would force re-procurement under load.
  • Pre-warmed AMIs. Bake the GPU driver, container runtime, and the most-used base image into the AMI so a fresh node is "ready to pull the inference image" within ~30 s of EC2 boot, instead of 90+ s of post-boot bootstrap.
Knative-style "scale to zero" doesn't reduce procurement cost — it just defers it to the first user. If your cold start is 4 minutes, scale-to-zero is unusable for any interactive workload. Pair it with a fast-restore primitive (snapshots, sleep mode) or skip it.
Bottleneck 2 — Container image pull

Modern LLM serving images are large and lazy-readable. A vLLM or SGLang image with CUDA, cuDNN, torch, flash-attn, xformers, and the engine itself easily lands in the 8–15 GB range; some TRT-LLM images push past 25 GB. The unpack step (gunzip + tar onto the node's gp3-style EBS volume) is usually more expensive than the network fetch — disk write throughput, not bandwidth, is the wall.

Worse, the engine doesn't read all those bytes at startup. Many imports are touched only on first request, so the cost of "image pull" leaks into "engine warmup" as page-faults pull blocks on demand. This lazy-load tax is what makes naive lazy-pull snapshotters (SOCI, Nydus, eStargz) underperform on inference workloads — vLLM imports a wide enough swath of its dependency tree at startup that lazy loaders save little.

Most-used techniques:

  • In-region registry mirror. Pull from your cloud's regional registry (ECR, Artifact Registry, ACR), not Docker Hub. Bandwidth jumps from ~100 MB/s to multi-GB/s. Single biggest one-line fix.
  • Slim multi-stage images. Strip dev tools, compilers, test deps, and unused PyTorch wheels. A well-built vLLM runtime image is 4–6 GB instead of 15+. Doesn't beat caching, but compounds with everything else.
  • EBS Fast Snapshot Restore (FSR). Pre-warm the gp3 blocks the image lives on so first-touch reads don't block on lazy hydration. Measured impact on a 9.5 GB vLLM image: cold start 588 s → 242 s (-59%). The trick is that FSR pre-warms the entire underlying volume, including blocks that will hold the unpacked image.
  • Pre-baked AMIs. Embed the inference image into a custom AMI via packer. Skip the pull entirely. Costs an AMI build per image change; pairs well with FSR.
  • NVMe instance store as image cache. g4dn / g6 / p4d come with local NVMe. Populate it from S3 once per node and serve image pulls from local 3–4 GB/s storage. Ephemeral, so you re-populate per node, but per-pod pulls are near-instant.
  • Lazy-pull snapshotters (Nydus, eStargz, SOCI). Mount the image as a FUSE filesystem and fetch chunks on first read. Wins for "wide-fanout, narrow-readset" images; loses for vLLM-style workloads that touch most of the dependency tree at startup. Reach for these only on multi-image / multi-model nodes.
  • Image streaming (Baseten BDN-style). Begin model load before image pull completes by overlapping the two stages. Not yet a standard primitive on EKS, but the pattern is on the production roadmap of every major inference platform.
Bottleneck 3 — Weight load (S3 → host → GPU)

For anything bigger than a 7B, weight load is the single biggest contributor to cold start. There are three sub-stages and any of them can be the wall:

  • S3 (or equivalent) → host disk. 100–700 MB/s on a single thread; multi-GB/s if you parallelize. A 140 GB Llama-70B checkpoint over a single connection is multiple minutes.
  • Host disk → host RAM. Bottlenecked by disk read throughput. gp3 caps around 1 GB/s tuned; NVMe instance store does 3–4 GB/s; FSR-warmed gp3 hits page cache at near-DRAM speed.
  • Host RAM → GPU HBM. Bound by PCIe generation. Gen3 (T4) is ~16 GB/s peak; Gen4 (A100) ~32 GB/s; Gen5 (H100/B200) ~64 GB/s. For a 14 GB 7B model, this leg alone is ~1–5 s depending on PCIe gen.

The default HuggingFace safetensors loader is serial across files, so on cold pods the engine often spends most of its weight-load budget waiting on a single stream. The fix is to parallelize all three legs aggressively.

Most-used techniques:

  • Run:AI Model Streamer (vLLM --load-format runai_streamer). Concurrent multi-stream reader that pulls safetensors shards from S3 or local disk into pinned host buffers, then async-copies to GPU. Reported ~2× faster weight load (Llama-3-8B on A10G: S3 → ready in ~23 s vs ~50 s with HF loader). One flag, vLLM-native, low risk. Highest-ROI Phase-5 lever for most teams.
  • CoreWeave Tensorizer. Custom serialization format with a streaming loader. Pre-2024 SOTA; today Run:AI Streamer is generally faster and doesn't require re-serialization.
  • NVMe instance store for the model cache. Mount local NVMe via initContainer, copy weights from S3 into it on first pod-on-node, point the engine's --download-dir at it. ~30–40 s shaved off when combined with Run:AI Streamer. Conflicts with FSR thinking — pick one.
  • In-cluster distributed weight cache (Fluid, Alluxio, JuiceFS, Baseten BDN). Read-only cache of weights spread across cluster nodes; new pods pull peer-to-peer instead of from S3. Alibaba reported a 17 GB model load 90 s → 20 s. Pays for itself once you have many replicas pulling the same model.
  • Tensor R-Fork (LMSYS, Dec 2025). GPU-Direct RDMA between a running and a new SGLang instance — the new pod's GPU pulls weights peer-to-peer from a warm pod's HBM. "Several minutes → mere seconds" for DeepSeek-R1. Requires RDMA fabric (InfiniBand or AWS EFA). Useless for first-pod-up; only helps scale-out from N to N+1.
  • Mountpoint-S3 / CSI drivers. Mount the bucket as a filesystem and let the loader stream. Works, but a naive single-stream mount is no faster than aws s3 cp — combine with Run:AI Streamer for parallelism.
Bottleneck 4 — Engine warmup

Once the weights are in HBM, the engine still isn't ready. There's a stack of one-time costs that all happen before the first token:

  • CUDA context creation — 1–3 s.
  • Library imports + module init — torch, transformers, the engine. 5–15 s on a slow disk.
  • torch.compile / torchinductor — fuses the forward pass into Triton kernels. 30–120 s the first time; cached on disk, so subsequent runs read from cache.
  • FlashInfer / FlashAttention / TVM-FFI JITs — separate kernel libraries with separate caches. Even with torch.compile cache warm, these run on first request and cost real seconds. Doubleword's SGLang dissection showed this is the bucket Round-1 inventories typically miss.
  • CUDA graph capture — vLLM and SGLang capture ~67 graphs covering decode batch shapes. ~10 s on T4-class, less on H100/B200. Skipping it costs 10–20% steady-state throughput forever, so almost no one skips.

Most-used techniques:

  • Persist all JIT/AOT caches. Mount ~/.cache/vllm/torch_compile_cache (and FlashInfer/Triton/TVM caches) on a PVC, bake into the AMI, or pull from S3 at startup. Tensorfuse measured 294 s → 82 s (-72%) by caching compile + weights together. The cache must match GPU + CUDA + PyTorch + engine version exactly — invalidate on any change.
  • Pin to a recent vLLM release. The Q1 2026 vLLM tracking shows cold-start as a first-class metric and adds caching paths for kernel libraries beyond torch.compile. v0.19 will under-represent achievable savings; ≥0.7.x is the floor for serious cold-start work.
  • vLLM compilation level knobs (-O0 … -O3). Trades compile time for runtime perf. -O0 is fastest start, slowest decode; -O1 = piecewise CUDA graphs only. Useful for one-shot batch jobs where you'll never amortize the compile.
  • Avoid --enforce-eager in steady state. Saves ~10 s now in exchange for 10–20% throughput loss forever — bad trade for production, fine for development.
  • Torch Memory Saver (TMS) daemon. Pre-allocates 2 MB transparent hugepages and pipelines cudaHostRegister with H2D copy instead of doing them serially. Reported 38 GB/s effective on PCIe Gen5. SGLang-integrated; vLLM integration is rougher. Bounded by PCIe gen — at Gen3 the ceiling is ~2.4 GB/s, so the win shrinks on T4-class hardware.
  • NVIDIA NIM. Pre-baked containers where stages 2–4 are mostly pre-done — quantized weights, compiled TRT-LLM engine, autotuned kernels. Cuts cold start to under a minute at the cost of using NVIDIA-shipped checkpoints rather than your own.
TRT-LLM's compile path is ~28 minutes per (model, config). Strict no for any iterative dev flow. The shipped engine plan loads fast (~10 s), but you eat the compile cost any time you change model, batch shape ranges, or quantization. Reconsider only when shipping a frozen model at fleet scale.
Bottleneck 5 — The "and now serve a different model" problem

Multi-tenant inference platforms hit a sixth bottleneck that doesn't fit the four-phase model: swapping which model is hot on a warm GPU. A full restart through bottlenecks 1–4 is unacceptable for tenant routing where the request itself selects the model. Two families of techniques target this directly, and they are the most active research area in 2025–2026 cold-start work.

vLLM Sleep Mode. Keep the vLLM process alive, free GPU memory by either offloading weights to CPU RAM (Level 1) or discarding them entirely (Level 2). On wake, reload from CPU/disk. Reported 18–200× faster than full restart, and 61–88% faster first inference than a true cold start. Drop-in for vLLM 0.19+. The right primary strategy for "warm process per node, hot-swap models per request" patterns.

GPU memory snapshots (Modal-style, CRIUgpu, vLLM RFC #34303). Boot vLLM once, run full warmup (compile, graph capture, weight load), then snapshot the entire CUDA process state — VRAM contents, kernel modules, captured graphs, host memory — to disk. On cold start, restore the snapshot instead of re-running init. Modal's published Qwen2.5 example: 45 s → 5 s (9×). Mistral 3 case study: 10×. CRIUgpu (CRIU 4.0 + NVIDIA's cuda-checkpoint userspace tool) is the open-source primitive that all of these stack on; it's now production at Modal and MemVerge, and Podman ships native support.

The trade-offs are real. Snapshot images are large (model weights × ~1.05). The technique is per-driver-version brittle — cuda-checkpoint requires display driver 570+, with 580+ for full device migration. NCCL communicators don't survive suspend, so multi-GPU restore needs reinit and tensor-parallel vLLM (TP > 1) is fragile today. Single-GPU is the safe path for now.

The vLLM RFC for native suspend/resume HTTP endpoints (#34303) was opened Feb 2026 and hasn't moved toward merge. Until it lands, you either pay Modal for the managed snapshot service or build the orchestration yourself on top of CRIUgpu. Both are credible paths in 2026; the choice is build-effort vs monthly bill.

A stack-up that actually works

Putting the levers together for a typical 7B vLLM workload on EBS-backed gp3, baseline ~600 s cold start:

  • In-region registry + slim image: ~600 → ~450 s.
  • + Pre-baked AMI + EBS FSR: ~450 → ~240 s. (Phase 4.1 measurement.)
  • + Run:AI Model Streamer: ~240 → ~200 s.
  • + Multi-compiler cache persistence (torch.compile + FlashInfer + Triton): ~200 → ~170 s.
  • + NVMe instance store for weights: ~170 → ~140 s.
  • + GPU memory snapshots (Modal or CRIUgpu): ~140 → ~5–10 s.

The order matters — early items pay for themselves no matter what comes next, while snapshots subsume most of the earlier work and turn the cold-start problem into "how big is the snapshot artifact and how fast can you read it from the snapshot store." Snapshots are the only technique that takes a 7B+ replica to truly sub-second from a fully cold state; everything else is shaving seconds off a multi-stage pipeline.

A useful triage rule: don't optimize anything below your dominant bottleneck. If you're spending 4 minutes in image pull because Docker Hub is throttling you, no amount of Run:AI Streamer or torch.compile caching matters. Measure each phase first, then attack the largest one.

Production › Autoscaling & Routing

Autoscaling and routing new

Kubernetes, the five autoscaling knobs, scale-to-zero, and why your load balancer needs to know about KV cache

The K8s control plane / worker plane split

A production LLM serving deployment on Kubernetes has the same shape as any other K8s workload: a control plane (API server, scheduler, controller manager) decides what should run where; worker nodes (with GPUs) run the actual pods. The GPU-specific pieces are the NVIDIA Device Plugin (advertises GPUs as schedulable resources), DCGM exporter (GPU metrics to Prometheus), and increasingly Dynamo or KAITO as a controller layer above plain HPA.

The five autoscaling knobs
KnobWhat it controlsCommon trap
min replicasFloor — the smallest fleet you'll ever runSet to 0 for dev environments, ≥1 for production (avoids cold start on first request)
max replicasCap — protects you from runaway scaling under attack or bugSetting it equal to your reservation means you can't burst; leave headroom
autoscaling windowHow long the metric must exceed threshold before scaling triggersToo short → flapping; too long → SLO violations during ramp
scale-down delayHow long to wait after load drops before removing replicasToo short → replicas churn during traffic dips; KV caches and warm engines die before they pay off
concurrency targetWhat metric counts as "loaded" (concurrent requests, queue depth, GPU utilization, TTFT)CPU utilization is wrong for GPU workloads; pick a queueing- or latency-based signal
What to scale on

For LLM serving specifically:

  • Queue depth or pending tokens — the queueing-theory signal that "we are about to violate SLO." Best leading indicator.
  • P95 TTFT or TBT — the SLO directly. Lagging indicator; you're already in trouble when this fires.
  • KV cache utilization — when the cache is full, new sequences can't be admitted regardless of compute headroom.
  • NOT raw GPU utilization — as discussed in the GPU internals chapter, nvidia-smi-style utilization is a binary signal that easily reports 100% on lightly-loaded systems.
Routing — KV-aware and LoRA-aware

Plain round-robin / least-connections load balancing is wrong for LLM serving. The scheduling chapter covers this in detail; the production-side recap:

  • KV-aware routing. Hash on prompt prefix; prefer the replica that already has it cached. Spill to other replicas above a utilization threshold.
  • LoRA-aware routing. Hash on adapter ID; prefer the replica with the adapter loaded. Cold adapters cost ~100 ms+ to fetch.
  • Session-aware routing. For multi-turn conversations with KV persistence, sticky-route on conversation ID.

Production routers (NVIDIA Dynamo, vLLM production stack, SGLang's sgl-router) implement these as a single decision: pick the replica that maximizes joint cache-hit score across prefix + adapter + session. Without this layer, multi-tenant LoRA serving and prefix-cached deployments collapse into pathological thrash.

Scale-to-zero — when it makes sense

Scale-to-zero (no replicas when there's no traffic) is appealing for dev environments, batch jobs, and business-hours-only agents. The cost: 1–5 minute cold start when traffic returns. Reasonable when:

  • The workload has long quiet periods (overnight, weekends).
  • The first user is OK waiting for warmup, or you can warm-up via a synthetic probe before real traffic hits.
  • The savings (you're paying $30+/hour per H100 idle) outweigh the latency cost.

For latency-critical APIs serving 24/7, scale-to-zero is wrong. Keep min replicas ≥ 1 and accept the floor cost.

📖 KAITO — K8s for LLM workloadsvLLM production deployment

Production › Multi-Cloud & Reliability

Multi-cloud capacity and reliability new

Bin packing across providers, neoclouds vs hyperscalers, and the failure rates you should actually plan for

Where GPU capacity comes from
SourceExamplesStrengthsWatch out for
HyperscalersAWS, GCP, Azure, OCIMature ecosystem, broad region coverage, full IAM/network/compliance integrationHighest $/GPU-hour; capacity constraints on flagship SKUs (H100 SXM, GB200) at peak demand
NeocloudsCoreWeave, Lambda, Nebius, Crusoe, Together, FireworksCheaper than hyperscalers, often have flagship GPUs available when AWS doesn't, GPU-specialist supportSmaller region footprint; less mature non-GPU tooling (networking, IAM); contract terms vary widely
Spot / preemptibleAWS Spot, GCP Preemptible, Lambda spot40–70% cheaper than on-demandCan be reclaimed with minutes' notice — only safe for batch, training, or stateless inference with fast restart
Resellers / brokersSF Compute, Foundry, sfcomputeAggregated capacity across many providers, shorter contracts, daily-spot-style pricingYou don't own the relationship with the underlying hardware operator; reliability varies
On-prem / coloYour own DGX or HGX clusterBest $/GPU at scale once you've absorbed the capex; full controlYou become a datacenter operator. Power, cooling, network, ops staff all on you.
Bin-packing across providers

At scale, no single provider has enough flagship GPUs to host your full footprint at on-demand prices. The answer is multi-cloud: a control plane that knows about replicas across AWS, GCP, CoreWeave, and your colo, and routes traffic accordingly. Benefits:

  • Capacity: bin-pack workloads across providers' available GPUs instead of being constrained by any one.
  • Redundancy: a regional outage at one provider doesn't kill your service.
  • Latency: serve EU users from a EU region (whichever provider has GPUs there); US users from US.
  • Compliance: data residency requirements can pin specific tenants to specific providers.

Cost: the control plane is genuinely complex. Identity, networking, secrets, monitoring, deployment all have to span providers. Most teams start single-cloud and add providers only when the GPU constraint forces the issue.

Geo-aware routing — the 5ms-per-timezone rule

Network latency is roughly speed-of-light limited at long distance. A useful rule of thumb: ~5 ms of round-trip latency per timezone of distance. NY ↔ LA = ~70 ms RTT; NY ↔ London = ~75 ms; SF ↔ Tokyo = ~110 ms. For a streaming LLM API with a 200 ms TTFT budget, that's a serious chunk consumed before the GPU even starts working. Multi-region deployments aren't optional for global low-latency products; they're table stakes.

GPU failure rates — the number you should plan for

The Llama 3 paper reported their pretraining cluster: 16,000 GPUs running for 54 days experienced 419 hardware interruptions — roughly 1 failure per 50,000 GPU-hours. At that rate, a steady-state 1,000-GPU inference fleet sees a hardware-driven incident every ~50 hours, or roughly 3–4 a week.

Implications for serving:

  • Active-passive failover is too slow for inference; you want active-active across at least two zones / regions.
  • Replicas should be cattle, not pets. A failing replica gets drained, replaced, and the request retries somewhere else within seconds.
  • Multi-region and multi-provider isn't just "for fun" — provider-wide outages happen and they happen during your peak.
📖 The Llama 3 Herd of Models — failure rates sectionSF Compute

Production › Security & Compliance

Security and compliance new

What's worth protecting in an inference deployment, and the certifications customers will ask for

Three things to protect
AssetThreatDefense
User data (prompts, completions, conversation history)Leakage to other tenants, to logs, to model providers, to training dataTLS in transit; encryption at rest; tenant isolation; opt-out from training; PII redaction; KV cache scoped per tenant
Model weightsExfiltration of fine-tunes, frontier checkpoints, or licensed third-party weightsEncrypted weight storage; signed images; minimal egress; container image attestation
InfrastructureLateral movement from a compromised tenant, supply-chain attacks via Python deps, malicious checkpoints (pickle exec)Network segmentation; rootless containers; safetensors-only loading; signed-package supply chain; runtime sandboxing for tool calls
Tenant isolation in shared inference

Multi-tenant LLM serving — many customers on one base model — has a specific risk surface that doesn't exist in single-tenant deployments:

  • KV cache leakage. Prefix cache hits are great for performance, but accidentally hitting another tenant's prefix is a data breach. Caches must be scoped per tenant (or per security boundary), with the cache key including the tenant ID.
  • LoRA adapter mix-up. Routing the wrong adapter is functionally a data leak in production tenanted fine-tunes. Adapter selection must be authoritative based on authenticated identity, not user-supplied parameters.
  • Tool call privileges. An agent's tool calls run with whatever permissions the sandbox has. A prompt injection that triggers a tool call can become a privilege-escalation primitive. Sandbox per tenant; never share tool execution environments.
The certifications customers ask for
CertificationWhat it coversWho asks for it
SOC 2 Type IIOperational controls (security, availability, confidentiality) audited over a multi-month periodMost B2B SaaS customers; effectively table stakes for enterprise sales
ISO 27001Information security management systemsEuropean enterprises; global procurement
HIPAAProtected health information handlingUS healthcare; requires Business Associate Agreement
FedRAMPUS federal government workloadsGovernment / public sector deals
PCI DSSPayment card data handlingAnyone touching cardholder data — usually not LLM serving directly, but sometimes downstream

For LLM serving specifically, customers increasingly also ask: are you training on customer data? (Default answer should be "no, opt-in only.") Where is data stored, and for how long? What happens if a customer requests deletion?

Data residency

Some customers — EU public sector, Canadian healthcare, Japanese financial — require their data never leave specific jurisdictions. This forces region-specific deployments and changes the multi-cloud calculus: you can't serve a Frankfurt customer from a Virginia replica. Operationally this means region-pinned tenancy at the routing layer (the request never crosses the boundary) and an audit trail proving it. Often the single biggest constraint on which providers and regions you must support.

📖 SOC 2HIPAA overview
