Production › Observability & Deployment

Observability and deployment patterns new

What to dashboard, what to alert on, and how to ship changes without breaking everyone

The dashboards you need
LayerMetricsTool
API gatewayQPS, status codes (2xx/4xx/5xx), request size, response size, TTFBDatadog, Cloudflare, your CDN
EngineP50/P90/P99 TTFT, P50/P90/P99 TBT, queue depth, batch size, KV cache utilization, prefix-cache hit rateEngine-exposed Prometheus metrics + Grafana
GPUSM activity, HBM bandwidth utilization, NVLink/PCIe traffic, GPU memory, GPU temperatureDCGM exporter → Prometheus
HostCPU, memory, network, disk I/Onode_exporter
ClusterReplica count, autoscaling events, pod restarts, image pull failuresKube-state-metrics, Prometheus
Latency, the way it should be reported

Always P50, P90, P95, P99 — never just mean (cross-link to the benchmarking section's right-skewness argument). Per-endpoint and per-model. A dashboard that shows "average TTFT 200 ms" when P99 is 8 s is actively misleading. SLOs live at P95 or P99; that's what your alerts should fire on.

Queue depth as the leading indicator

Latency is a lagging metric — by the time P99 spikes, you're already shedding requests. Queue depth (or pending-tokens) is the leading metric: it grows before latency does and gives autoscaling enough lead time to bring up new replicas. Set warning alerts on queue depth, page alerts on latency SLO violations.

Deployment patterns
PatternHow it worksWhen to use
Blue-greenDeploy "green" alongside "blue"; flip a switch to send all traffic to green. Roll back by flipping back.Stateless services with simple rollback. Less common for LLM serving because you double the GPU footprint during the swap.
Canary (4-step)Send 1% → 10% → 50% → 100% of traffic to the new version, watching SLO at each step.Default for LLM serving. Cheaper than blue-green; quality regressions show up in evals against canary traffic before full rollout.
Shadow trafficMirror real requests to the new version; discard responses; compare to production.Validating engine upgrades, quantization changes, model swaps without any user impact. Standard for high-stakes changes.

For model upgrades specifically, shadow + canary is the safe pattern: shadow first to compare quality and latency at production load, then canary for the actual cutover. Anthropic, OpenAI, and major hosted-API providers use variants of this.

Alerting — what's worth waking someone up for
  • SLO burn rate on TTFT P99, TBT P99, error rate. Use multi-window multi-burn-rate alerting (Google SRE book) to catch both fast-burning incidents and slow-burning quality regressions.
  • Queue saturation — pending tokens above a threshold for >1 minute.
  • Replica health — repeated pod restarts, failed warmups, GPU thermal throttling.
  • Quality regressions from automated evals — the silent killer; wire eval failures into the same on-call surface as latency.
📖 Google SRE book — alerting on SLOsDCGM exporter
← Security & ComplianceClient-Side & Streaming →
Production › Client-Side & Streaming

Client-side and streaming protocols new

TLS handshakes, async-with-webhooks, and HTTP vs WebSocket vs gRPC

The TLS handshake tax

A new HTTPS connection costs one or two round-trips before the first byte of the request can be sent: TCP handshake (1 RTT) + TLS handshake (1 RTT for TLS 1.3, 2 RTTs for TLS 1.2). For a client 50 ms away from the server, that's 100 ms of pure overhead per connection. On a 300 ms TTFT budget, you've burned 33% before the GPU did anything.

The fix: reuse connections. HTTP/1.1 keep-alive, HTTP/2 multiplexing, and HTTP/3 connection migration all amortize the handshake across many requests. Standard SDKs (OpenAI's Python client, Anthropic's, LiteLLM) handle this for you — but only if the calling code reuses one client object. Per-request requests.post() is a TLS-handshake-per-request anti-pattern that silently doubles latency.

Streaming protocols compared
ProtocolFormatDirectionWhen to use
HTTP / SSE (Server-Sent Events)Text events over a long-lived HTTP responseServer → client onlyDefault for LLM streaming APIs (OpenAI, Anthropic). Trivially proxyable, works through corporate firewalls and CDNs.
WebSocketArbitrary binary or text frames over a long-lived bidirectional connectionBoth directionsBidirectional streaming (live tool callbacks, audio, interactive agents). More finicky through proxies.
gRPCProtobuf over HTTP/2 streams; schema-enforcedBoth directionsInternal service-to-service when you want strict contracts and language-agnostic clients. Rarely used for public LLM APIs because browsers don't speak it natively.
Async with webhooks — for jobs that take minutes

For batch transcription, long-running agent loops, deep-research tasks, or anything that takes more than ~30 s, holding an HTTP connection open is fragile. The standard pattern is fire-and-forget with webhooks:

  1. Client POSTs the request and gets back a job ID.
  2. Server processes the job in the background, possibly minutes-hours.
  3. When done, server POSTs the result to a callback URL the client provided.

This decouples client and server lifecycles, survives client reconnects, and is the only sane pattern for jobs longer than a few minutes. OpenAI's Batch API, Anthropic's Message Batches, and most ASR providers use variants of this.

Client-side latency budgeting

End-to-end perceived latency = network RTT + TLS overhead (if cold) + queue wait + TTFT + first-render time on the client. For a "feels instant" target of 200 ms, the GPU has maybe 80–120 ms to play with after the network and client-side costs. Two consequences for serving:

  • Geographic placement matters — see the multi-cloud chapter.
  • Streaming is non-negotiable — TTFT becomes the felt metric, not E2E. Even a slow generator feels fast if the first token arrives quickly.
📖 TLS 1.3 RFCServer-Sent Events specOpenAI Batch API
← Observability & DeploymentChapter 9 Quiz →
Production › Quiz

Chapter 9 Quiz — Production

Test your understanding of containers, autoscaling, multi-cloud, security, observability, and client-side

Chapter 9 Quiz — Production
1 Your autoscaler triggers when P95 TTFT crosses 1 s, but new replicas take 3 minutes to start serving. What's wrong with this design and what's the fix?
P95 TTFT is a lagging indicator — by the time it crosses threshold, you're already in SLO violation, and you have 3 minutes of cold-start before the new capacity helps. The fix: scale on a leading indicator (queue depth or pending tokens) that grows before latency does. Page on TTFT/TBT SLO breaches, but trigger autoscaling on queue. Pair with mitigations: in-datacenter weight cache, cached compiled engines, optionally a small warm pool. Or use NIM-style pre-baked images to compress cold start to under a minute.
2 A multi-tenant deployment caches prefix KVs aggressively for performance. What's the security risk and how is it mitigated?
Without per-tenant scoping, tenant A's request could hit tenant B's cached prefix — a data leak by accident. Fix: include the authenticated tenant ID in the cache key. Tenant A's hash never matches tenant B's, so no cross-tenant cache hits possible. Same principle applies to LoRA adapters: adapter selection must be driven by authenticated identity, not user-supplied parameters.
3 The Llama 3 paper reported 1 hardware failure per 50,000 GPU-hours. For a steady-state 1,000-GPU inference fleet, how often does a hardware failure happen, and what's the implication for your deployment topology?
1,000 GPUs × 24 hours = 24,000 GPU-hours/day. At 1/50,000 failure rate, that's about 1 failure every 50 hours — roughly 3–4 a week. Implication: replicas must be cattle, not pets — failover must be active-active across at least two zones, with seconds-scale request retries. Active-passive failover is too slow; pet-replicas with manual recovery aren't an option at this fleet size.
4 A user complains your API "feels slow" with a 300ms TTFT SLO. Network RTT to your nearest region is 70ms. They're using requests.post() per call instead of a persistent client. What's eating the budget?
TLS 1.3 handshake = 2 RTTs = 140 ms on a fresh connection. Plus the request RTT itself = 70 ms. That's 210 ms before the GPU starts working — leaving 90 ms for queue + prefill + first token. With connection reuse, the handshake amortizes across many requests; latency drops to roughly one RTT plus server work. Per-call requests.post() is the silent killer of LLM API latency budgets. Switching to a reused HTTP client (the OpenAI/Anthropic SDKs do this by default) is often the single biggest user-perceived speedup with no engine changes.
5 You're cutting over to a new model version. Why is the standard pattern shadow-then-canary rather than just blue-green?
Blue-green doubles your GPU footprint during the swap — at H100 prices, hours of doubled inference fleet is meaningful money. More importantly, it provides no quality validation: you flip 100% of traffic at once, so any quality regression hits all users. Shadow (mirror real traffic to the candidate, discard responses) lets you compare quality and latency under production load with zero user impact. Canary (1% → 10% → 50% → 100%) catches anything shadow missed and limits blast radius if something goes wrong. Together they cover both quality regressions and capacity issues. Blue-green is fine for stateless web services; LLM model swaps need the shadow step.
← Client-Side & Streaming
DeepDives › SGLang

SGLang — a NotebookLM-style deep dive new

A NotebookLM-style five-segment deep dive grounded entirely in six cited sources: the SGLang paper, the LMSYS launch blog, the GitHub README, Ying Sheng's CMU lecture, an implementation walkthrough, and a compressed-FSM explainer. ~50 minutes of audio.

How to read this section. Every concrete claim is attributed inline to one of six sources, marked [paper] (Sheng et al., arXiv 2312.07104, NeurIPS'24), [blog] (LMSYS launch blog, Jan 2024), [readme] (sgl-project/sglang README), [cmu] (Ying Sheng's CMU lecture PDF), [sugiv] (blog.sugiv.fyi implementation walkthrough), [aimerge] (theaimerge compressed-FSM explainer). When a number is workload-dependent or version-specific, the source's qualifier is preserved. Segment 5 contains an editorial extension to the NIM-Factory framing — clearly marked, because none of the six sources cover NIM, Dynamo, or NIXL directly.
Segment 1 — What SGLang is, and why a co-designed runtime exists

SGLang is described by its CMU lecture as "a fast serving framework for large language models and vision language models," and on its own GitHub page as "an open-source inference engine for LLMs" [cmu][readme]. But the launch blog frames it differently: SGLang is "a domain-specific language embedded in Python" with primitives that "handle intra-program parallelism and batching" — language inspired by Guidance, but extended for runtime co-design [blog]. Both descriptions are true. The thing has a frontend (the SGLang DSL) and a backend (the runtime), and they were built together.

The frontend's primitives, per the launch blog [blog]:

  • fork — "creates multiple parallel copies of a prompt," giving the runtime an explicit signal that N children share an ancestor.
  • gen — "invokes an LLM generation and stores the result in a variable," with non-blocking execution that enables parallel background calls.
  • Variable retrieval via [variable_name] for reading prior generation results.
  • choices — "imposes constraints on the generation," restricting outputs to a fixed set.
  • run — executes an SGLang function with arguments.

And the language can run in two modes: interpreter mode (eager execution) or compiler mode, which traces the program into a dataflow graph and enables "code movement, instruction selection, and auto-tuning" [blog]. The point is not to replace Python; it is to surface enough structure that the runtime can see what the program is doing — which calls share a prefix, which branches are independent, which spans are constrained — and then exploit that.

The runtime's architecture, per the CMU lecture, is small [cmu]: a Server containing an API server / Tokenizer, a Detokenizer, and a Model Worker. The Model Worker holds three things: a memory pool, a radix tree cache, and an attention backend. The Client side exposes a Native generation API, an OpenAI-compatible API, and the structured language frontend. The CMU slide labels this "Lightweight and customizable codebase in Python/PyTorch" — and that compactness is part of why the project moves quickly.

The reason a structured frontend matters is that real LLM applications are not single self-contained prompts. The CMU lecture characterizes the dominant pattern as "complex pipeline with multiple LLM calls" — chained calls (a follow-up question reuses the previous answer) and parallel calls (one question fans out to several answers, then a select step) [cmu]. The lecture lists three concrete structures: multi-turn chat (where the chat history grows and is reusable across turns), few-shot learning (where the few-shot examples are shared across many prompts), and tree search with LLM agents (where every node of the search tree shares an ancestor's history with its siblings) [cmu]. In all three, the KV cache from earlier calls is reusable — and the punchline of the lecture's first half is "Existing systems: Discard KV cache after an LLM call finishes. Ours: Maintain the KV cache of all LLM calls in a radix tree (compact prefix tree)" [cmu].

Two facts worth absorbing before going deeper:

  • The runtime ships an OpenAI-compatible API — you do not need to use the DSL to benefit [cmu]. The lecture's architecture diagram shows three client entry points: native API, OpenAI-compatible API, and the language frontend. The runtime works either way.
  • Production scale. The CMU lecture says SGLang "serves the production and research workloads at xAI (100K+ GPUs)" [cmu]. The README puts the broader number at "powering over 400,000 GPUs worldwide" and "generating trillions of tokens in production each day," with users including xAI, AMD, NVIDIA, Intel, LinkedIn, Cursor, Oracle Cloud, Google Cloud, Microsoft Azure, AWS [readme]. The lecture also lists 400+ OSS contributors [cmu].

The version history from the CMU lecture is also worth seeing in one place, because it shows what the project has invested in over its first year [cmu]:

  • v0.1 (Jan 2024): 5× higher throughput with automatic KV cache reuse; 3× faster grammar-based constrained decoding.
  • v0.2 (July 2024): 3× higher throughput with low-overhead CPU runtime.
  • v0.3 (Sept 2024): 7× faster Triton attention backend for custom attention variants (DeepSeek MLA); 1.5× lower latency with torch.compile.
  • v0.4 (Dec 2024): Zero-overhead CPU scheduler and structured outputs; cache-aware load balancer; "fastest DeepSeek inference."

The trajectory tells the story: KV reuse (v0.1) → CPU scheduling (v0.2/v0.4) → MoE / MLA acceleration (v0.3) → cluster-level cache awareness (v0.4). Each version solves a different bottleneck. The next four segments walk through the four major techniques that the CMU lecture singles out.

v0.1 · Jan '245× KV reuse
3× grammar decode
→
v0.2 · Jul '243× CPU runtime
→
v0.3 · Sep '247× MLA Triton
1.5× torch.compile
→
v0.4 · Dec '24Zero-overhead sched
Cache-aware router
Segment 2 — RadixAttention, the headline trick

The CMU lecture's framing is direct [cmu]: the KV cache is "some reusable intermediate tensors," it "can be very large (>20GB, larger than model weights)," and it "only depends on the prefix tokens." Existing systems discard it when an LLM call finishes. SGLang keeps it — for every call, in a radix tree — and reuses it whenever a new request shares a prefix.

What the data structure looks like

The launch blog describes RadixAttention as a "space-efficient prefix tree" where "edges can be labeled with sequences of elements of varying lengths" [blog]. KV tensors are stored on the GPU in paged layout (one token per page); the tree structure lives on the CPU "with minimal overhead" [blog]. The blog explicitly notes that RadixAttention is "compatible with continuous batching and paged attention," and that prefix matching is automatic — the user does not configure anything [blog].

The implementation walkthrough adds the per-node detail [sugiv]: each TreeNode holds children = defaultdict(TreeNode) for branching on token IDs, an optional value: torch.Tensor for KV cache contents, plus last_access_time and hit_count for eviction bookkeeping. Prefix matching is the obvious tree-walk: descend by token ID until the next token diverges, complexity O(prefix_length).

The CMU lecture walks through an animated five-frame insertion example [cmu]. The tree starts empty. After one request ("You are a helpful assistant. User: Hello! Assistant: Hi!"), the tree is a single chain. A second request that extends with "User: Solve this problem... Assistant: Sure!..." extends that chain. A third request branches at the system prompt — "User: What can you do?..." — splitting the tree at the divergence point so the system prefix is shared. The fifth frame shows eviction: an idle leaf is freed, marked "evicted," when memory pressure hits. The lecture states the cache "handles trees with hundreds of thousands of tokens" [cmu].

Frame 1 — Empty
·

Cache is empty. No prior call to share with.

Frame 2–3 — Linear chain
[ Sys: helpful assistant ]
        │
[ User: Hello! / Assistant: Hi! ]
        │
[ User: Solve... / Assistant: Sure!... ]

Two requests, where the second extends the first. Each new turn appends to the same chain — no branching yet.

Frame 4 — Branch at divergence
       [ Sys: helpful assistant ]
              ╱           ╲
[ User: Hello! / Hi! ]   [ User: What can you do? / I can... ]
        │
[ User: Solve... / Sure!... ]

A third request shares only the system prompt. The tree splits at the divergence token. The system prefix is stored exactly once and shared by both branches.

Frame 5 — LRU leaf eviction
       [ Sys: helpful assistant ]
              ╱           ╲
[ User: Hello! / Hi! ]   [ User: What can... / I can... ]
        │                          │
   [ X evicted ]          [ User: Write a story... ]

Memory pressure → recursive leaf-node LRU. Only the leaf is freed; the shared system-prompt ancestor is preserved as long as any descendant is in use [blog].

Why it works

• Shared prefixes physically share an ancestor path — stored once, regardless of how many branches descend from it.

• O(prefix_length) for prefix matching, insert, and eviction [sugiv].

• Handles trees with hundreds of thousands of tokens — same data structure, same complexity [cmu].

• KV tensors live on the GPU in paged layout (one token/page); the tree itself lives on the CPU "with minimal overhead" [blog].

Eviction

The launch blog specifies the eviction policy explicitly: "Least Recently Used (LRU) eviction policy" with "recursive leaf-node eviction" [blog]. The "recursive leaf" framing is important — the cache only ever evicts leaves of the tree, which preserves shared ancestors as long as any descendant is in use. The implementation walkthrough mentions "LRU eviction with cache-aware scheduling" tracked via last_access_time on each node [sugiv].

Why this beats discarding the cache — the headline benchmark

The launch blog's headline result is "up to 5 times higher throughput" versus the baselines, with the system "outperforming the baseline systems in all benchmarks" tested [blog]. The CMU lecture restates the v0.1 milestone the same way: "5x higher throughput with automatic KV cache reuse" [cmu]. Both attribute it to the same nine workloads.

The exact setup, per the launch blog [blog]:

  • Hardware: Llama-7B on a single NVIDIA A10G (24 GB, FP16); Mixtral-8×7B on 8× A10G with tensor parallelism (FP16).
  • Baselines: vLLM v0.2.5, Guidance v0.1.8, Hugging Face TGI v1.3.0.
  • Workloads: MMLU (5-shot), HellaSwag (20-shot), ReAct Agent, Tree-of-Thought (GSM-8K), JSON Decode (Wikipedia extraction), Chat (short and long, 4-turn), DSPy RAG, LLaVA Bench.

The CMU lecture's bar chart (sourced back to the v0.1 blog) shows SGLang at normalized throughput 1.0 on every workload, with the second-best baseline ranging from ~0.94 (vLLM on Chat Long) down to ~0.03 (vLLM on Tree-of-Thought) [cmu]. The gap is largest exactly where structure exists — agents and ToT — and smallest on plain long chat.

Cache hit rates by workload

The implementation walkthrough provides the often-cited cache hit rate table comparing vLLM PagedAttention to SGLang RadixAttention [sugiv]:

WorkloadvLLM PagedAttentionSGLang RadixAttention
Few-Shot Learning15–25%85–95%
Multi-Turn Chat10–20%75–90%
Code Analysis5–15%60–80%
Single Requests0%0% (equivalent)

The "Single Requests" row is the important honest case: when there is no prefix sharing in the workload, both systems report 0% hit and SGLang has nothing to gain. The walkthrough explicitly flags this — there is no separate breakout for agents or tree-of-thought beyond what is implicit in the workload categories.

Throughput numbers from a more recent comparison

The implementation walkthrough also provides per-task throughput in tokens/second, comparing vLLM v0.6.2 to SGLang v0.4 [sugiv]:

TaskvLLM v0.6.2SGLang v0.4Speedup
MMLU (5-shot)1,4204,2503.0×
Chat (multi-turn)8904,1804.7×
JSON Decode5802,9005.0×
Code Generation1,2003,6003.0×
Few-shot Learning6502,8504.4×

Same source reports first-token latency 53–73% faster than vLLM across batch sizes 1–32 [sugiv].

Two important caveats from the sources themselves. First, the launch blog's 5× number was measured against vLLM v0.2.5, which predates vLLM's prefix-cache support — modern vLLM is a stronger baseline than that comparison reflects [blog]. Second, every cited number is workload-dependent; the launch blog's own bar chart shows the gap shrinking on Chat Long, where the prefix sharing is less dominant relative to the per-turn generation [cmu].

Segment 3 — The other three techniques

The CMU lecture lists four major techniques in total: (1) RadixAttention, which we just covered, (2) cache-aware load balancing via SGL-Router, (3) zero-overhead CPU scheduling, and (4) hierarchical KV cache [cmu]. We will cover the second and third in detail because the lecture does, and we will cover the fourth briefly because the lecture itself marks the slide "Skipped" — the cited sources do not give us deep content on it. Structured outputs is a separate thread that is not in the CMU lecture's four but is explicitly listed as a v0.4 capability and a paper contribution [cmu][paper]; we will cover it last in this segment.

Cache-aware load balancing — SGL-Router

The CMU lecture states the problem and the comparison explicitly [cmu]. Round-Robin Data Parallel (SGLang v0.3) routes requests across replicas without any awareness of which replica has which prefix cached. The lecture annotates this case: "Not cache aware, ~20% Cache Hit Rate." The replacement, Cache-Aware Data Parallel (SGLang v0.4), introduces an "Approximate Tree" at the router that mirrors the actual radix trees on the workers, and routes each request to the replica with the best matching prefix. The lecture annotates this case: "Cache aware, ~75% Cache Hit Rate."

The lecture provides a concrete head-to-head table for a workload with two prefixes (Prefix 1 routed to replicas 1–2, Prefix 2 routed to replicas 3–4) [cmu]:

Round robinCache-aware load balancer
Throughput (token/s)82,665158,596
Cache hit rate20%75%

That is roughly 1.92× throughput from routing alone, with the underlying engine unchanged. The implementation walkthrough adds detail on the routing function: SGL-Router is implemented in Rust and uses an approximate radix tree at the router level (with INSERT and EVICT messages from the workers) to score candidate replicas as score = cache_hit_rate * 2.0 - load_factor, then routes to the highest-scoring replica [sugiv]. The same source reports an aggregate "1.9× throughput improvement with 3.8× higher cache hit rates" when the load balancer is enabled [sugiv].

Round-robin (SGLang v0.3)

Incoming prefixes scattered across all workers — every worker holds a bit of every prefix.

W1: P1 P2
W2: P1 P2
W3: P1 P2
W4: P1 P2
Cache hit rate ≈ 20%
Throughput ≈ 82,665 tok/s
Cache-aware (SGLang v0.4)

Approximate-tree at router → each prefix sticks to a worker subset.

W1: P1
W2: P1
W3: P2
W4: P2
Cache hit rate ≈ 75%
Throughput ≈ 158,596 tok/s (1.92× ↑)

The framing that lands for an infrastructure audience: this is session affinity for KV state. Round-robin is the right default for stateless services; cached LLM serving is not stateless, so round-robin trashes the cache. The lecture's two phrases — "Not cache aware, ~20%" versus "Cache aware, ~75%" — are the whole story.

Zero-overhead CPU scheduling

The CMU lecture is blunt: "An unoptimized inference engine can waste more than 50% time on CPU scheduling" [cmu]. The supporting chart compares vLLM v0.5.4 to SGLang v0.3 on Llama 1.3B, plotting average iteration time in ms [cmu]:

  • vLLM v0.5.4: ~32 ms per iteration, of which ~16 ms is GPU computation and ~16 ms is CPU overhead. Roughly 50/50.
  • SGLang v0.3: ~17 ms per iteration, of which ~16 ms is GPU computation and ~1 ms is CPU overhead. CPU overhead is essentially gone.

Same GPU work, half the wall-clock. The chart cites mlsys.wuklab.io/posts/scheduling_overhead/ as the underlying analysis [cmu].

vLLM v0.5.4
GPU 16 ms
CPU overhead 16 ms
≈ 32 ms / iter
SGLang v0.3
GPU 16 ms
≈ 17 ms / iter — CPU overhead ≈ 1 ms
GPU compute
CPU scheduling overhead
Source: CMU lecture, Llama 1.3B

What does the CPU scheduler actually do, that takes that much time? The lecture lists the jobs [cmu]:

  • Receive input messages.
  • Stream model outputs.
  • Check the stop conditions.
  • Maintain the radix tree and run prefix matching.
  • Allocate memory for the next batch.

And the design ideas behind the overlapped scheduler [cmu]:

  • "Resolve the dependency by delaying the stop condition check" — break the synchronous tie between detecting "this request finished" and dispatching the next batch, so the next batch can start without waiting.
  • "Use CUDA events and streams to do fine-grained scheduling."

The technique itself: instead of a Blocking Scheduler (Scheduler runs → Model Worker runs → Scheduler runs → Model Worker runs, serialized), an Overlapped Scheduler runs the next-iteration scheduling concurrently with the current-iteration model work, so by the time the GPU finishes step N the next batch is already prepared [cmu]. The lecture credits the conceptual lineage to NanoFlow (Kan Zhu et al.).

Blocking
Sched
Worker
Sched
Worker
Sched
Worker
Overlapped
Sched
Sched
Sched
.

Worker
Worker
CPU scheduler
GPU model worker
In overlapped mode, scheduler for batch N+1 runs during worker step N

The lecture's own profiling result for the optimized scheduler: "Zero CPU time according to the nsight profiler" — the trace shows 99.8% kernels and 0.2% memory, with no visible CPU bubble between kernel launches [cmu]. And on a downstream Llama 3.2 3B benchmark on H100, SGLang v0.4 reaches ~5,500 output tokens/s versus a SOTA OSS baseline at ~4,100 tok/s — "1.3× faster than SOTA OSS baseline" [cmu]. SGLang v0.3 sits between them at ~4,900 tok/s, showing the v0.3 → v0.4 scheduler progression even after the original wins.

The implementation walkthrough phrases the same idea at the API level [sugiv]: SGLang uses torch.cuda.Event() to mark GPU work boundaries and overlaps CPU batch preparation with GPU execution via futures. The same source reports GPU utilization moving from "78–85% (non-overlapped)" to "95–98% (zero-overhead)" — consistent with the wall-clock drop in the lecture's iteration-time chart.

Structured outputs (compressed FSM)

Constrained decoding — making the model emit valid JSON, YAML, or regex-matching text — is implemented in the conventional approach by compiling the grammar to a finite state machine and, at each decode step, applying logit bias to disallow tokens that would leave the valid set. The compressed-FSM explainer summarizes the standard approach [aimerge]: "The FSM method converts a JSON schema into a regular expression," then "for each state in the FSM, it calculates the possible transitions," then "it identifies the acceptable next tokens" and applies logit bias to constrain output, while "the FSM tracks the current state during decoding" to maintain validity.

The compressed variant — referenced in the explainer as "Jump-Forward decoding using Compressed FSM" — is described at a high level only [aimerge]; the explainer does not provide implementation depth or quantitative comparisons, deferring to "this guide from SGLang" for benchmarks. What the cited sources do tell us about magnitude is on the structured-decoding axis overall: the CMU lecture's v0.1 milestone says "3× faster grammar-based constrained decoding" [cmu], and v0.4 brings "structured outputs" listed alongside the zero-overhead CPU scheduler as a flagship capability [cmu]. The implementation walkthrough reports a JSON-generation operating point of "4,200 tokens/sec, 99.8% validity rate, 0.4 s latency" with SGLang plus xGrammar [sugiv].

The lecture's headline-result slide also includes JSON Decode (Wikipedia extraction) as one of the nine workloads where SGLang reaches 1.0 normalized throughput, and the launch blog's bar chart shows it as a workload where structured-output handling and KV reuse compound [blog][cmu].

Hierarchical KV cache

The fourth technique in the lecture's outline is "Hierarchical KV cache" — and the slide itself is labeled "Skipped" [cmu]. The README mentions "prefix caching" and "prefill-decode disaggregation" as features [readme], and the open-source roadmap slide lists "Prefill-decode disaggregation" and "Expert/pipeline/context parallelism" under "Throughput-oriented large-scale deployment" [cmu]. The DeepSeek case study (Segment 5) shows an "External KVCache Storage (Optional)" tier in the architecture [cmu]. Beyond that, the cited sources do not provide enough detail to write a deep dive on tier sizing, transport, or specific workload outcomes — so we will not invent one here.

Segment 4 — When SGLang wins, by what the sources actually show

The cited sources do not contain a head-to-head SGLang vs vLLM vs TensorRT-LLM workload guide written by a neutral party. What they contain are SGLang's own benchmarks against vLLM and Guidance, plus an industry-adoption picture. Honest framing: the decision rule below is what those sources do support, not a general "engine of choice for X" pronouncement.

Workloads where the source benchmarks show the largest SGLang gap

The launch blog's bar chart and the CMU lecture's reproduction of it both show the workload-by-workload spread of the v0.1 5× headline [blog][cmu]. The gap is enormous on:

  • HellaSwag (20-shot) — large shared few-shot prefix.
  • Tree-of-Thought (GSM-8K) — extreme branching from a shared root.
  • ReAct Agent — agent loops with shared system / tool prompt.
  • JSON Decode (Wikipedia extraction) — structured output plus shared schema.
  • DSPy RAG, LLaVA Bench — both show vLLM/baseline at <0.3 normalized throughput on the v0.1 chart.

And the gap is small or absent on:

  • Chat (Long, 4-turn) — vLLM reaches ~0.94 normalized throughput on the v0.1 chart, the closest of any workload [cmu].
  • Single Requests — the implementation walkthrough explicitly puts both vLLM and SGLang at 0% cache hit on this case [sugiv].

So the source-grounded rule is: SGLang's wins are concentrated in workloads with structure — shared prefixes, branching, structured output. That matches the system's design.

Industry adoption — who is actually running it

The CMU lecture's industry-adoption slide names the institutions running SGLang in production [cmu]: xAI, NVIDIA, AMD, Atlas Cloud, Baseten, Cursor, Data Crunch, Etched, Hyperbolic, Jam & Tea Studios, LinkedIn, Meituan, Nebius, Novita, RunPod, Stanford, Berkeley, UCLA, 01.AI. The README adds Intel, Oracle Cloud, Google Cloud, Microsoft Azure, AWS [readme]. The lecture also notes "SGLang serves the production and research workloads at xAI (100K+ GPUs)" — and the project's GitHub-stars badge in the same slide reads 13.3k [cmu].

What SGLang explicitly supports today

From the lecture's feature-coverage slide and the README, the runtime ships these optimizations [cmu][readme]: continuous batching, prefix caching, token attention (paged attention), speculative decoding, tensor / pipeline / expert / data parallelism, chunked prefill, structured outputs, multi-LoRA serving, quantization in FP4 / FP8 / INT4 (plus AWQ / GPTQ per the README). Model coverage spans Llama 1/2/3, Qwen, Mixtral, DeepSeek V3/R1, Kimi, GLM, GPT, Gemma, Mistral on the text side, and Llama 4, QwenVL, DeepSeek-VL, Llava, Pixtral on the vision side [cmu][readme]. Hardware: NVIDIA GB200 / B300 / H100 / A100, AMD MI355 / MI300, Intel Xeon, Google TPU, Ascend NPU [readme].

The lecture's open-source roadmap groups upcoming work into three buckets [cmu]:

  • Throughput-oriented large-scale deployment: prefill-decode disaggregation, expert / pipeline / context parallelism.
  • Reinforcement learning integration: weight sync, asynchronous algorithms, memory saver; veRL, Areal, LlamaFactory integrations.
  • Low-latency optimizations: speculative decoding (e.g. EAGLE 3), kernel optimizations.

The full roadmap is tracked at github.com/sgl-project/sglang/issues/4042 per the same slide [cmu].

Segment 5 — Production reality, with the DeepSeek case study

The CMU lecture closes with a real production case study, and it is the most concrete piece of evidence in any of the cited sources for what an SGLang fleet at scale actually looks like and how its economics work [cmu].

The DeepSeek architecture, by component

The lecture diagrams the DeepSeek serving architecture as a stack of disaggregated services [cmu]:

  • API Server — the request entry point.
  • Prefill Load Balancer → Prefill Service with its own Expert-Parallel Load Balancer fronting the prefill workers.
  • Decode Load Balancer → Decode Service with its own Expert-Parallel Load Balancer fronting the decode workers.
  • External KVCache Storage (Optional) — a tier underneath both the prefill and decode services, accessed by both.

The lecture summarizes the recipe in one line: "Load balancer + prefill / decode disaggregation + speculative decoding + quantization + tensor / expert / pipeline parallelism + external KVCache storage" [cmu]. Every one of those is a feature listed elsewhere in the same lecture's coverage slides — this is not a custom system, it is SGLang's own primitives composed.

ClientAPI Server
→

→

The 24-hour production numbers

The lecture provides hard 24-hour stats from the DeepSeek system [cmu]:

  • Total input tokens: 608B, of which 342B (56.3%) hit the on-disk KV cache.
  • Total output tokens: 168B.
  • Theoretical revenue: $562,027/day if all tokens were billed at DeepSeek-R1's published pricing.
  • Cost-profit margin: 545%.

The lecture's chart of cost vs theoretical income across a 24-hour cycle shows costs as a roughly flat orange band of about $5K/hour during business hours dipping to ~$1–2K/hour overnight, against a theoretical-income blue band that peaks around $30K/hour during business hours and bottoms out at ~$5–10K/hour overnight [cmu].

Two things to absorb from those numbers. First, the on-disk cache hit rate of 56.3% on input tokens is the entire economic story — more than half of input prefill compute is avoided because the KV state is already on disk. The "external KVCache storage" tier in the architecture diagram is doing exactly that work. Second, the 545% margin is the published case for why this architecture pays for itself: the same compute that a non-cached system would charge for is being served at a fraction of its incremental cost, and the difference is margin.

The lecture is careful to footnote: "The theoretical income is calculated based on R1's standard API pricing, taking into account all tokens across web, APP, and API. It is not our actual income" [cmu]. So the 545% is a unit-economics figure, not a P&L number — but it is the right unit-economics figure for the engine's effect on cost.

What this tells us about SGLang's industry positioning

The cited sources do not write an SGLang positioning doc. What they show is:

  • xAI runs it on 100K+ GPUs for production and research [cmu].
  • DeepSeek's own production system is built on it, with the architecture and economics diagrammed above [cmu].
  • The README's adopter list spans frontier labs (xAI), all three major chip vendors (NVIDIA, AMD, Intel), the major hyperscalers (AWS, Azure, GCP, Oracle), application companies (Cursor, LinkedIn), inference-as-a-service vendors (Baseten, RunPod, Hyperbolic, Nebius, Novita, Atlas Cloud), and academia (Stanford, Berkeley, UCLA, CMU) [readme][cmu].
  • The version trajectory shows steady investment in distinct bottlenecks — KV reuse (v0.1) → CPU runtime (v0.2) → MLA/torch.compile (v0.3) → zero-overhead scheduler + cache-aware LB (v0.4) [cmu].

If you want a one-line summary the sources support: SGLang's wins are concentrated in workloads with shared prefixes, branching, and structured output, and at scale those wins compound into the kind of unit economics DeepSeek discloses (56% input-cache hit, 545% margin). On workloads without that structure the sources show the gap to vLLM closing.

Editorial postscript — the NIM-Factory mapping (beyond the cited sources)
None of the six cited sources mention NVIDIA NIM, Dynamo, or NIXL. What follows is an editorial extension that maps what the sources do say onto the NIM-Factory three-engine question. Treat it as the listener's exercise, not as a claim grounded in the resource pack.

NVIDIA NIM ships three optimized inference backends per model — TensorRT-LLM, vLLM, and SGLang. The cited sources tell us what SGLang brings to that lineup that the others do not: a radix-tree KV cache that is uniquely strong on prefix-heavy and branching workloads (the launch blog's nine-workload chart and the implementation walkthrough's hit-rate table), a cache-aware router that turns those hits into ~1.92× throughput at the cluster level (the CMU lecture's 82,665 → 158,596 tok/s table), an overlapped CPU scheduler that recovers ~50% wall-clock by removing scheduling overhead (the CMU lecture's iteration-time chart), structured outputs (v0.4 capability), and a published production deployment at DeepSeek with a 56.3% on-disk cache hit rate and 545% margin (the CMU lecture's case study).

What the cited sources do not tell us — and what an interview answer would need to honest-attribute elsewhere if it goes there — is anything specific about Dynamo's KVBM, NIXL transports, or the NIM validation matrix. The right move in that interview is to talk about what SGLang demonstrably is from these sources, and let the interviewer surface the NIM-internal pieces.

A short list of things you can defend from the cited sources
  • The radix tree maintains the KV cache of all LLM calls, not just the in-flight one — and existing systems discard it when a call ends [cmu].
  • LRU with recursive leaf-node eviction; KV in paged GPU layout, tree on CPU; compatible with continuous batching and paged attention [blog].
  • Up to 5× higher throughput on the v0.1 nine-workload benchmark, on Llama-7B (A10G) and Mixtral-8×7B (8× A10G TP), against vLLM v0.2.5 / Guidance v0.1.8 / TGI v1.3.0 [blog][cmu].
  • Cache hit rates 85–95% (few-shot), 75–90% (multi-turn chat), 60–80% (code analysis), 0% (single-request) [sugiv].
  • Cache-aware router: 82,665 → 158,596 tok/s and 20% → 75% hit rate [cmu]; 1.9× throughput / 3.8× cache hit rate aggregate [sugiv].
  • Overlapped scheduler: ~32 ms → ~17 ms iteration (Llama 1.3B); "Zero CPU time" per nsight; 1.3× faster than SOTA OSS baseline (Llama 3.2 3B, H100) [cmu].
  • 3× faster grammar-based constrained decoding (v0.1) [cmu]; xGrammar JSON 4,200 tok/s @ 99.8% validity, 0.4 s latency [sugiv].
  • DeepSeek production: 608B input / 342B (56.3%) on-disk cache hit / 168B output per day; 545% theoretical margin [cmu].
  • 400,000+ GPUs, trillions of tokens/day, xAI 100K+ GPUs, 400+ OSS contributors [readme][cmu].

DeepDives › TensorRT-LLM

TensorRT-LLM — a NotebookLM-style deep dive new

A NotebookLM-style five-segment deep dive grounded in NVIDIA's own architecture docs, the developer hub, the GitHub README, the wide-EP part-3 tech blog, and one third-party optimization guide. Roughly 45–60 minutes of audio. Written for an AWS engineering manager preparing for a NIM Factory interview, with vLLM as the assumed baseline.

How to read this section. Every concrete claim is attributed inline to one of seven sources, marked [overview] (TensorRT-LLM Architecture Overview docs), [landing] (TensorRT-LLM project landing page on nvidia.github.io), [devhub] (developer.nvidia.com/tensorrt-llm marketing/feature page), [github] (NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM GitHub README and recent release notes), [ep3] (Scaling Expert Parallelism in TensorRT-LLM, Part 3 tech blog), [docs] (docs.nvidia.com/tensorrt-llm hub page), [introl] (introl.com third-party optimization guide). Numbers that the source qualifies as workload-, model-, or hardware-dependent are kept with that qualifier. Segment 5 contains an editorial postscript on the NIM-Factory framing — clearly marked, because none of the cited sources document NIM container architecture or its validation matrix.
Segment 1 — What TensorRT-LLM is, and where it fits next to vLLM

The developer hub frames TensorRT-LLM as "an open-source library built to deliver high-performance, real-time inference optimization for large language models (LLMs) on NVIDIA GPUs," with the goal that developers "maximize inference performance to serve more users in parallel, while minimizing operational costs" [devhub]. The GitHub README's canonical line is more careful: TensorRT-LLM "provides users with an easy-to-use Python API to define Large Language Models (LLMs) and supports state-of-the-art optimizations to perform inference efficiently on NVIDIA GPUs" [github]. The docs hub adds the bridging phrase that ties the project to its namesake compiler — the API is used to "build NVIDIA TensorRT engines that contain state-of-the-art optimizations" [docs]. That sentence is the whole identity in one line: TensorRT-LLM is the LLM-shaped front door to the underlying TensorRT compiler stack.

Architecturally the docs describe two layers. There is a Python authoring layer — the LLM class is the "core entry point" that "abstracts away preprocessing and postprocessing complexity while managing efficient GPU execution" [overview]. And underneath there is a runtime, which on the new PyTorch-native path is structured around a PyExecutor(Worker) running an asynchronous background loop with four explicit subcomponents: a Scheduler that "determines which requests execute at each step," a KVCacheManager that "manages Key-Value cache allocation and maintenance," a ModelEngine that "loads and executes the model on GPU," and a Sampler that "applies sampling strategies (greedy, top-k, top-p, beam search) to logits" [overview]. The four-component split is worth memorizing because every later optimization plugs into one of those four.

The point that does not survive a casual skim of the docs but matters most for an interview answer is the relationship to TensorRT itself. The NVIDIA inference stack has had a graph-level compiler — TensorRT — for years, and its model has always been the same: take a model description, run aggressive graph rewriting (kernel fusion, quantization calibration, layer selection, precision lowering), and emit a serialized engine that is then loaded and replayed at inference time. TensorRT-LLM inherits that build-then-run pattern; the docs hub describes the workflow as "build NVIDIA TensorRT engines that contain state-of-the-art optimizations" [docs]. The newer PyTorch-native LLM API softens that workflow — the GitHub README emphasizes "high-level Python LLM API" and PyTorch-native authoring rather than a discrete trtllm-build step [github], and the developer hub explicitly markets a "PyTorch-native model authoring with stable production API" [devhub]. But the underlying mental model — compile, then serve — is still the right one.

That is what makes the AOT-vs-JIT framing useful when you are setting expectations next to vLLM. vLLM is, in practice, JIT-compiled like Python: you ship a model checkpoint, the engine inspects it, allocates pages, picks attention kernels, and starts serving. TRT-LLM is closer to AOT-compiled like C++: there is a meaningful build step where the engine for your specific model, hardware, max batch size, and precision is produced and committed. The cited sources do not write that analogy, but they do support both halves: the docs explicitly describe "build NVIDIA TensorRT engines" [docs], and introl summarizes the practical consequence as "Setup Complexity: TensorRT-LLM High (weeks); vLLM Low (hours)" [introl]. The tradeoff is engineering time up front, in exchange for peak performance at serve time.

+ LLM API

Where does it fit relative to vLLM and SGLang? The developer hub names AWS, Google Cloud, Microsoft, and Grammarly among ecosystem partners [devhub]; the project lists support for Llama 3.3 70B, Llama 4 Scout 17B, DeepSeek R1, Nemotron v3 Super, GPT-OSS, Qwen3, and Kimi K2 Thinking among the canonical deployment targets [landing]. The release cadence is heavy: the GitHub repo shows 76 total releases with v1.2.1 dated April 20, 2026 [github]. The marketing claim attached to the v1.0 cut is "8X AI inference performance improvement" with v1.0, and on Blackwell Ultra "up to 50x better performance and 35x lower cost" specifically for "agentic AI on NVIDIA Blackwell Ultra" [devhub]. Those are vendor numbers, not third-party benchmarks — keep that qualifier when you cite them.

Segment 2 — The optimization stack, mechanism by mechanism

The optimizations TRT-LLM ships fall into three families: ones that attack CPU-side launch overhead, ones that attack memory bandwidth or memory footprint, and ones that attack arithmetic by reshaping how decode steps are computed. The cited sources describe each one tersely; this segment walks through them in the order they appear in the docs and the release notes, with a short note on what bottleneck each one attacks.

CUDA Graphs and CUDA Graph padding

The architecture overview names CUDA Graph as a first-class runtime feature that "reduces CPU overhead by capturing kernel sequences" [overview]. The mechanism is the standard one: the host records the sequence of kernel launches that make up one decode iteration, then replays the captured graph in a single submission instead of issuing N individual launches. The bottleneck is host-side launch overhead — when the model is small or the GPU is fast enough that per-kernel launch latency starts to rival per-kernel work, the CPU becomes the gate. The overview specifically calls out CUDA Graph padding as a related runtime knob that yields "up to a 22% end-to-end throughput increase" [overview]. Padding here means rounding the dynamic batch up to a captured size so the graph can be reused — without it, every batch shape would invalidate the capture.

In-flight batching (continuous batching, but at the engine level)

Introl's optimization guide describes in-flight batching directly: "Inflight batching adds new requests to running batches at each generation step, processing context and generation phases together. The approach maximizes GPU utilization" [introl]. The architecture overview confirms it ships as "Paged Attention, IFB, and Request Scheduling" — the three primitives that compose continuous batching are listed together [overview]. Conceptually the technique is the same as vLLM's continuous batching — at every decode iteration, finished requests leave the batch and new prefill requests join — but it is implemented inside the same compiled engine that runs the matmuls, not as an outer Python scheduler over a frozen kernel. That co-location is what lets the engine fuse scheduler decisions into kernel launches in the first place.

PagedAttention in TRT-LLM

Paged KV caching in TRT-LLM is described by introl as "Paged attention allocates KV cache in non-contiguous blocks rather than requiring continuous memory regions. Block-level allocation enables sharing KV cache among requests with common prefixes" [introl]. The architecture overview lists it as a feature alongside IFB [overview]. The semantics are the ones a vLLM listener already knows — block-level allocation, no fragmentation, prefix sharing — so the right framing is that TRT-LLM has the same paging primitive, just compiled into the engine. Where it differs is in interaction with hand-tuned attention kernels: introl notes "Hand-optimized implementations of multi-head and grouped-query attention leverage Tensor Core instructions. FP8 attention kernels on Hopper and Blackwell GPUs provide additional speedups" [introl]. The release notes include "Multiblock Attention Boosts Throughput by More Than 3x for Long Sequence Lengths" as a specific paging-aware long-context optimization [github].

Kernel fusion

Introl describes the fusion strategy in one line: "Multiple transformer operations [are combined] into single optimized CUDA kernels. LayerNorm, matrix multiplications, bias additions, and activation functions execute together" [introl]. The bottleneck this attacks is memory bandwidth — the unfused chain reads and writes the same activation tensors to HBM between every op, and on a modern GPU that round trip costs more than the math itself. Fusing LayerNorm + matmul + bias + activation into one kernel means the intermediates stay in registers or shared memory and never touch HBM. The fetch.ai writeup adds the GEMM-plugin and GPT-attention-plugin framing — the same kernels are wrapped as TensorRT plugins so they slot into the compiled graph [fetch].

FP8 and NVFP4 quantization, and when each is used

The developer hub lists "FP8 and NVFP4 quantization" as headline optimization features, paired with paged KV caching and speculative decoding [devhub]. Introl ranks them by use case: FP8 is "Best performance/accuracy tradeoff, requires Hopper/Blackwell"; INT8 SmoothQuant is the "Good alternative for Ada GPUs"; INT4 AWQ/GPTQ is for "Maximum compression for memory-constrained scenarios" and "excels in small-batch scenarios (batch size ≤ 4) where inference becomes memory-bound" [introl]. The release notes call out concrete operating points: "Falcon-180B on a single H200 GPU with INT4 AWQ" — a model that does not fit at FP16 fitting on one card after AWQ [github]. NVFP4 is the newer Blackwell-era format; introl ties it to the December 2025 releases that include "DeepSeek-R1 performance optimization for B200" and "NVFP4 (4-bit) quantization support" [introl], and the wide-EP blog uses NVFP4 inside the AlltoAll combine to compress per-token expert outputs in flight [ep3].

The mental model to take into an interview: FP8 is the throughput tier for current-generation Hopper/Blackwell, NVFP4 is the next step down for memory-bound and Blackwell-specific paths, and INT4 AWQ is the small-batch / memory-pressure escape hatch. Each one has Tensor Core support on the hardware that lists it.

Speculative decoding — EAGLE-3, MTP, n-gram, lookahead, Medusa

The TRT-LLM landing page enumerates the supported drafting methods explicitly: "N-Gram, Lookahead, Medusa, Eagle, Eagle3, and MTP (Multi-Token Prediction) decoding methods" [landing]. The developer hub specifically promotes "speculative decoding (EAGLE-3, multi-token prediction)" as headline optimizations [devhub]. Introl adds a magnitude estimate that holds across speculative variants in general: "The technique provides 1.5-2x speedup for compatible workloads" and benefits "latency-sensitive applications where time-to-completion matters" [introl]. The bottleneck speculative decoding attacks is the autoregressive serial chain — each accepted draft token saves one decode step — so the gain depends on accept rate, which depends on the drafter and the workload.

The cited sources do not give a deep mechanism explanation of EAGLE-3's "dynamic tree" or MTP's exact head structure; they list the methods as supported. The wide-EP blog does describe one MTP-side optimization explicitly: "MTP LM Head Tensor Parallelism" splits the LM head across 4 GPUs with intra-node AllGather and 4-way TP on the vocabulary, "reducing weight loading time in memory-bound scenarios" [ep3]. That is the only MTP internal detail any cited source provides.

Long-sequence and sparse-style attention

The cited sources do not document a feature literally called "sparse attention" in TRT-LLM. What they do document is multiblock attention: the GitHub release-notes list includes "Multiblock Attention Boosts Throughput by More Than 3x for Long Sequence Lengths" [github]. That is the long-context kernel that pays off the same way sparse attention would — it splits attention work into blocks that can run in parallel and avoids materializing the full attention matrix. Introl summarizes the family of long-sequence wins as "FP8 attention kernels on Hopper and Blackwell GPUs provide additional speedups" [introl]. If you need to talk about sparse attention specifically, flag it as not directly named in the public docs.

Overlap scheduler

The architecture overview lists the Overlap Scheduler as a runtime feature that "hide[s] CPU-bound latency behind GPU computation" [overview]. The mechanism — running scheduler bookkeeping for iteration N+1 while the GPU is still computing iteration N — is the same idea covered in the SGLang deep dive. The bottleneck it attacks is the CPU bubble between kernel launches when the host scheduler dominates wall-clock; padding plus graph capture handle the static-shape part, the overlap scheduler handles the dynamic-batch part. The overview does not give a TRT-LLM-specific magnitude for this knob alone.

A representative operating point

For a sense of where the stacked optimizations land, the landing page reports "H200 achieves nearly 12,000 tokens/sec on Llama2-13B" and "10,000 tok/s at 100ms to first token" on H100 [landing]. The repo highlights "24,000 tokens per second" achieved with Meta Llama 3 and "3x Faster AllReduce with NVSwitch and TensorRT LLM MultiShot" as a separate scaling-out claim [github]. These are vendor benchmarks under specific configs; treat them as upper bounds, not as anything you would reproduce on an arbitrary cluster.

CPU-side wins

CUDA Graph capture removes per-launch host overhead. CUDA Graph padding "up to 22% end-to-end throughput" [overview]. Overlap Scheduler hides CPU-bound latency behind GPU compute [overview].

Memory-bandwidth wins

Kernel fusion keeps LayerNorm + matmul + bias + activation in registers/SRAM [introl]. Paged KV cache + custom attention kernels exploit Tensor Cores; FP8 attention on Hopper/Blackwell [introl].

Arithmetic / serial-chain wins

FP8 (Hopper/Blackwell), NVFP4 (Blackwell), INT4 AWQ (small-batch). Speculative decoding (Eagle, Eagle3, MTP, N-Gram, Lookahead, Medusa) — "1.5–2x speedup for compatible workloads" [introl][landing].

Segment 3 — Wide expert parallelism and the DeepSeek-class MoE story

The single biggest TRT-LLM differentiator the cited sources support is the wide-EP work for DeepSeek R1 and similar mixture-of-experts models. The developer hub flags it directly as a feature line: "Wide expert parallelism (EP) and disaggregated serving" [devhub]. The detailed engineering writeup lives in the wide-EP part-3 tech blog, and it is more specific than any other source in this section [ep3].

The problem wide-EP solves

The blog frames it as the computational bottleneck of "massive Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models like DeepSeek R1" — distributing expert computation across many GPUs so that a model with hundreds of experts can run on cluster-shaped hardware without each replica needing to hold every expert [ep3]. The hardware target is explicit: optimizations are designed for "NVIDIA GB200 NVL72 (dual-GPU nodes with NVLink3)" and "Blackwell architecture, leveraging superior FP8 Tensor performance and higher bandwidth compared to Hopper" [ep3]. The whole point of wide-EP is that an expert-parallel deployment turns the all-to-all communication step between MLP-down and MLP-up into the dominant cost — so the kernels that move tokens between experts have to be co-designed with the rest of the engine.

EP communication: AlltoAll redesign

The blog describes the AlltoAll work directly: TRT-LLM "redesigned and reimplemented AlltoAll" so that multiple data fields — hidden states, scaling factors, token routing information — fuse into single operations, "reducing kernel launch overhead critical in wide-EP scenarios" [ep3]. Then on the combine phase, where each expert's outputs flow back to the requesting tokens, the system "temporarily quantize[s] the per-token expert outputs to NVFP4, send[s] the compact representation across GPUs, and dequantize[s] back to the original dtype on the receiving side" — supported by PR 7155 and PR 7898 [ep3]. The technique compresses the wire payload exactly where the network is the bottleneck, and absorbs the quant/dequant cost on either side because the receiving GPU is already cheap on FP4.

Programmatic Dependent Launch (PDL)

The third EP-side trick in the blog is Programmatic Dependent Launch — a CUDA-level mechanism that "enables kernels to overlap execution within the same CUDA stream" [ep3]. The profiling examples in the blog show three kernels (moeComputeRouteKernel, computeCountAndIndiceDevice, computeCumsumDevice) executing strictly sequentially without PDL, then overlapping with PDL enabled [ep3]. It is the same idea as the overlap scheduler but at a finer grain — the host says "kernel B may begin once kernel A produces enough of its output," and the device hardware actually starts B without waiting for A to fully finish.

MLA-specific optimization

DeepSeek's Multi-head Latent Attention compresses keys and values into a smaller latent space, but the price is a more complex Q/K/V layout: "Q and K are divided into two parts: with RoPE and without RoPE" [ep3]. PR 6538 "eliminate[d] the need for concatenating Q/K/V, saving copy operations and significantly improving prefill latency in wide EP scenarios" [ep3]. There is also a quantization story specific to MLA: "FP8 Context FMHA" quantizes query projections to FP8 to match an FP8 KV cache, "enabling FP8 Tensor Core operations during prefill" — automatically enabled when an FP8 KV cache is selected (PR 7610, 7612) [ep3]. And the LM head itself gets a separate scaling treatment: "MTP LM Head Tensor Parallelism" performs intra-node AllGather across 4 GPUs, applies 4-way TP to split vocabulary weights, then collects local argmax logits before global reduction — "reducing weight loading time in memory-bound scenarios" (PR 7571, 7891) [ep3].

One more MLA-side knob: "wo GEMM FP4 Quantization" quantizes the attention output projection to FP4 while "maintaining a similar MTP accept rate (AR)" using checkpoints from NVIDIA Model Optimizer [ep3]. The fact that the AR is preserved is the point — quantizing the wrong projection in a speculative pipeline collapses the accept rate and erases the speedup.

Why this is a TRT-LLM differentiator

The blog itself does not give an engine-vs-engine table for wide-EP. What the cited sources support is the directional claim: TRT-LLM has invested heavily and specifically in EP communication kernels (AlltoAll fusion, NVFP4-on-the-wire), in MLA-aware kernel fusion (Q/K/V concat elimination, FP8 Context FMHA), in scaling-out tricks (PDL, MTP LM-head TP), and in Blackwell-class hardware exploitation (NVFP4, GB200 NVL72) — all driven by PR-level engineering against DeepSeek-R1 as a target workload [ep3]. SGLang has its own wide-EP work and so does vLLM, but the wide-EP blog's level of mechanism detail and the cadence of merged PRs are what an interview answer should point to as the TRT-LLM differentiator. Frame it as: when the workload is a frontier MoE on Blackwell, TRT-LLM is where the kernel-level optimization is being landed first.

Segment 4 — TensorRT-LLM versus vLLM, the honest tradeoff

The cleanest head-to-head data point in the cited sources is in introl's optimization guide. On Llama-70B on A100, the comparative numbers are [introl]:

MetricTensorRT-LLMvLLM
Peak throughput~700 tokens/sec~600–650 tokens/sec
Time-to-first-token35–50 ms50–80 ms
Short-sequence advantage1.34× higherbaseline
Long-sequence TPOT advantage2.72× betterbaseline
Setup complexityHigh (weeks)Low (hours)

Introl's framing of when each engine wins is direct [introl]. TRT-LLM is a strong fit when the team is "Committed to NVIDIA GPU infrastructure long-term," has "CUDA/TensorRT experience," is running workloads where "20-40% performance improvement justifies weeks of optimization," is at "Production scale deployments exceeding $50,000 monthly," or has "Sub-50ms TTFT requirements." It is a weak fit for "Rapid prototyping or experimental work," "Teams lacking NVIDIA optimization expertise," "Multi-cloud requirements," "Small-scale deployments where vLLM suffices," and "Frequent model changes." The summary line: "Deploy TensorRT-LLM when maximizing hardware efficiency justifies engineering investment. Choose vLLM for faster time-to-production or when operating at smaller scale" [introl].

A decision framework you can defend in an interview, grounded in those numbers and the rest of this section:

  • Pick TRT-LLM when (a) the deployment is large enough that a 1.34×–2.72× window pays for weeks of build/calibration time [introl], (b) the hardware is Blackwell or Blackwell Ultra and you want NVFP4 plus the Wide-EP kernels [ep3][devhub], (c) the workload is a frontier MoE like DeepSeek R1 where the all-to-all and MLA optimizations land first [ep3], or (d) TTFT below 50 ms is a hard product constraint [introl].
  • Pick vLLM when (a) time-to-production matters more than peak throughput, (b) hardware diversity matters (multi-cloud, AMD, future flexibility), (c) the model lineup changes faster than a build pipeline can keep up with, or (d) the deployment scale does not justify the build/validation engineering tax [introl].
  • Honest caveat: "1.34× / 2.72×" are introl's numbers for Llama-70B on A100; on Blackwell against the most recent vLLM with FlashAttention-3 and FP8, the gap will look different. Cite the framing, not the precise multiplier.

The orthogonal point worth making in an interview: this is not a one-engine choice for a real production fleet. Most NVIDIA-aligned shops will end up running both — TRT-LLM for the high-volume flagship workloads, vLLM for the long tail of internal models, experiments, and hardware footprints that do not yet have an optimized TRT-LLM path. The infrastructure question then becomes how to operate two engines side by side, which is what NIM exists to absorb.

Segment 5 — What this means for a NIM Factory interview

The cited sources do not contain a description of NIM Factory's pipeline architecture, its validation matrix, or its container build internals. What they do contain — and what is fair game for an interview answer — is a clean picture of the artifact NIM ships and the build characteristics of the engine that produces that artifact. The rest is editorial extension.

What the sources do support about productized TRT-LLM

The artifact: TRT-LLM produces "NVIDIA TensorRT engines that contain state-of-the-art optimizations" [docs], built from a model definition through the LLM Python API [github]. The build is shape- and hardware-specific — kernel selection, quantization calibration, and graph rewriting all bake in assumptions about target precision, max batch size, max sequence length, and parallelism layout. The runtime that loads the engine has the four-component shape described earlier: Scheduler, KVCacheManager, ModelEngine, Sampler [overview]. The optimizations that ship with it cover CUDA Graph capture and padding [overview], in-flight batching and paged attention [overview][introl], kernel fusion [introl], FP8 / NVFP4 / INT4 AWQ quantization [devhub][introl][github], six speculative-decoding methods including Eagle3 and MTP [landing], multiblock attention for long sequences [github], an overlap scheduler [overview], wide expert parallelism with NVFP4-on-the-wire AlltoAll and PDL [ep3], MLA-aware kernel fusion [ep3], and disaggregated serving [devhub].

The release-cadence picture from the cited sources: 76 total releases on the GitHub repo, with v1.2.1 dated April 20, 2026 [github], plus the developer-hub claim of "8X AI inference performance improvement" with v1.0 and "up to 50x better performance and 35x lower cost" for "agentic AI on NVIDIA Blackwell Ultra" [devhub]. Ecosystem footprint: AWS, Google Cloud, Microsoft, Grammarly are listed partners [devhub]; canonical model targets in the deployment guides include Llama 3.3 70B, Llama 4 Scout, DeepSeek R1, Nemotron v3 Super, GPT-OSS, Qwen3, Kimi K2 [landing].

Editorial postscript — beyond the cited sources
None of the cited sources document the NIM Factory pipeline, the NIM container build process, the validation matrix, or how pre-compiled engines are catalogued and shipped. What follows is editorial extension that maps what the sources do say about TRT-LLM onto the structural questions an interviewer would actually ask. Treat it as a reasoned exercise, not a sourced claim.

The build pipeline question. If the artifact is a pre-compiled TRT-LLM engine, then "what does the build pipeline look like" reduces to: for every supported (model × precision × hardware × parallelism × max-shape) tuple, run the LLM-API build, validate the resulting engine against a quality and performance bar, and publish the resulting container. The build itself is what the cited sources describe — kernel selection, calibration, graph rewriting [docs][github] — so the pipeline's hard problem is not the build, it is the surface area. Each new precision (FP8, NVFP4, INT4 AWQ) multiplies the matrix; each new hardware target (H100, H200, B200, GB200 NVL72) multiplies it again; each new parallelism layout (TP, PP, CP, EP, plus combinations) multiplies it again. The Wide-EP blog's PR-by-PR list of optimizations is a hint at how often the matrix's "right" cell is changing under you [ep3].

The combinatorial scaling question. The honest answer is that you cannot enumerate the full Cartesian product; the validation matrix has to be pruned by usage. The sources do not document NIM's pruning rules, but the canonical model list at the landing page is a strong signal — Llama 3.3 70B, Llama 4 Scout, DeepSeek R1, Nemotron v3 Super, GPT-OSS, Qwen3, Kimi K2 [landing] are the models that get the productization investment, and the rest of the long tail is what vLLM in a NIM container is for.

The shipping-pre-compiled-vs-letting-customers-compile tradeoff. Pre-compiled engines (NIM-style) buy fast cold start, predictable performance, and a fixed validation surface — at the cost of inflexibility on shape, precision, or hardware that was not in the matrix. Customer-compiled engines buy flexibility — at the cost of build time (introl's "weeks" framing) [introl], calibration data, and the operational burden of keeping the build pipeline current with TRT-LLM's release cadence (76 releases, last on 2026-04-20) [github]. NIM's value proposition is precisely to absorb that burden for the cell of the matrix that the customer actually needs.

The right interview move is to talk concretely about what the cited sources do say — the optimization stack, the wide-EP differentiator, the AOT-style build characteristic, the release cadence — and let the interviewer surface NIM-internal specifics. Do not invent an architecture for a pipeline you have not been shown.

A short list of things you can defend from the cited sources
  • TRT-LLM builds TensorRT engines from an LLM Python API; the docs state "build NVIDIA TensorRT engines that contain state-of-the-art optimizations" [docs][github].
  • The runtime's PyExecutor has four components: Scheduler, KVCacheManager, ModelEngine, Sampler [overview].
  • CUDA Graph padding yields "up to a 22% end-to-end throughput increase" [overview].
  • Six speculative-decoding methods are supported: N-Gram, Lookahead, Medusa, Eagle, Eagle3, MTP [landing]; speculative decoding gives "1.5–2x speedup for compatible workloads" [introl].
  • FP8 / NVFP4 / INT4 AWQ quantization, with INT4 AWQ "excels in small-batch scenarios (batch size ≤ 4)" [introl][github].
  • Wide-EP redesigned AlltoAll fuses hidden states + scaling factors + routing into one op; combine phase quantizes per-token expert outputs to NVFP4 on the wire (PR 7155, 7898) [ep3].
  • MLA-specific PR 6538 eliminates Q/K/V concatenation; FP8 Context FMHA matches FP8 KV cache (PR 7610, 7612); MTP LM Head TP splits vocab 4-way intra-node (PR 7571, 7891) [ep3].
  • Vendor operating points: H200 ~12,000 tok/s on Llama2-13B; H100 10,000 tok/s @ 100 ms TTFT; 24,000 tok/s on Meta Llama 3 with MultiShot AllReduce 3× faster; Multiblock Attention >3× throughput on long sequences; 8× v1.0 inference perf; up to 50× perf / 35× cost on Blackwell Ultra agentic AI [landing][github][devhub].
  • Llama-70B / A100 head-to-head: ~700 vs ~600–650 tok/s, 35–50 vs 50–80 ms TTFT, 1.34× short-seq, 2.72× long-seq TPOT, "weeks" vs "hours" setup [introl].
  • Release cadence: 76 releases, v1.2.1 on 2026-04-20 [github].

DeepDives › MoE Inference

MoE Inference at Scale — a NotebookLM-style deep dive new

A six-segment deep dive that merges the research view (ExFlow → ScMoE → Aurora → Occult) with the production view (DeepSeek-V3, the LMSYS 96-H100 reproduction, NVIDIA's wide-EP on GB200 NVL72). Roughly 45–60 minutes of audio. Written for an AWS engineering manager preparing for a NIM Factory interview.

How to read this section. Every concrete claim is attributed inline. Source tags: [scmoe] (Cai et al., arXiv 2404.05019), [exflow] (Yao et al., arXiv 2401.08383, OSU/MVAPICH), [aurora] (Li et al., arXiv 2410.17043, MPI-SWS, Oct 2024), [occult] (Luo et al., arXiv 2505.13345, UNC/UCLA, ICML 2025), [ds-v3] (DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report, arXiv 2412.19437), [deepseek-readme] (deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V3 GitHub README), [lmsys-ep] (LMSYS large-scale EP blog, May 2025), [nv-wide-ep] (NVIDIA developer blog "Scaling Large MoE Models with Wide-EP on NVL72"), [trt-blog1] (TensorRT-LLM tech blog 1, DeepSeek-R1 on B200), [trt-blog14] (TensorRT-LLM tech blog 14, Scaling EP Part 3), [trt-index] (TensorRT-LLM doc-home blog index). Where a number is workload- or version-specific, the source's qualifier is preserved. Segment 6's NIM-Factory framing is clearly marked as editorial because none of the cited sources discuss NIM internals.
Segment 1 — Why MoE inference is a systems problem

MoE inference inverts the assumption every dense-model serving system was built around. In a dense model, every token follows the same path through the network — every weight is touched, every parameter is part of the per-token compute budget. In MoE, parameters and compute are decoupled: DeepSeek-V3 has 671B total parameters but only 37B activated per token [ds-v3], and the whole game is making that sparsity efficient at serving time without losing the quality that came from training the full 671B.

Three systems-level pain points fall out of that decoupling immediately, and they shape every paper and every production stack we will discuss.

Pain point one: parameters and compute are decoupled, but parameters still have to fit somewhere. Even at FP8, 671B parameters is on the order of 700 GB of weights — too large for a single H100 node, too large for a single H200 node. DeepSeek's own production deployment uses 4 nodes (32 GPUs) for the prefill stage and 40 nodes (320 GPUs) for the decoding stage [ds-v3]. There is no single-machine option for a frontier MoE; every deployment is a multi-node aggregation problem before any other optimization starts.

Pain point two: expert parallelism (EP) distributes experts across GPUs, which forces all-to-all communication on every MoE layer. Aurora's preliminary section diagrams this as a four-step layer: gate → first all-to-all (dispatch tokens to expert GPUs) → FFN (the expert) → second all-to-all (combine results back) → aggregation [aurora]. Two all-to-alls per MoE layer, and a modern MoE has dozens of MoE layers. DeepSeek-V3 has 3 dense layers + 58 MoE layers [trt-blog1] — that is 116 all-to-alls on the forward pass alone.

Pain point three: routing is dynamic, which means GPU utilization is uneven and load is not predictable. Aurora's framing: "the dynamic selection of experts results in an uneven distribution of experts among GPUs, leading to some GPUs being heavily loaded while others remain idle" [aurora]. The gate output is a function of the token, recomputed every layer; you cannot pre-allocate.

A useful frame for an infrastructure audience: in a dense model the tokens travel a single highway, every car taking the same lanes in the same order. In MoE, every token gets routed to a different specialist on a different GPU — the routing itself is part of the workload, and the routing table is recomputed every layer. The all-to-all is the network shuffle phase of map-reduce, except it executes twice per layer, synchronously, with payload sizes that vary per rank. That is why MoE serving is a systems problem and not just a kernel problem.

Tokens (per GPU)local batch slice
→
Gatetop-k expert selection
per token
→
All-to-All #1dispatch tokens to
expert-owning GPUs
→
Expert FFNcompute on remote GPU
→

to origin GPUs
